### [1] [Trapping magnetic flux quanta in superconducting 3D caps](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04446)
*Igor Bogush,Vladimir M. Fomin,Oleksandr Dobrovolskiy*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Shaping a superconducting thin film into a 3D curved (cap) geometry enables controllable vortex trapping and transport under a uniform magnetic field, offering fluxonic devices with magnetic-field-programmable vortex pinning, including a superconducting diode effect.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In planar thin films, individual vortices (magnetic flux quanta) are hard to manipulate, requiring intricate nanopatterning or demanding scanning-probe instrumentation. The work seeks a simpler, reconfigurable route to trap and control vortices.

Method: The authors use a conformal formulation of the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau equation to simulate vortex dynamics in a cap-shaped curved superconducting membrane and compare it against a planar reference.

Result: Curvature converts a uniform applied magnetic field into a nonuniform local normal component, creating a reconfigurable asymmetric rim-pinning potential tunable by field orientation. Rotating the field controls the number and positions of vortices trapped along the rim, and the asymmetric pinning yields a superconducting diode effect via current-direction-dependent vortex capture.

Conclusion: 3D curvature provides a route to engineer local vortex pinning and realize fluxonic devices with magnetic-field-programmable vortex trapping and transport.

Abstract: Magnetic flux quanta in type-II superconductors are topological excitations of the order parameter whose sensitivity to the local environment makes them probes of current density, magnetic fields, and pinning landscapes. Yet, in planar thin films, individual vortices are difficult to manipulate, often requiring intricate nanopatterning or demanding scanning-probe instrumentation. Here, we show that shaping a superconducting thin film into a three-dimensional curved geometry enables controllable vortex trapping under a spatially uniform applied magnetic field. Employing a conformal formulation of the time-dependent Ginzburg-Landau equation, we simulate vortex dynamics in a cap-shaped superconducting membrane and compare it with a planar reference. Curvature converts the uniform applied magnetic field into a nonuniform local normal component, creating a reconfigurable asymmetric rim-pinning potential tunable by magnetic-field orientation. Rotating the magnetic field therefore controls the number and positions of vortices trapped along the rim. Moreover, the asymmetric pinning produces a superconducting diode effect through current-direction-dependent vortex capture. Three-dimensional curvature thus provides a route to engineer local vortex pinning and realize fluxonic devices with magnetic-field-programmable vortex trapping and transport.

</details>


### [2] [Superconducting landscape of the strongly correlated bilayer Hubbard model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04855)
*Motoharu Kitatani,Yusuke Nomura,Shiro Sakai,Ryotaro Arita*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using dynamical vertex approximation, the authors map the superconducting phase diagram of the strongly correlated bilayer Hubbard model across filling and interlayer hopping, pinpointing an optimal regime at intermediate interlayer hopping and moderate doping with an interband s±-wave gap, while identifying self-energy-driven suppression near half filling that can be alleviated by longer-range-hopping-induced Fermi-surface distortion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Coupling two strongly correlated layers offers a route to repulsion-driven superconductivity, but the same interlayer coupling makes optimizing superconductivity nontrivial, motivating a systematic study of how filling and interlayer hopping jointly shape the superconducting landscape.

Method: Dynamical vertex approximation (DVA) is applied to the strongly correlated bilayer Hubbard model to determine the superconducting phase diagram in the parameter space spanned by band filling and interlayer hopping, including analysis of self-energy effects and Fermi-surface geometry.

Result: An optimal superconducting regime is identified at intermediate interlayer hopping and moderate doping away from half filling, featuring high transition temperatures Tc with an interband s±-wave gap. Toward half filling, strong self-energy effects suppress superconductivity by reducing low-energy spectral weight. A Fermi-surface distortion induced by longer-range hoppings weakens this suppression and enhances Tc.

Conclusion: Filling, interlayer hopping, and Fermi-surface geometry jointly govern the optimal superconducting regime in the strongly correlated bilayer Hubbard model, establishing a framework for optimizing repulsion-driven superconductivity in coupled-layer systems.

Abstract: Coupling two strongly correlated layers opens a distinct route to repulsion-driven superconductivity. Yet the same interlayer coupling makes the optimization of superconductivity highly nontrivial. Using the dynamical vertex approximation, we determine the superconducting landscape in the enlarged parameter space spanned by filling and interlayer hopping. We identify an optimal regime at intermediate interlayer hopping and moderate doping away from half filling, where the system exhibits high superconducting transition temperatures ($T_{\rm c}$) with an interband $s_{\pm}$-wave gap structure. Toward half filling, however, strong self-energy effects can suppress the superconducting tendency by reducing the low-energy spectral weight. We further show that a Fermi-surface distortion induced by longer-range hoppings can weaken this suppression and enhance $T_{\rm c}$. Our results establish how filling, interlayer hopping, and Fermi-surface geometry jointly optimize the superconducting regime in the strongly correlated bilayer Hubbard model.

</details>


### [3] [Paramagnon softening upon doping in oxychloride cuprate superconductors](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05160)
*Chafic Fawaz,Léo Gaspard,Laura Chaix,Luca Laveder,Benjamin Bacq-Labreuil,Victor Porée,Flora Yakhou-Harris,Kurt Kummer,Nicholas B. Brookes,Alessandro Nicolaou,Hajime Yamamoto,Yuichi Okazaki,Ikuya Yamada,Masaki Azuma,David Santos-Cottin,Marie-Aude Méasson,Benjamin Lenz,Matteo d'Astuto*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paramagnon dispersion in oxychloride cuprate Na_xCa_{2-x}CuO_2Cl_2 is tracked up to the highest doping: the zone-boundary bandwidth stays constant while the nodal-direction energy softens strongly, and a one-band Hubbard model with t'' hopping reproduces this wave-vector- and direction-dependent behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Deciphering the cuprate phase diagram requires knowing how magnetic excitations and exchange interactions evolve with doping. Prior knowledge of the doping dependence of the paramagnon dispersion, particularly its anisotropy, was unclear.

Method: Measurement of the paramagnon dispersion in Na_xCa_{2-x}CuO_2Cl_2 up to the highest doping, characterizing the energy at the X zone boundary (0.5,0,0) and along the nodal direction Γ-M from (0,0,0) to (0.5,0.5,0). Comparison with calculations of the dynamical spin structure factor for a one-band Hubbard model including hopping up to the third-nearest neighbor (t'').

Result: The paramagnon bandwidth at the X zone boundary remains unchanged within experimental uncertainty despite significant broadening. Along the nodal direction Γ-M, the paramagnon energy softens markedly with doping, with a shift of up to ΔE~150 meV at the midpoint of the zone for the highest doping, roughly half its initial value in the antiferromagnetic phase. The Hubbard-model calculations reproduce the observed wave-vector dependence and directional variation in the Brillouin zone.

Conclusion: Magnetic excitations in this oxychloride cuprate show an anisotropic doping evolution: robust zone-boundary bandwidth versus strong nodal softening. A one-band Hubbard model with t'' captures this behavior, indicating that short-range hopping terms govern the directional dependence of paramagnon renormalization.

Abstract: Understanding how magnetic excitations and exchange interactions evolve with doping is crucial for deciphering the physics that shapes the phase diagram of high-temperature cuprate superconductors. Here, we clearly determine the behavior of the paramagnon dispersion in the oxychloride cuprate Na$_{x}$Ca$_{2-x}$CuO$_2$Cl$_2$ up to the highest doping level. We find that the paramagnon bandwidth, defined by the energy at the $X$ zone boundary $(0.5,0,0)$, remains unchanged within experimental uncertainty, even though it exhibits significant broadening. In contrast, along the nodal direction $Γ-M$ (\textit{i.e.}, from $(0,0,0)$ to $(0.5,0.5,0)$), the paramagnon energy shows a marked softening with doping, with a shift of up to $ΔE\sim150$ meV at the midpoint of the zone for the highest doping, which is about half of its initial value in the antiferromagnetic phase. Calculations of the dynamical spin structure factor for a one-band Hubbard model, including hopping terms up to the third-nearest neighbor ($t^{\prime\prime}$), explain this behavior and accurately reproduce the observed wave vector dependence and directional variation in the Brillouin zone.

</details>


### [4] [Solid-state reaction synthesis of superconducting refractory-metal germanides for Ge/SiGe quantum devices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05210)
*Sebastiaan R. Roelofs,Jan Cornelis Wolff,Nick van Loo,Karina L. Hudson,Giordano Scappucci,Greg P. Mazur*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: This study examines low-temperature (365–390 °C) solid-phase reactions of V, Ta, and Nb with Ge and Si0.2Ge0.8 to form superconducting layers near buried Ge quantum wells. On pure Ge at 390 °C, incremental deposition with in-situ annealing yields superconducting transitions at ~0.14 K (V-Ge), 2.1 K (Ta-Ge), and 2.8 K (Nb-Ge). In heterostructures, intermixing is limited to a 5–12 nm interfacial region, leaving a residual metal film that dominates the superconducting response, which is identified as the main obstacle to reaching the quantum well.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Integrating superconductors into Ge/SiGe heterostructures is desirable, but forming high-quality superconducting layers near the buried Ge quantum well is difficult. The reactivity and superconducting properties of refractory metals (V, Ta, Nb) with Ge and the SiGe barrier at low processing temperatures need to be understood.

Method: V, Ta, and Nb were reacted with elemental Ge and with the Si0.2Ge0.8 barrier of Ge/SiGe heterostructures at 365–390 °C. On Ge at 390 °C, deposition was done in ~2 nm metal increments separated by growth stops for in-situ annealing. Cross-sectional electron microscopy and low-temperature electrical transport were used to characterize the films and heterostructure samples.

Result: On Ge at 390 °C, reactions occurred throughout the deposited films, giving superconducting transitions at ~0.14 K (V-Ge), 2.1 K (Ta-Ge), and 2.8 K (Nb-Ge). The Nb-Ge layer showed an out-of-plane critical field of ~0.8 T at 2.1 K. In heterostructure samples, intermixing was confined to ~5–12 nm near the metal-SiGe interface, leaving a substantial residual metal layer that dominates the measured superconducting response.

Conclusion: Low-temperature formation of superconducting refractory-metal-Ge layers is demonstrated. However, the limited interfacial reaction—leaving a residual metal film—is the principal obstacle to extending this approach toward the buried quantum well.

Abstract: Solid-phase reactions offer a route to integrate superconductors into Ge/SiGe heterostructures, but forming high-quality superconducting layers near the buried Ge quantum well remains challenging. We investigate reactions of V, Ta, and Nb with elemental Ge and with the Si$_{0.2}$Ge$_{0.8}$ barrier of Ge/SiGe heterostructures at processing temperatures of 365-390 $^\circ$C. Cross-sectional electron microscopy and low-temperature electrical transport show that deposition on Ge at 390 $^\circ$C, in which approximately 2 nm metal increments are separated by growth stops for in-situ annealing, produces reactions throughout the deposited films and superconducting transitions at approximately 0.14 K for V-Ge, 2.1 K for Ta-Ge, and 2.8 K for Nb-Ge. The Nb-Ge layer has an out-of-plane critical field of approximately 0.8 T at 2.1 K. In the heterostructure samples, intermixing is confined to regions approximately 5-12 nm thick near the metal-SiGe interface, leaving a substantial residual metal layer. Microscopy and transport together suggest that this residual film dominates the measured superconducting response. These results establish low-temperature formation of superconducting refractory-metal-Ge layers while identifying the limited interfacial reaction as the principal obstacle to extending this approach toward the buried quantum well.

</details>


### [5] [Competing charge density-wave and superconducting states in a quasi-one dimensional two-band electron-phonon model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05321)
*C. Bourbonnais,A. Ghosh,M. Haguier*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Using a two-cutoff renormalization group scheme at finite temperature, the interplay between charge density-wave (CDW) and superconductivity (SC) is studied in two quasi-one-dimensional tight-binding bands coupled by electron-phonon interaction, yielding finite-temperature phase diagrams, superimposed CDW-SC states, and SC domes from quantum critical CDW fluctuations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The competition and possible coexistence between charge density-wave and superconducting orders in quasi-one-dimensional multiband electron-phonon systems, especially across adiabatic and antiadiabatic regimes and at finite temperature, is not fully understood. Nesting frustration of the electron spectrum and multiband effects on ordering temperatures, quantum criticality, and the isotope effect require a systematic renormalization-group treatment.

Method: Renormalization group method applied to two quasi-one-dimensional tight-binding electronic bands coupled by electron-phonon interaction. Electronic and phonon energy scales are embedded in a two-cutoff scaling scheme at finite temperature. Flow equations are derived for intra- and inter-band phonon-mediated electron-electron interactions at the one-loop level, covering adiabatic and antiadiabatic regimes for small and large phonon frequencies. Finite-temperature phase diagrams are obtained as a function of nesting frustration.

Result: Different renormalization regimes are identified across adiabatic and antiadiabatic domains. Finite-temperature phase diagrams versus nesting frustration are obtained. Possibilities for superimposed CDW and SC ordered states are found. Domes of superconductivity emerge from quantum critical reinforcement of Cooper pairing by CDW order fluctuations. The multiband character of the electronic structure impacts the SC ordering temperature, quantum criticality, and the isotope effect.

Conclusion: The renormalization group analysis shows that CDW fluctuations can quantum-critically enhance Cooper pairing, producing superconducting domes, and that multiband electronic structure significantly influences SC ordering temperature, quantum criticality, and isotope effect in quasi-one-dimensional electron-phonon systems.

Abstract: We apply the renormalization group method to examine the interplay between the charge density-wave and superconductivity phases in two quasi-one-dimensional tight-binding electronic bands coupled by the electron-phonon interaction. The electronic and phonon energy scales are embedded in a two-cutoff scaling scheme at finite temperature for the renormalization group flow equations of intra- and inter-band phonon-mediated interactions between electrons. At the one-loop level different regimes of renormalization are derived covering both the adiabatic and antiadiabatic domains for small and large phonon frequencies. Finite temperature phase diagrams as a function of nesting frustration of the electron spectrum are thus obtained. The possibilities for superimposed charge density-wave and superconducting ordered states are given. The emergence of domes of superconductivity resulting from quantum critical reinforcement of Cooper pairing by charge density-wave order fluctuations is found. The impact of the multiband character of the electronic structure on the ordering temperature of superconductivity, quantum criticality, and isotope effect is discussed.

</details>


### [6] [Field-free dual superconducting diode via photon-assisted interference](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05536)
*Laura Borgongino,Rubén Seoane Souto,José Luis del Olmo,Ramón Aguado,Alessandro Crippa,Francesco Giazotto,Elia Strambini*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: A single conventional Al/AlOx/Al tunnel junction becomes a dynamically reconfigurable dual-function superconducting diode via microwave biharmonic driving, switching between supercurrent and ideal quasiparticle rectification with zero forward resistance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional semiconductor diodes degrade at sub-Kelvin temperatures, but scaling cryogenic electronics requires low-temperature, low-dissipation rectification. Existing superconducting diodes are limited to either Cooper-pair or quasiparticle transport and typically demand complex material stacks, asymmetric geometries, or external magnetic fields; no geometrically symmetric junction had integrated rectification across both transport channels.

Method: The authors use a conventional, geometrically symmetric Al/AlOx/Al tunnel junction and apply microwave biharmonic driving, exploiting multi-tone photon-assisted tunneling to independently control the dissipationless (supercurrent) and dissipative (quasiparticle) transport channels in the same device. Rectification is tested under both current-bias and voltage-bias configurations, and drive amplitude is varied to tune the operating regime.

Result: Varying the microwave drive amplitude induces a transition from a supercurrent diode to an ideal quasiparticle diode, with rectification efficiencies exceeding those of conventional Schottky diodes and contemporary superconducting diodes. AC signal rectification is demonstrated under both current- and voltage-bias. A proof-of-concept "absolute diode" combining zero forward resistance with strongly suppressed reverse conduction is realized.

Conclusion: Microwave biharmonic driving of a simple symmetric tunnel junction yields a tunable, dual-function superconducting diode without complex stacks, asymmetric geometry, or magnetic fields. This provides a versatile building block for low-power cryogenic electronic architectures.

Abstract: Non-reciprocal transport underpins key functionalities in signal processing and logic; however, conventional semiconductor diodes exhibit reduced performance at sub-Kelvin temperatures. As cryogenic electronics continue to scale, there is increasing demand for diode operation compatible with low-temperature and low-dissipation conditions. This need has driven the development of alternatives to p-n junctions that rely on non-reciprocal electron transport mechanisms efficient in the deep-cryogenic regime. Superconducting devices constitute a natural low-loss platform, yet most existing superconducting diodes are restricted to either Cooper-pair or quasiparticle transport, often requiring complex material stacks, asymmetric geometries, or external magnetic fields. No single, geometrically symmetric junction has yet integrated rectification across both transport channels to achieve vanishing resistance under forward bias and strongly suppressed conduction under reverse bias. Here, we demonstrate a dynamically reconfigurable dual-function superconducting diode based on a conventional Al/AlOx/Al tunnel junction. Via microwave biharmonic driving, we exploit multi-tone photon-assisted tunneling to independently control dissipationless and dissipative transport channels within the same device. Varying the microwave drive amplitude induces a transition from a supercurrent diode to an ideal quasiparticle diode, achieving rectification efficiencies exceeding those of conventional Schottky diodes and contemporary superconducting diodes. We further demonstrate AC signal rectification under both current-bias and voltage-bias configurations. Finally, we implement a proof-of-concept "absolute diode" that combines zero forward resistance with strongly suppressed reverse conduction. This tunable approach provides a versatile building block for low-power cryogenic electronic architectures.

</details>


### [7] [Giant nonlinearity of kinetic inductance in superconducting hybrid strip](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05903)
*D. Yu. Vodolazov,Yu. P. Korneeva,M. A. Dryazgov,S. S. Ustavschikov,N. V. Porokhov,N. N. Osipov,I. Yu. Pashenkin,A. A. Korneev,M. A. Tarkhov*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper reports a giant nonlinearity of kinetic inductance in a superconducting NbN/Al hybrid strip, achieving a 50-fold change—far exceeding the tens-of-percent variation typical of conventional superconductors—due to proximity-induced superconductivity and a plateau in the current–momentum relation below the depairing current.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional superconductors exhibit only limited current-induced variation of kinetic inductance (at most tens of percent), which constrains the sensitivity and dynamic range of superconducting devices. There is a need for stronger nonlinearity to enable more sensitive detectors, amplifiers, and related technologies.

Method: The authors study the kinetic inductance L_k in a superconducting NbN/Al hybrid strip. They exploit proximity-induced superconductivity in the Al layer and select parameters such that the dependence of superconducting current I on Cooper pair momentum ℏq exhibits a plateau at a current I* well below the depairing current. This yields L_k ∼ dq/dI → ∞ as I → I*.

Result: A 50-fold change in kinetic inductance is observed, in contrast to conventional superconductors where current-induced variation does not exceed tens of percent. The effect is attributed to the plateau in the I(ℏq) relation occurring below the depairing current.

Conclusion: The giant nonlinearity of kinetic inductance in NbN/Al hybrid strips opens a new route for creating next-generation highly sensitive detectors of electromagnetic radiation, calorimeters, parametric amplifiers, and magnetometers.

Abstract: We demonstrate giant nonlinearity of the kinetic inductance $L_k$ in a superconducting NbN/Al hybrid strip. In contrast to ordinary superconductors, where the maximum observed current-induced variation of $L_k$ does not exceed tens of percent, we observe a 50-fold change in $L_k$. Physically, this effect is connected with the proximity-induced superconductivity in the Al layer and the specific choice of parameters for which the dependence of the superconducting current $I$ on the Cooper pair momentum $\hbar q$ exhibits a plateau at a current $I^*$ {\it well below} the depairing current. Formally, this leads to $L_k \sim dq/dI \to \infty$ as $I \to I^*$. We believe that our results open a new route for creating the next generation of highly sensitive detectors of electromagnetic radiation, calorimeters, parametric amplifiers, and magnetometers.

</details>


### [8] [Two superconducting pairing models with a Kitaev spin-liquid glue: microscopic kernel and parity alternation on odd-fold bond stars](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06157)
*Youping Dai*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: Two microscopic superconducting pairing models on doped Kitaev honeycomb show that bond-local glue sets a chirality–parity linkage governed by bond-star geometry, yielding odd-parity unitary-triplet (model 1, C=±2) and even-parity d+id singlet (model 2, C=±4) states, generalized by a parity-alternation theorem for chiral form factors.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superconducting pairing kernels in doped Kitaev honeycomb systems are typically assumed rather than derived, obscuring the microscopic origin of chirality and its relationship to parity. The paper aims to derive the pairing kernels from concrete microscopic selection rules (flux-sector and C3 little-group rules) and to establish how bond-star geometry—rather than the microscopic glue—fixes the chirality–parity linkage.

Method: Model 1 (odd parity) derives its kernel from the pure model's flux-sector selection rule, fixing a component-diagonal, nearest-neighbor separable, rank-one-per-bond form with wα=J_K^2 χα ∝ rα mα (mα from Hellmann–Feynman, rα≈0.24 numerically); the spin-vertex is a unitary triplet with bond-locked anisotropic d vector and a Gram-sum gap. Model 2 (even parity) derives its kernel from the C3 little-group selection rule of a zone-corner chiral phonon, with an explicit Cooper-channel momentum constraint; the charge-vertex pairing is d+id singlet, parity decomposition f±=g±+p± projecting onto g±, giving C=±4 and κxy/T=2κ0. A general theorem analyzes single-star chiral form factors on odd-fold bond stars.

Result: Model 1 yields an odd-parity unitary triplet with geometry-fixed nodal directions and spin-glue Chern number C=±2. Model 2 yields an even-parity d+id singlet with chirality slaved to phonon polarization, C=±4, κxy/T=2κ0; reversing pump helicity reverses Chern number, Kerr rotation, and thermal Hall sign. The general theorem shows the angular-momentum ladder of a single-star chiral form factor alternates in parity on odd-fold bond stars, so chiral states from such form factors are geometrically enforced singlet–triplet composites with universal amplitude ratios (n=3: d⊕p; n=5: g⊕p or d⊕f); coherent doubled-star combinations restore parity purity, and the mixed-state Chern number follows the dominant on-shell component across a topological boundary.

Conclusion: The chirality–parity linkage in doped Kitaev honeycomb superconductors is dictated by bond-star geometry via a bond-local glue neutral between even and odd pairing channels, not by the microscopic glue itself. Derived kernels give distinct parity-resolved topological states (spin glue C=±2; charge kernel C=±4) and a general parity-alternation theorem for chiral form factors on odd-fold bond stars.

Abstract: We construct two superconducting pairing models on doped Kitaev honeycomb, whose microscopic kernels are derived rather than assumed. Both are driven by a bond-local glue neutral between the even- and odd-parity pairing channels; the chirality--parity linkage is set by bond-star geometry, not by the microscopic glue. (i) Model~1(odd-parity sector): the kernel follows from the flux-sector selection rule of the pure model, fixing its component-diagonal, nearest-neighbor separable, rank-one-per-bond form, with $w_α=J_K^2χ_α\propto r_αm_α$, where $m_α$ follows from the Hellmann--Feynman theorem and $r_α\simeq0.24$ is computed numerically. The spin-vertex state is a unitary triplet with a bond-locked anisotropic $\mathbf d$ vector; its gap is a Gram sum of bond sines, so nodal directions are fixed by geometry. (ii) Model~2(even): the kernel is fixed by the $C_3$ little-group selection rule of a zone-corner chiral phonon, up to a Cooper-channel extension whose momentum constraint we state explicitly. The charge-vertex pairing is a $d+id$ singlet: the parity decomposition $f_\pm=g_\pm+p_\pm$ projects onto $g_\pm$, so chirality is slaved to the phonon polarization, with $C=\pm4$ and $κ_{xy}/T=2κ_0$. Reversing the pump helicity reverses the Chern number, Kerr rotation, and thermal Hall sign. (iii) General theorem: on odd-fold bond stars, the angular-momentum ladder of a single-star chiral form factor alternates in parity. Every chiral state selected by such a bond-local form factor is a geometrically enforced singlet--triplet composite with universal amplitude ratios ($n=3$: $d\oplus p$; $n=5$: $g\oplus p$ or $d\oplus f$); coherent doubled-star combinations can restore parity purity. The Chern number of the mixed state follows the dominant on-shell component across a topological boundary. The spin glue drives $C=\pm2$; the charge kernel $C=\pm4$.

</details>


### [9] [General boundary conditions for the quasiclassical kinetic equations in the presence of magnetism and unconventional superconductivity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06486)
*Tim Kokkeler,Yukio Tanaka,F. Sebastian Bergeret*

Main category: cond-mat.supr-con

TL;DR: The paper derives action-formalism-based boundary conditions for quasiclassical Green's functions at spin-active interfaces (with at least one diffusive material), yielding tractable expressions valid in normal and superconducting states, and applies them to anomalous proximity and Josephson effects.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin-active interfaces between diffusive materials lack clean, tractable boundary conditions for quasiclassical Green's functions that work in both normal and superconducting states; existing descriptions are cumbersome and don't readily extend to full counting statistics or anomalous proximity/Josephson phenomena.

Method: Boundary conditions are derived using the action formalism within the Keldysh-Nambu-spin formalism, ensuring validity in normal and superconducting states. Limiting cases are connected to prior literature, and normal, magnetoresistance, and spin-precession transport are described. The formalism also yields full counting statistics for charge, thermal, and spin currents.

Result: The authors obtain an analytic expression for the zero-bias conductance in p-wave superconductor/spin-filter junctions, showing its dependence on the relative orientation of the exchange field and d-vector. For Josephson junctions, they show the conditions on relative spin orientations for anomalous Josephson currents differ between two spin-split superconductors and a spin-split/triplet superconductor pair, consistent with time-reversal symmetry. Anomalous Josephson currents are accompanied by a diode effect only when spin-up and spin-down transparencies are both nonzero and distinct.

Conclusion: The action-formalism boundary conditions provide compact, versatile tools for spin-active interfaces, valid across normal and superconducting regimes, and reveal orientation-dependent anomalous proximity and Josephson phenomena, including conditions for the diode effect.

Abstract: We present boundary conditions for the quasiclassical Green's functions describing spin active interfaces between two materials, at least one of which is in the diffusive regime. These boundary conditions are developed with the help of the action formalism, and provide clean and easily tractable expressions. We connect our expressions to previous literature in limiting cases and describe normal transport, magneto-resistance transport and spin-precession through the bound ary. By using the Keldysh-Nambu-spin formalism, our equations are valid both in the normal and superconducting state. The construction through the action formalism also allows us to derive the corresponding full counting statistics for charge currents, thermal currents and spin currents. We use this boundary condition to consider the anomalous proximity effect, and to study the Josephson coupling between triplet and spin-split superconductors through a magnetic interface. We find an analytic expression for the zero bias conductance in junctions with a p-wave superconductor and a spin-filter, and show how it depends on the relative orientation of the exchange field and d-vector. For Josephson junctions, we show that, in agreement with time-reversal symmetry, the conditions on the relative orientations of the spins in the materials for the appearance of anomalous Josephson currents, are distinctly different for two spin-split superconductors and a spin-split superconductor with a triplet superconductor. Moreover, we show that the anomalous Josephson currents are only accompanied by a diode effect if both transparencies for spin up and down are nonzero and distinct.

</details>

### [10] [Space Charge Layer and Facile Halide Rearrangement Enable Fast Lithium-Ion Transport at Halide Solid Electrolyte Interfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03821)
*Md Salman Rabbi Limon,Matthew Beltran,Boyu Wang,Laisuo Su,Zeeshan Ahmad*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work unravels the origin of enhanced Li-ion conduction at Li2ZrCl6|Li3YCl6 halide heterointerfaces using DFT, defect thermodynamics, ML-driven MD, and vibrational/Raman spectroscopy, revealing a cooperative mechanism combining space-charge-layer formation with a dynamically flexible interfacial Cl framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Halide solid electrolytes are attractive for energy-dense all-solid-state batteries due to high-voltage stability, facile processing, and fast Li-ion conduction. A recent experiment showed that physically mixing two halides, Li2ZrCl6 (LZC) and Li3YCl6 (LYC), increases Li-ion conductivity by 46% and 58% relative to LZC and LYC, respectively. However, the atomistic origin of this heterointerface-driven enhancement remained unknown, motivating a mechanistic investigation.

Method: The authors combined density functional theory calculations, defect thermodynamics, machine-learning-driven molecular dynamics, and vibrational and Raman spectroscopy. Low-energy ordered supercells were used to represent the configurational disorder of LZC and LYC. Cl angular orientations around metal cations were analyzed in MD trajectories of a coherent interface, and simulated vibrational power spectra were compared with measured Raman spectra.

Result: Li and Cl interstitials are the dominant charged defects in both LZC and LYC. Forming an LZC|LYC interface favors interfacial Li Frenkel pair generation, producing Li interstitials in LZC and Li vacancies in LYC, thereby increasing near-interface charge-carrier concentration. The interface creates new stable Cl configurations that accommodate the framework rearrangement accompanying Li hops. Simulated vibrational power spectra and measured Raman spectra show lattice softening and octahedral distortion at the interface, consistent with these Cl configurations.

Conclusion: The enhanced Li-ion conduction arises from a cooperative mechanism in which space-charge-layer formation combines with a dynamically flexible interfacial halide framework. These findings provide atomistic design principles for superionic halide heterostructures and multicomponent solid electrolytes.

Abstract: Halide solid electrolytes are promising candidates for energy-dense all-solid-state batteries because they combine high-voltage stability, facile processing, and fast Li-ion conduction. A recent study showed that physically mixing two halide solid electrolytes, Li$_2$ZrCl$_6$ (LZC) and Li$_3$YCl$_6$ (LYC), raises the Li-ion conductivity by 46% and 58% relative to LZC and LYC, respectively, suggesting that halide heterointerfaces offer a new route to faster ion transport; however, the origin of this enhancement remains unknown. Here, we combine density functional theory calculations, defect thermodynamics, machine-learning-driven molecular dynamics, and vibrational and Raman spectroscopy to elucidate Li-ion transport in LZC, LYC, and their heterointerface. Using low-energy ordered supercells that represent the configurational disorder of these halides, we find that Li and Cl interstitials are the dominant charged defects in both materials. Forming an LZC|LYC interface favors the generation of interfacial Li Frenkel pairs, producing Li interstitials in LZC and Li vacancies in LYC, thereby increasing the charge-carrier concentration near the interface. By analyzing the angular orientations of Cl around the metal cations in molecular dynamics trajectories of a coherent interface, we show that the interface creates new stable Cl configurations that accommodate the framework rearrangement accompanying Li hops. Simulated vibrational power spectra and measured Raman spectra indicate lattice softening and octahedral distortion at the interface, consistent with the formation of these Cl configurations. These results support a cooperative mechanism in which space charge layer formation and a dynamically flexible interfacial halide framework enhance Li-ion conduction, providing atomistic design principles for superionic halide heterostructures and multicomponent solid electrolytes.

</details>


### [11] [Photoferroelectric Coupling and Polarization-Controlled Interfacial Band Modulation in van der Waal Compound CuInP2S6](https://arxiv.org/abs/2603.19148)
*Subhashree Chatterjee,Rabindra Basnet,Rajeev Nepal,Ramesh C. Budhani*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper provides direct nanoscale evidence of photoferroionic coupling in the van der Waals ferroelectric semiconductor CuInP2S6 (CIPS), showing that above-bandgap illumination jointly modulates band bending, ferroelectric switching, and Cu+ ionic relaxation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding how optical excitation couples with polarization and interfacial electrostatics in van der Waals ferroelectrics is essential for developing light-programmable nanoelectronic and optoelectronic devices.

Method: Correlated Kelvin probe force microscopy, piezoresponse force microscopy, and conductive atomic force microscopy were performed under above-bandgap illumination to probe illumination-induced changes in surface work function, surface photovoltage, coercive field, and imprint in CIPS.

Result: Illumination induced enhancement of surface work function, persistent surface photovoltage, reduced coercive field, and positive imprint shifts. These effects stem from synergistic photocarrier redistribution and slow Cu+ migration that reshape interfacial depletion widths and internal electric fields. Illumination-assisted barrier lowering further enhances carrier injection and produces sweep-rate-dependent ferroionic transport hysteresis.

Conclusion: Photoferroionic coupling is established as the governing mechanism for light-controlled band modulation and polarization stability in CIPS, offering a nanoscale framework for designing light-addressable ferroelectric memories, optoelectronic switches, and neuromorphic devices based on layered ferroionic materials.

Abstract: Understanding how optical excitation couples with polarization and interfacial electrostatics in van der Waals (vdW) ferroelectrics (FEs) is essential for the development of light-programmable nanoelectronic and optoelectronic devices. Here, we present direct nanoscale evidence of photoferroionic coupling in the vdW FE semiconductor CuInP2S6 (CIPS), where optical excitation jointly modulates electronic band bending, FE switching, and Cu+ ionic relaxation. The use of correlated Kelvin probe force microscopy, piezoresponse force microscopy, and conductive atomic force microscopy under above-bandgap illumination reveals illumination-induced enhancement of surface work function, persistent surface photovoltage, reduced coercive field, and positive imprint shifts. These effects arise from synergistic photocarrier redistribution and slow Cu+ migration that reshape interfacial depletion widths and internal electric fields. Illumination-assisted barrier lowering further enhances carrier injection and produces sweep-rate-dependent ferroionic transport hysteresis. Our results establish photoferroionic coupling as the governing mechanism for light-controlled band modulation and polarization stability in CIPS, providing a nanoscale framework for designing light-addressable FE memories, optoelectronic switches, and neuromorphic devices based on layered ferroionic materials.

</details>


### [12] [Equivariant generative diffusion learns and generalizes the structural ensemble of amorphous oxides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03973)
*Jun Jiang,Ian Berry,James N. Fry,Hai-Ping Cheng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: An SE(3)-equivariant denoising-diffusion model learns the configurational distribution of amorphous oxides from only 1,781 DFT configurations, enabling generation of >300,000-atom models at pair-potential-level cost and even discovering a lower-energy amorphous Zr-Ta-O structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Amorphous materials are statistical ensembles, not single definitive structures, but conventional DFT and machine-learned-potential simulations sample only a small fraction of that ensemble, so the full configurational distribution is poorly captured.

Method: An SE(3)-equivariant denoising-diffusion model is trained to learn the configurational distribution of amorphous oxides directly, making the model itself serve as the structure database. It is data-efficient (1,781 DFT configurations) and can propose structures for first-principles relaxation; generation can be steered toward a requested energy and extended to non-stoichiometric compositions, other densities, interfaces, and doping.

Result: The model reproduces partial radial distribution functions, coordination statistics, and bond-angle distributions, and generates models of over 3×10⁵ atoms at a cost comparable to the cheapest classical pair potentials. It locates an amorphous Zr-Ta-O structure 36 meV/atom below the previously known minimum. First-principles verification confirms energy-steered generation and shows the denoising training loss does not rank generative quality since the two measure different things.

Conclusion: The diffusion model functions as a generative structure database for amorphous oxides, achieving accuracy, data efficiency, and scalability while enabling exploration beyond trained conditions and revealing that training loss is not a proxy for generative quality.

Abstract: Amorphous materials are statistical ensembles rather than definitive structures, and conventional density-functional (DFT) and machine-learned-potential simulations sample only a small part of that ensemble. We present an $SE(3)$-equivariant denoising-diffusion model that learns the configurational distribution of amorphous oxides, so the model itself is the structure database. The learning is data efficient. A model trained on $1{,}781$ DFT configurations suffices to reproduce partial radial distribution functions, coordination statistics and bond-angle distributions, and to generate models of over $3\times10^{5}$ atoms at a cost comparable to that of the cheapest classical pair potentials. The trained model can propose amorphous atomic structures for first-principles relaxation to explore the configuration space. For example, it locates an amorphous Zr-Ta-O structure $36$ meV/atom below the previously known minimum. Generation can also extend beyond trained conditions to non-stoichiometric compositions, other mass densities, interfaces, and doping. First-principles verification confirms that generation can be steered to a requested energy, and shows that the denoising training loss does not rank generative quality, because the two measure different things.

</details>


### [13] [Studying the evolution of exchange and dipolar interactions and spin correlation in vdW magnet CrCl3 across magnetic phases](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04080)
*Prem B. Karki,Ayodimeji E. Aregbesola,Karishma Prasad,Jonathan Skeels,Avani Inamdar,Pooja Kalal,Jian Wang,Kapildeb Ambal**

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Broadband (1–16 GHz) magnetic resonance on bulk CrCl3 reveals exchange narrowing, exponentially evolving exchange, constant dipole-dipole coupling, and a room-temperature half-field transition indicating short-range magnetic correlations far above the global ordering temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding quantum interactions in van der Waals magnets is essential for their use in next-generation magnetic, electronic, and spintronic devices, yet the temperature evolution of exchange and dipole-dipole interactions in such materials remains insufficiently characterized.

Method: Multifrequency broadband (1–16 GHz) magnetic resonance (electron spin resonance) measurements on a bulk single-crystal CrCl3 sample, tracking resonance linewidth and half-field transitions as a function of temperature (room temperature down to 50 K) and resonance field.

Result: The dipolar-broadened low-field resonance linewidth narrows at higher resonance fields due to exchange narrowing. The exchange interaction evolves exponentially with decreasing temperature (room temperature to 50 K), while the dipole-dipole interaction stays constant over the same range. A half-field magnetic resonance transition is detected at room temperature, indicating robust dipole-dipole interaction among spins, with a measured inter-spin distance of 2.4(3) Å.

Conclusion: The evolving dipole interactions, half-field resonance, and strong exchange interaction indicate short-range ordering/correlation in CrCl3 at temperatures far above the magnetic global ordering temperature. The work also highlights the sensitivity and value of multifrequency electron spin resonance for probing quantum interactions and magnetic correlations in van der Waals magnetic materials.

Abstract: Understanding quantum interactions in van der Waals magnets are critical to realizing their potential for next-generation magnetic, electronic, and spintronic applications. In this study, we investigated the evolution of exchange and dipole-dipole interactions in van der Waals magnet CrCl3 using multifrequency broadband (1-16 GHz) magnetic resonance on a bulk single-crystal CrCl3 sample. We found that the dipolar-broadened low-field resonance linewidth narrows due to exchange narrowing at higher resonance fields. Our findings show that the exchange interaction evolves exponentially as we lower the sample temperature from room temperature to 50 K. However, we found that the dipole-dipole interaction remains constant in the same temperature range. We have also detected the half-field magnetic resonance transition at room temperature, indicating a robust dipole-dipole interaction among spins. The measured inter-spin distance is 2.4(3) A at room temperature. The evolution of dipole interactions, the observation of half-field magnetic resonance, and the strong exchange interaction indicate short-range ordering/correlation in CrCl3 at temperatures far above the magnetic global ordering temperature. This study also demonstrates the significance and sensitivity of the multifrequency electron spin resonance technique for understanding quantum interactions and magnetic correlations in van der Waals magnetic materials.

</details>


### [14] [Structural, electronic, and magnetic properties of HoCo$_2$ thin films grown by pulsed laser deposition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06655)
*Ajay Kumar,Christopher Burgio,Anis Biswas,Yaroslav Mudryk*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Highly smooth HoCo$_2$ thin films ($35\pm1$~nm) were grown on Si by pulsed laser deposition, showing reproducible ferrimagnetic ordering at $T_\mathrm{C}=77\pm3$~K, a continuous (second-order-like) transition, and enhanced coercivity ($\sim$600~Oe at 5~K) versus bulk.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bulk HoCo$_2$ exhibits a characteristic first-order ferrimagnetic transition; whether this ordering and transition character are retained in thin-film form is unclear, and understanding it is relevant for integrating such materials into thin-film devices.

Method: Pulsed laser deposition of HoCo$_2$ films on Si substrates, characterized by x-ray reflectivity (thickness and interface quality), atomic force microscopy (morphology/roughness), magnetization measurements (transition temperature, hysteresis, coercivity), and x-ray photoelectron spectroscopy (Ho $4d$, Co $2p$ core levels and valence band).

Result: Films are $35\pm1$~nm thick with smooth surfaces/interfaces (Kiessig fringes) and $\sim$170~pm roughness. Two films show reproducible ferrimagnetic transitions at $T_\mathrm{C}=77\pm3$~K, but without resolvable thermal hysteresis (continuous, second-order-like). At 5~K, hysteresis with $\sim$600~Oe coercivity. XPS shows comparable electronic structure to bulk.

Conclusion: Thin-film HoCo$_2$ preserves the bulk magnetic ordering temperature and local electronic structure, but its transition becomes continuous rather than first-order, and coercivity is strongly enhanced, indicating that dimensionality/thin-film effects modify the magnetic transition nature while retaining the essential electronic environment.

Abstract: We report the growth and physical properties of HoCo$_2$ thin films on Si substrate by pulsed laser deposition. Well-defined Kiessig fringes in the x-ray reflectivity data yield a film thickness of $35 \pm 1$~nm and demonstrate smooth film surface and interface profiles. Atomic force microscopy confirms the high morphological quality of the films, revealing subnanometer surface roughness of $\sim$170~pm. Two independently prepared films exhibit a reproducible ferrimagnetic transition at $T_{\mathrm{C}}=77\pm3$~K, demonstrating retention of the characteristic magnetic ordering of bulk HoCo$_2$. However, the absence of resolvable thermal hysteresis indicates a predominantly continuous, second-order like transition in the films, in contrast to the first-order transition of bulk HoCo$_2$. At 5~K, the films exhibit clear magnetic hysteresis with a coercive field of $\sim$600~Oe, substantially larger than the negligible coercivity of bulk HoCo$_2$. X-ray photoelectron spectroscopy reveals comparable Ho $4d$ and Co $2p$ core-level line shapes and broadly similar valence-band spectra for the bulk and film, indicating preservation of the essential local electronic environment and occupied electronic structure.

</details>


### [15] [Distilling universal machine-learning potentials for moiré lattices across one million atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04115)
*Thomas Huang,Yueyao Fan,Kaichen Xie,Bolun Li,Kaijie Yang,Jenna A. Bilbrey,Eric Bylaska,Peter V. Sushko,Di Xiao,Ting Cao*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: MoiréMLIP is a fine-tuned universal atomistic model that reproduces ab initio atomic reconstruction in moiré materials with 6-8 meV/Å force errors, transfers to unseen and smaller-twist structures, and via knowledge distillation (MoiréMLIP-mini) scales single-GPU inference to one million atoms, revealing hierarchical nanoscale polarization networks in twisted MoTe2.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Atomic reconstruction governs moiré materials across scales, from local structure and polarization textures to global electronic topology, but direct ab initio modeling is computationally prohibitive for large superstructures such as marginal-twist-angle moirés and moiré-of-moirés. Existing atomistic models face accuracy, transferability, and scaling bottlenecks.

Method: The authors develop MoiréMLIP by fine-tuning a universal atomistic model on the DFT-labeled Moiré Kaleidoscope dataset, which spans transition metal dichalcogenide compositions, symmetries, stackings, and twist angles. Knowledge distillation is then used to produce MoiréMLIP-mini for efficient large-scale inference.

Result: MoiréMLIP reproduces ab initio reconstruction with force errors of 6-8 meV/Å and transfers to smaller twist angles and unseen structures. MoiréMLIP-mini retains this accuracy while extending single-GPU inference to one million atoms. Applied to an alternate-twist MoTe2 trilayer, it reveals a hierarchical polarization network spanning tens of nanometers, arising from large moiré-cell distortions sharply localized along moiré-of-moiré domain walls.

Conclusion: The work overcomes key accuracy, transferability, and scaling bottlenecks of existing atomistic models, enabling predictive simulations across emergent moiré length scales.

Abstract: Atomic reconstruction reshapes moiré materials across multiple scales, from local structure and polarization textures to global electronic topology, yet direct \textit{ab initio} modeling becomes prohibitive for large superstructures such as marginal-twist-angle moirés and moiré-of-moirés. We develop MoiréMLIP by fine-tuning a universal atomistic model on the density functional theory labeled Moiré Kaleidoscope dataset, which spans transition metal dichalcogenide compositions, symmetries, stackings, and twist angles. MoiréMLIP reproduces \textit{ab initio} reconstruction with force errors of 6--8\,meV/Å and transfers to smaller twist angles and unseen structures. Knowledge distillation yields MoiréMLIP-mini, which retains this accuracy while extending single-GPU inference to one million atoms. Applied to an alternate-twist MoTe$_2$ trilayer, it reveals a hierarchical polarization network spanning tens of nanometers arising from large moiré-cell distortions sharply localized along the moiré-of-moiré domain walls. These results overcome key accuracy, transferability, and scaling bottlenecks of existing atomistic models and enable predictive simulations across emergent moiré length scales.

</details>


### [16] [Agentic Resource Allocation for Batch Multi-Objective Bayesian Optimization in Autonomous Materials Discovery](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04134)
*Robert Robinson,Shakti Prasad Padhy,Sushant Sinha,Sk Md Ahnaf Akif Alvi,Juan Florez Coronel,Brent Vela,Trevor Hastings,Douglas Allaire,Raymundo Arróyave*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper develops a multi-objective Bayesian optimization framework for alloy design under resource constraints, benchmarking adaptive policy-tuning strategies at each iteration, and shows that adaptive allocation improves information gain with modest hypervolume cost.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The discovery and development of advanced materials is challenging due to high time and monetary costs of synthesis, processing, and characterization, with enormous design spaces and multiple competing objectives. Most Bayesian optimization workflows rely on fixed exploration-exploitation policies that cannot adapt to shifting constraints in dynamic campaigns typical of self-driving laboratories.

Method: A multi-objective Bayesian optimization framework for alloy design under resource constraints is developed, benchmarking strategies for adaptive policy tuning at each iteration. The evaluation covers a septenary refractory high-entropy alloy (RHEA) system maximizing melting temperature while minimizing density, and an Fe-Co-Ni-based soft magnetic alloy system targeting saturation magnetization, coercivity, and hardness. Compared strategies include an exploitation-focused strategy, a fixed mixed exploratory/exploitative policy, and two distinct LLM-based adaptive strategies differing in batch allocation and campaign signal interpretation, tested across baseline and mid-campaign resource event conditions such as budget reductions, timeline cuts, and combined disruptions.

Result: Mixed allocation strategies accumulate substantially more mutual information than the exploitation-focused baseline at a proportionally smaller cost to hypervolume and optimization speed. Adaptive strategies outperform a fixed-mixed allocation policy by adjusting allocation in response to both evolving campaign statistics and resource constraints.

Conclusion: Adaptive resource allocation offers a favorable tradeoff for materials discovery campaigns, reducing predictive uncertainty on Pareto-optimal compositions.

Abstract: The discovery and development of advanced materials is a challenging process constrained by the high time and monetary costs of synthesis, processing, and characterization. The underlying design spaces can be enormous, often with multiple competing objectives. Bayesian optimization (BO) provides a principled approach for efficiently navigating such spaces, but most workflows rely on fixed exploration-exploitation policies that lack the capacity to adapt to shifting constraints in dynamic campaigns typical of self-driving laboratories. In this work, we develop a multi-objective BO framework for alloy design under resource constraints, benchmarking strategies for adaptive policy tuning at each iteration. Our evaluation covers a septenary refractory high-entropy alloy (RHEA) system focused on maximizing melting temperature and minimizing density, and an Fe-Co-Ni-based soft magnetic alloy system targeting saturation magnetization, coercivity, and hardness. We compare an exploitation-focused strategy, a fixed mixed exploratory/exploitative policy, and two distinct LLM-based adaptive strategies with different approaches to batch allocation and campaign signal interpretation, evaluated across baseline and mid-campaign resource event conditions including budget reductions, timeline cuts, and combined disruptions. Our results show that mixed allocation strategies accumulate substantially more mutual information than the exploitation-focused baseline at a proportionally smaller cost to hypervolume and optimization speed, with adaptive strategies outperforming a fixed-mixed allocation policy by adjusting their allocation in response to both evolving campaign statistics and resource constraints. These findings suggest that adaptive resource allocation offers a favorable tradeoff for materials discovery campaigns in reducing predictive uncertainty on Pareto-optimal compositions.

</details>


### [17] [MOSAIC: Addressing Space for Local, Steerable, and Scalable Atomistic Generation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04161)
*Ishan Nadkarni,Sebastien Hamel,Flynn Walsh,Fei Zhou*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Mosaic is a hybrid mesh-particle representation that partitions space into anchored regions with atom slots, enabling local spatial control and scalable, steerable generative modeling of molecules and large atomic structures exceeding one million atoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Realizing the potential of generative models in molecular discovery and materials science requires two capabilities: fine-grained local control over atomic placement and the ability to assemble large, heterogeneous structures. Existing approaches struggle to provide persistent spatial addressing or to scale generation to very large, multi-scale systems.

Method: Mosaic is a representation (not a model) that partitions space into anchored regions containing atom slots. Each slot encodes occupancy, atomic species, and a continuous displacement from its anchor, yielding persistent spatial addresses that support local conditioning, hierarchical generation, and scalable assembly. Because it is a representation, it can be paired with different generative backbones; it is tested with a fixed molecular backbone and applied to larger material scales.

Result: With the molecular backbone held fixed, Mosaic's local support and coordinate prior improve molecular connectivity and validity, sample efficiently across molecular sizes, and enable fine-grained spatial control. At larger scales, the same representation generates atomistic polycrystals conditioned on coarse spatial layouts. Locality reduces large-scale generation to a bounded assembly problem, enabling structures with more than one million atoms.

Conclusion: Local spatial addressing provides a practical foundation for steerable, cross-scale generation of matter, spanning individual molecules to large-scale engineering-relevant structures, and is model-agnostic since it is a representation rather than a specific architecture.

Abstract: Generative models are opening new possibilities in molecular discovery and materials science. Realizing this potential, however, requires both local control over atomic placement and the ability to assemble large, heterogeneous structures. We introduce Mosaic, a hybrid mesh-particle representation that partitions space into anchored regions containing atom slots. Each slot encodes occupancy, atomic species, and a continuous displacement from its anchor, providing persistent spatial addresses for local conditioning, hierarchical generation, and scalable assembly. Because Mosaic defines a representation rather than a model, it can be paired with different generative backbones. With the molecular backbone held fixed, its local support and coordinate prior improve molecular connectivity and validity, the resulting model samples efficiently across molecular sizes and enables fine-grained spatial control. At larger material scales, the same representation enables generation of atomistic polycrystals conditioned on coarse spatial layouts. Most notably, locality turns large-scale generation into a bounded assembly problem, enabling structure generation with more than one million atoms. Together, this establishes local spatial addressing as a practical foundation for steerable generation of matter across scales, from individual molecules to large-scale structures of broader engineering significance.

</details>


### [18] [Molecular Crystal Structure Prediction from Conditional Flow on the Unit Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04193)
*Qiang Zhu,Yihan Weng*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper decouples molecular crystal structure prediction into three steps—predicting invariant lattice descriptors with a flow model, reconstructing cell parameters via quasi-random sampling, and packing molecules under symmetry constraints—achieving matches for 83 of 84 single-component systems with Z' ≤ 1.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Crystal structure is jointly defined by space group symmetry, unit cell, and molecular alignment in the asymmetric unit, but predicting all three simultaneously is hard because it mixes discrete symmetry choices with high-dimensional continuous search.

Method: A three-step decoupled generation: a flow model learns the conditional distribution of invariant lattice descriptors (direct- and reciprocal-lattice successive minima, Selling scalars) from a molecular graph, a Hall setting, and Z'; then two sequential quasi-random sampling processes reconstruct cell parameters matching predicted lattice invariants and density, followed by a molecular packing search within the given symmetry and unit cell constraints.

Result: On 84 single-component systems with Z' ≤ 1, the approach reproduces experimental matches for 83 systems; the single failure is attributed to force-field limitations in preserving the experimental structure.

Conclusion: Learned cell proposals can effectively support crystal structure prediction (CSP) given a Hall setting and Z', with potential extension to fully blind prediction across variable symmetry and Z' settings.

Abstract: A molecular crystal structure is jointly described by its space group symmetry, unit cell, and the molecular alignment within the asymmetric unit. Concurrently predicting all three variables is a daunting task, as it mixes discrete symmetry choices with a high-dimensional search in the continuous space. To address this challenge, we decouple these variables using a three-step generation process. Specifically, we train a flow model to learn the conditional distribution of invariant lattice descriptors (e.g. direct- and reciprocal-lattice successive minima and Selling scalars) from a molecular graph, a Hall setting, and the number of molecules in the asymmetric unit ($Z'$). Using a two sequential quasi-random sampling processes, we first reconstruct the cell parameters that match the predicted lattice invariants and density requirements, and then conduct a molecular packing search within the give symmetry and unit cell constraint. On 84 single-component systems with $Z' \le 1$, our approach reproduces experimental matches for 83 systems; the remaining failure stems from force-field limitations in preserving the experimental structure. These results demonstrate that learned cell proposals can effectively support crystal structure prediction (CSP) for a given Hall setting and $Z'$, which may be extended to fully blind prediction with variable symmetry and $Z'$ settings in the future.

</details>


### [19] [Materials and Metrology for Out-of-Plane Damping-Like Spin-Orbit Torque](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04248)
*Xiaoxi Huang,Yuhan Liang,Orion Smedley,Daniel C. Ralph*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This Perspective critically reviews out-of-plane damping-like spin-orbit torques (SOTs) as a route to efficient switching of perpendicularly magnetized nanodevices, covering the material classes that host such torques, the methods used to quantify them, and the state of progress toward technologically viable torque efficiencies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Energy-efficient, high-density, non-volatile memory requires a more efficient means of switching the magnetization orientation of nanoscale magnets with perpendicular magnetic anisotropy. An unconventional out-of-plane damping-like SOT component is a promising candidate, but the field is fragmented: results from different measurement methods disagree, artifacts are misinterpreted, and qualitative observations are overinterpreted as evidence of technological promise.

Method: Perspective/critical review approach, structured along three axes: (1) classification of the materials capable of generating out-of-plane damping-like SOTs according to the underlying symmetry breaking—low crystal symmetry in single crystals, antiferromagnetic order, and ferromagnetic order; (2) analysis of the experimental techniques used to quantify these torques, including their limitations and artifact sources; and (3) assessment of reported torque efficiencies against the requirements of scalable magnetic technologies.

Result: Out-of-plane damping-like SOTs have been reported across a broad range of material platforms, arising primarily from the three symmetry-breaking classes noted. However, discrepancies between measurement methods, misinterpreted artifacts, and overinterpretation of qualitative observations have left the field fragmented and the reliability of reported efficiencies unclear.

Conclusion: A rigorous, quantitatively comparable understanding of out-of-plane damping-like SOTs is needed: material classes, measurement methodologies, and efficiency benchmarks must be reconciled before these torques can be judged suitable for scalable magnetic memory and logic technologies.

Abstract: A new generation of energy-efficient, high-density, and non-volatile memory devices could be enabled if a more efficient mechanism is developed for switching the magnetization orientation of nanoscale magnetic devices possessing perpendicular magnetic anisotropy. A promising strategy under widespread investigation is current-induced damping-like spin-orbit torque with a strong unconventional out-of-plane component, rather than only the conventional in-plane component. In recent years, such torques have been reported in a broad range of materials platforms, arising primarily from three classes of symmetry breaking within the material generating the torque: low crystal symmetry in single-crystalline materials, antiferromagnetic order, and ferromagnetic order. Despite this progress, the field remains fragmented. Measurements using different methods often disagree, artifacts have been misinterpreted, and qualitative observations are sometimes overinterpreted as evidence of technological promise. This Perspective will provide a critical overview of out-of-plane damping-like spin-orbit torques, analyzing the classes of materials which can generate such torques, the experimental methods by which the torques can be quantified, and the progress toward achieving torque efficiencies sufficient for scalable magnetic technologies.

</details>


### [20] [Out-of-Plane Oscillating Electric Fields Unlock Low-Temperature Nonequilibrium Superionicity in Quasi-Two-Dimensional AgCrSe2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05941)
*Jia-Wen Li,Kun Yang,Sheng Meng,Xinghua Shi,Wei-Hai Fang,Jin Zhang*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Electric-field-responsive machine-learning molecular dynamics shows that an out-of-plane oscillating field drives nonequilibrium superionic Ag+ transport in quasi-2D AgCrSe2 at 300 K, far below its ~475 K order-disorder transition, achieving ~1.7 S/cm via field-induced dynamic disorder.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superionic conductors offer fast solid-state ion transport with broad technological promise, but their highly conductive states are usually only accessible above the mobile-ion sublattice order-disorder transition, restricting low-temperature operation. A route to achieve superionic transport below this transition is therefore desirable.

Method: Electric-field-responsive machine-learning molecular dynamics simulations applied to quasi-two-dimensional AgCrSe2 under an out-of-plane oscillating electric field.

Result: At 300 K (well below the ~475 K transition), the field activates fast in-plane Ag+ transport with ionic conductivity ~1.7 S/cm, arising from field-driven dynamic disorder of Ag+ across two equivalent sublattices rather than thermal disordering. With increasing frequency, Ag occupation evolves from complete switching to a highly conductive dynamically disordered state and then to an ordered state with weak field response. These regimes shift to higher frequencies with increasing temperature or field amplitude, consistent with competition between the driving period and the Ag-sublattice response time. At strong fields the timescale matches field-assisted thermal activation, while weak-field deviations imply additional dynamics beyond single-ion activation.

Conclusion: Timescale-matched periodic driving is proposed as a route to transport-active ionic disorder far below an equilibrium superionic transition.

Abstract: Superionic conductors enable exceptionally fast ion transport in solids and offer broad technological potential. However, their highly conductive states are typically accessed only above the order-disorder transition of the mobile-ion sublattice, which limits low-temperature operation. Here, electric-field-responsive machine-learning molecular dynamics reveals a nonequilibrium route to superionic transport in quasi-two-dimensional AgCrSe2 driven by an out-of-plane oscillating electric field. At 300 K, far below the transition temperature near 475 K, the field activates fast in-plane Ag+ transport with an ionic conductivity reaching ~1.7 S/cm. This originates from field-driven dynamic disorder of Ag+ across two equivalent sublattices rather than thermally induced disordering. With increasing frequency, the Ag occupation evolves from complete switching to a highly conductive dynamically disordered state and finally to an ordered state with weak field response. These regimes shift to higher frequencies with increasing temperature or field amplitude, consistent with competition between the driving period and Ag-sublattice response time. At strong fields, this timescale is consistent with field-assisted thermal activation, while weak-field deviations suggest additional dynamics beyond single-ion activation. These results suggest timescale-matched periodic driving as a route to transport-active ionic disorder far below an equilibrium superionic transition.

</details>


### [21] [Band Structure Modulation in Ternary-Based InAs/ AlAs1-xSbx /InAs1-xSbx Superlattices](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04308)
*Arash Dehzangi,Jiakai Li*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A modified sp3s* empirical tight-binding method combined with the virtual crystal approximation and s-on-site energy bowing accurately models electronic band structure of InAs/InAs1-xSbx and InAs/InAs1-xSbx/AlAs1-xSbx strained-layer superlattices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To expand the capability of calculating electronic band structure for strained-layer superlattices composed of ternary InAs/InAs1-xSbx and related AlAs1-xSbx materials, which are relevant for infrared detection and barrier architectures.

Method: A modified sp3s* empirical tight-binding method is used to construct the superlattice Hamiltonian. The virtual crystal approximation is combined with a bowing parameter applied to the s-on-site energy to accurately capture ternary superlattice behavior. Numerical simulations cover diverse superlattice configurations spanning long wavelength infrared to wide-bandgap barrier architectures.

Result: Simulated energy levels strongly agree with experimental bandgap measurements of the ternary superlattices and their structural variants across the investigated configurations.

Conclusion: The proposed modified sp3s* tight-binding approach with VCA-based s-on-site bowing provides an accurate predictive tool for electronic band structure of ternary strained-layer superlattices, validated against experimental bandgaps.

Abstract: This study demonstrates an overall view of recent quantum mechanical based modeling effort using empirical tight-binding method to expand the ability of calculation of electronic band structure for strained-layer superlattices composed of ternary InAs/InAs1-xSbx and InAs/InAs1-xSbx/AlAs1-xSbx materials. Using a modified sp3s* empirical tight-binding method and building the superlattice Hamiltonian, we demonstrate that combining the virtual crystal approximation with a bowing parameter for the s-on-site energy accurately predicts ternary superlattice behavior. We provide a comprehensive overview of our numerical simulations, which model diverse superlattice configurations ranging from long wavelength infrared range to wide-bandgap barrier architectures. Notably, the simulated energy levels strongly agree with experimental bandgap measurements of the ternary superlattices and their structural variants.

</details>


### [22] [First-Principles Investigation of Multimodal Toxic Gas Sensing in Carbon-Tuned hBN-Graphene Alloys: Chemiresistive, Work-Function, and Optical Responses](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06283)
*Tanjuma Shikder Jhumu,Ahmed Zubair*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This DFT study evaluates hBN-graphene alloys (BNGr-2, BNGr-3) as chemiresistive, work-function-based, and optical gas sensors for Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN, finding strong sensitivity, distinct selectivity, and short recovery times.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Compact, reliable, and cost-effective gas sensors are in high demand for safety management across the medical sector, chemical manufacturing, food quality monitoring, agriculture, and industrial safety. Hazardous gas emissions must be controlled and monitored with fast-responsive, highly sensitive sensing devices, motivating the search for suitable sensing materials such as hBN-graphene alloys.

Method: First-principles density functional theory (DFT) was employed to investigate the adsorption behavior of Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN gas molecules on proposed hexagonal boron nitride (hBN)-graphene (Gr) alloys. Adsorption energy, charge transfer, bandgap alteration, chemiresistive sensitivity, selectivity ratios, work function variation, absorption spectra, and temperature-dependent desorption were analyzed.

Result: The 22% carbon alloy (BNGr-2) was most competent for sensing Cl2, CO, CO2, and HCN, with sufficient adsorption energy, charge transfer, and bandgap alteration. NO and NO2 engaged more strongly with the 33% carbon alloy (BNGr-3). NOx gases exhibited the highest chemiresistive sensitivity, while other gases showed significant sensitivity and distinct selectivity ratios. Noticeable work function variation (~20%) indicated potential as work-function-based sensors. Cl2 and NO2 showed strong physical adsorption; the rest were weakly to moderately physisorbed, giving very short recovery times (10^-1 to 10^-6 s). Distinctive absorption spectra highlighted potential as optical gas sensors, and all gas molecules can be freed from BNGr-2 at 425 K.

Conclusion: hBN-Gr alloys are promising candidates for gas sensing and pollution auditing, functioning as chemiresistive, work-function-based, and optical gas sensors with high sensitivity, selectivity, and rapid recovery.

Abstract: Compact, reliable, and cost-effective gas sensors have become a highly demanding subject for safety management in the medical sector, chemical manufacturing, food quality monitoring, agriculture, and industrial safety. Hazardous gas emissions need to be controlled and monitored with fast-responsive and highly sensitive sensing devices. A first-principles study employing density functional theory (DFT) was used to investigate the adsorption behavior of Cl2, CO, CO2, NO, NO2, and HCN gas molecules with our proposed alloys, which consisted of hexagonal boron nitride (hBN) and graphene (Gr). The alloy consisting of 22% carbon (BNGr-2) was found to be most competent for sensing Cl2, CO, CO2, and HCN with sufficient adsorption energy, charge transfer, and bandgap alteration. However, NO and NO2 gas molecules showed more engagement with 33% carbon-proportioned alloy (BNGr-3) in terms of adequate gas sensing properties. NOx gases exhibited the most chemiresistive sensitivity towards the adsorbents. Other gases also showed significant chemiresistive sensitivity and distinct selectivity ratios, which would facilitate these alloys as chemiresistive sensors. Besides, noticeable work function variation (~20%) of these systems manifested potential as work function based sensors. Cl2 and NO2 showed strong physical adsorption, while the rest of the gases were weakly to moderately physisorbed, resulting in very short recovery times (10-1 ~ 10-6 seconds). Additionally, the distinctive absorption spectra observed for the gas analyte systems highlighted the potential of the proposed alloys as optical gas sensors. Temperature variation revealed that all gas molecules can be freed from the adsorbent BNGr-2 at 425 K. These findings imply hBN-Gr alloys as promising gas sensors for pollution auditing.

</details>


### [23] [Kilovolt-class Vertical (011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 Schottky Diodes for High Voltage and High Temperature Applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04388)
*Emerson J. Hollar,Aaron M. Adams,Nolan S. Hendricks,Andrew J. Green,Esmat Farzana*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Kilovolt-class vertical (011) β-Ga2O3 Schottky diodes with TiO2/Al2O3 field-plate achieve 3.90–4.16 kV breakdown, excellent forward conduction, ~10^9 rectification ratio up to 150 °C, and low area-dependence, showing strong potential for high-temperature power switches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: β-Ga2O3 is attractive for high-voltage power electronics, but vertical Schottky barrier diodes suffer from premature breakdown, killer dislocation effects, and limited performance at elevated temperatures. There is a need to improve edge termination and field management while leveraging high-quality (011) epilayers with low background doping.

Method: Pt/(011) β-Ga2O3 Schottky barrier diodes with diameters from 100 to 300 μm were fabricated with and without a high-permittivity TiO2/Al2O3 dielectric field-plate. Systematic electrical characterization compared reverse blocking and forward conduction, including high-temperature measurements from 25 to 150 °C.

Result: Field-plate SBDs achieved breakdown voltages of 3.90–4.16 kV versus 3.06–3.16 kV without field-plate. Breakdown voltage showed excellent consistency across diode areas.
