High-temperature testing showed excellent forward conduction, a rectification ratio of ~10^9 over 25–150 °C, and kV-range breakdown with no evident leakage increase up to 150 °C.

Conclusion: Effective edge termination via a TiO2/Al2O3 field-plate, combined with advantageous (011) β-Ga2O3 epilayers showing low background doping and reduced killer dislocation effects, advances vertical β-Ga2O3 SBD performance for high-voltage and high-temperature power switching applications.

Abstract: We report kilovolt-class vertical (011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 Schottky diodes utilizing high-permittivity dielectric TiO2/Al2O3 field-plate for high voltage and high temperature applications. A systematic study was performed with Pt/(011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 Schottky barrier diodes (SBDs) with varied diameters from 100 μm to 300 μm, for both with and without field-plate, that revealed excellent consistency of reverse blocking performance regardless of diode area. The field-plate SBDs achieved superior breakdown voltages (3.90 - 4.16 kV) compared to the diodes without field-plate (3.06 - 3.16 kV) owing to effective edge termination. Furthermore, we explored high-temperature performance of the field-plate (011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 diodes that revealed excellent forward conduction properties and high rectification ratio (~10^9) throughout the temperature range (25 - 150 C). At reverse bias, the field-plate diodes also exhibited kV-range breakdown voltage with no evident increase in leakage current up to the explored elevated temperature of 150 C. The low area-dependence of breakdown voltage, excellent forward transport properties, and minimal reverse leakage at both high-voltage and elevated temperature demonstrate the exciting potential of (011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 SBDs in high-temperature power switches. Thus, our work demonstrates the strategy of advancing the performance of vertical \b{eta}-Ga2O3 SBDs by utilizing the advantageous (011) \b{eta}-Ga2O3 epilayers with low background doping, reduced killer dislocation effects, and effective field management for high-voltage and high-temperature applications.

</details>


### [24] [Quantitative near-atomic-resolution oxidation-state mapping in complex oxide heterostructures by electron energy-loss spectroscopy](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04797)
*Ioannis Iatrakis,Zitao Zhang,Ricardo Egoavil,Emma van der Minne,Christoph Baeumer,Frank de Groot*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper introduces a theoretical framework for quantitative oxidation-state mapping of transition-metal and rare-earth elements using near-atomic-resolution EELS in complex oxide heterostructures, combining Dirac-Hartree-Fock ionization cross sections with ligand-field multiplet theory.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Complex oxide heterostructures contain multiple transition-metal and rare-earth elements with overlapping spectral features, making it difficult to extract reliable oxidation states and local electronic structure from EELS. A quantitative, generic method for valence mapping is needed.

Method: The total inelastic differential cross section of transition-metal L2,3 and rare-earth M4,5 edges is calculated by combining relaxed Dirac-Hartree-Fock ionization cross sections with ligand-field multiplet theory for discrete transitions. The approach is applied to two correlated-oxide heterostructures: La0.67Sr0.33MnO3/LaCr0.2Mn0.2Fe0.2Co0.2Ni0.2O3/SrTiO3 and NdNiO3/La0.67Sr0.33MnO3/NdGaO3.

Result: The method resolves oxidation states of Cr, Mn, Fe, and Co and identifies the Ni contribution within the LaCr0.2Mn0.2Fe0.2Co0.2Ni0.2O3 layer at near-atomic resolution. It finds predominantly Co2+ with mixed Mn2+/Mn3+/Mn4+ character, indicating site-specific valence redistribution. It also separates overlapping La-M4,5 and Ni-L2,3 edges in the NdNiO3/La0.67Sr0.33MnO3/NdGaO3 heterostructure, recovering the intrinsic Ni-L2,3 excitation spectrum and showing that the effective Ni valence in NdNiO3 deviates from a simple ionic picture.

Conclusion: The work establishes a generic method for quantitative valence mapping from near-atomic-resolution EELS, applicable to a wide range of experimental conditions for studying the local electronic structure of complex oxides.

Abstract: We present a theoretical framework for quantitative oxidation state mapping of transition-metal and rare-earth elements from near-atomic-resolution electron energy-loss spectroscopy (EELS) in complex oxide heterostructures. The total inelastic differential cross section of transition-metal L$_{2,3}$ and rare-earth M$_{4,5}$ edges is calculated by combining the relaxed Dirac-Hartree-Fock ionization cross section with ligand-field multiplet theory for the discrete transitions. We apply the approach to two correlated-oxide heterostructures, La$_{0.67}$Sr$_{0.33}$MnO$_3$/LaCr$_{0.2}$Mn$_{0.2}$Fe$_{0.2}$Co$_{0.2}$Ni$_{0.2}$O$_3$/SrTiO$_3$ and NdNiO$_3$/La$_{0.67}$Sr$_{0.33}$MnO$_3$/NdGaO$_3$, which include a wide range of transition-metal and rare-earth elements with overlapping features. The method resolves the oxidation states of Cr, Mn, Fe and Co and identifies the Ni contribution within the LaCr$_{0.2}$Mn$_{0.2}$Fe$_{0.2}$Co$_{0.2}$Ni$_{0.2}$O$_3$ layer at near-atomic resolution. We find predominantly Co$^{2+}$ together with mixed Mn$^{2+}$/Mn$^{3+}$/Mn$^{4+}$ character, indicating site-specific valence redistribution within the layer. We also separate overlapping spectral features, such as La-M$_{4,5}$ and Ni-L$_{2,3}$ edges in the NdNiO$_3$/La$_{0.67}$Sr$_{0.33}$MnO$_3$/NdGaO$_3$ heterostructure, thereby recovering the intrinsic Ni-L$_{2,3}$ excitation spectrum. We find that the effective Ni valence in NdNiO$_3$ deviates from the simple ionic picture. More broadly, this work establishes a generic method for quantitative valence mapping from near-atomic-resolution EELS, applicable to a wide range of experimental conditions for the study of local electronic structure of complex oxides.

</details>


### [25] [Emergent magnetic nonanalyticity in Dirac altermagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04496)
*Xizhi Fu,Zhenqiao Huang,Fei Yang,Mengli Hu,Xingkai Cheng,Junwei Liu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Symmetry permits responses that may not actually emerge; in Dirac altermagnets, a perturbation can drive a Lifshitz reconstruction yielding a nonanalytic effective potential. This nonanalyticity gives C-paired spin-polarized Dirac cones a magnetic manifestation: breaking the pairing symmetry C converts the Lifshitz reconstruction into itinerant magnetization, making piezomagnetism nonlinear and nonanalytic (M ∝ sgn(ε)ε²) while the symmetry-allowed linear response vanishes. Monolayer Fe₂S is identified as a candidate material.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Symmetry-based paradigms (Neumann's principle and analytic Landau expansion) have long been the organizing principle for material responses, assuming that what symmetry permits is what emerges. This work challenges that assumption, showing that actual responses can differ from symmetry-allowed ones, especially for symmetry-related gapless Dirac fermions.

Method: Theoretical analysis of gapless Dirac fermions such as in altermagnets: investigating how a perturbation shifting Dirac points drives a Lifshitz reconstruction and generates an intrinsically nonanalytic effective potential. Application to Dirac altermagnets with C-paired spin-polarized Dirac cones. First-principles calculations to identify candidate materials.

Result: Perturbations drive a Lifshitz reconstruction producing a nonanalytic effective potential and singular responses. In Dirac altermagnets, breaking pairing symmetry C lifts degeneracy and converts the Lifshitz reconstruction into itinerant magnetization. Piezomagnetism becomes nonlinear and nonanalytic with M ∝ sgn(ε)ε², while the linear response is absent despite being symmetry-allowed. Monolayer Fe₂S is identified as a candidate material.

Conclusion: The leading response cannot be inferred from the conventional symmetry-based paradigm built on Neumann's principle and analytic Landau expansion. Quantum singularities are proposed as a new degree of freedom for engineering material responses.

Abstract: Symmetry has long provided the organizing principle for understanding how materials respond to external perturbations. Here we show that what is permitted by symmetry need not be what actually emerges from the underlying system, and this distinction becomes particularly consequential for symmetry-related gapless Dirac fermions such as in altermagnets. A perturbation that shifts the Dirac points drives a Lifshitz reconstruction and generates an intrinsically nonanalytic effective potential, giving rise to undiscovered singular responses. In Dirac altermagnets, this nonanalyticity acquires a magnetic manifestation through $C$-paired spin-polarized Dirac cones: breaking the pairing symmetry $C$ lifts their degeneracy and converts the associated Lifshitz reconstruction into an itinerant magnetization. As a consequence, piezomagnetism, the induction of a net magnetization by a mechanical strain, becomes nonlinear and nonanalytic, with $M\propto\mathrm{sgn}(\varepsilon)\varepsilon^2$, whereas the linear response is absent despite being allowed by symmetry. Accordingly, the leading response cannot be inferred from the conventional symmetry-based paradigm of material response built upon the cornerstones of Neumann's principle and analytic Landau expansion. First-principles calculations identify the monolayer Fe$_2$S as a candidate material realization for such singular magnetic responses. These results suggest quantum singularities as a new degree of freedom for engineering material responses.

</details>


### [26] [Development and Application of a Magnetoelectric Coupling Measurement System for Strain-Mediated Effects](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04599)
*Samanway Mohanta,Poonacha C. T,Hemant Singh,Gunjan Verma,A. Ahlawat,S. C. Das,G. Sinha,V. G. Sathe,D. K. Shukla*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A dynamic lock-in magnetoelectric (ME) measurement system was developed and used to study strain-mediated coupling in PMN-PT/NFO and CFO/BTO composites; PMN-PT/NFO shows a much stronger ME coefficient (~1.85 vs ~0.147 mV mm⁻¹ Oe⁻¹), attributed to strong piezoelectricity and low magnetic anisotropy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To investigate strain-mediated ME coupling in magnetoelectric composites and identify the material parameters that govern the strength of the ME response.

Method: Development of a dynamic lock-in based ME coupling measurement system and its application to two magnetoelectric composites: 0.65Pb(Mg₁/₃Nb₂/₃)O₃-0.35PbTiO₃/NiFe₂O₄ (PMN-PT/NFO) and CoFe₂O₄/BaTiO₃ (CFO/BTO).

Result: PMN-PT/NFO exhibits a significantly enhanced ME coefficient (α_ME ≈ 1.85 mV mm⁻¹ Oe⁻¹) compared to CFO/BTO (≈ 0.147 mV mm⁻¹ Oe⁻¹). Antisymmetric field dependence and 180° phase reversal confirm a strain-driven coupling origin in both samples.

Conclusion: The superior response of PMN-PT/NFO arises from high piezoelectric strength of PMN-PT combined with low magnetic anisotropy of NFO, whereas the high magnetic anisotropy of CFO and weaker piezoelectricity of BTO limit the ME output, highlighting the decisive role of magnetic softness and piezoelectric strength in optimizing strain-mediated ME coupling.

Abstract: This paper reports about development of a dynamic lock-in based magnetoelectric (ME) coupling measurement system and its application to investigate strain-mediated coupling in 0.65Pb(Mg$_{1/3}$Nb$_{2/3}$)O$_3$-0.35PbTiO$_3$/NiFe$_2$O$_4$ (PMN-PT/NFO) and CoFe$_2$O$_4$/BaTiO$_3$ (CFO/BTO) composites. The PMN-PT/NFO composite exhibits a significantly enhanced ME coefficient ($α_{\mathrm{ME}} \approx 1.85$~mV\,mm$^{-1}$\,Oe$^{-1}$) compared to CFO/BTO ($\approx 0.147$~mV\,mm$^{-1}$\,Oe$^{-1}$). The antisymmetric field dependence and $180^\circ$ phase reversal confirm the strain-driven origin of coupling in both the samples. The superior response of PMN-PT/NFO arises from the combined effect of high piezoelectric strength in PMN-PT and low magnetic anisotropy of NFO, whereas the high magnetic anisotropy of CFO and weaker piezoelectricity of BTO limit the ME output. These results highlight the decisive role of magnetic softness and piezoelectric strength in optimizing strain-mediated ME coupling.

</details>


### [27] [The Hidden Cost of Two-Dimensional TEM Reconstruction of Magnetic Nanoparticle Morphology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04713)
*Iago López-Vázquez,Amira Páez,Yilian Fernández-Afonso,Roy W. Chantrell,Thomas S. van Zanten,Sergiu Ruta,Lucía Gutiérrez,Òscar Iglesias,David Serantes*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A statistical inverse-reconstruction method infers 3D morphology of quasi-spherical iron oxide nanoparticles from conventional 2D TEM projections, showing that triaxial and size-polydisperse particle populations are needed to jointly reproduce measured size and axial-ratio distributions, with strong implications for magnetic hyperthermia predictions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional TEM only records 2D projections of 3D objects, making nanoparticle morphology inference difficult. The limitation is examined for quasi-spherical iron oxide nanoparticles imaged both after deposition on a TEM grid and after cellular internalization and resin immobilization.

Method: A statistical inverse-reconstruction approach is developed: synthetic 3D particle populations are randomly oriented, projected onto a TEM-like plane, and compared with experimental equivalent-diameter and axial-ratio distributions. Models of increasing geometrical complexity are tested, from monodisperse prolate spheroids to size- and shape-polydisperse prolates and triaxial ellipsoids. The inferred population is evaluated physically via a macrospin model including shape-anisotropy contributions.

Result: Size polydispersity is necessary to reproduce the projected equivalent-diameter distribution, while relaxing axial symmetry through a triaxial description enables simultaneous reproduction of both size and axial-ratio distributions. The inferred population is a statistically compatible 3D morphology, not a unique particle-by-particle reconstruction. Application to magnetic fluid hyperthermia shows pronounced sensitivity of the predicted heating response to particle geometry and polydispersity.

Conclusion: The method provides a general framework linking conventional TEM measurements to morphology-dependent properties of nanoparticle ensembles, and highlights the importance of accounting for triaxiality and polydispersity when predicting functional responses such as magnetic hyperthermia.

Abstract: Conventional transmission electron microscopy (TEM) records two-dimensional (2D) projections of three-dimensional (3D) objects, complicating the inference of nanoparticle morphology. This limitation is investigated for quasi-spherical iron oxide nanoparticles (IONPs) imaged after deposition on a TEM grid and after cellular internalization and immobilization in resin. A statistical inverse-reconstruction method is developed in which synthetic 3D particle populations are randomly oriented, projected onto a TEM-like plane, and compared with the experimental equivalent-diameter and axial-ratio distributions. Models of increasing geometrical complexity are considered, from monodisperse prolate spheroids to size- and shape-polydisperse prolate populations and triaxial ellipsoids. Size polydispersity is required to reproduce the projected equivalent-diameter distribution, whereas relaxing axial symmetry through a triaxial description enables simultaneous reproduction of the size and axial-ratio distributions. The inferred population represents a statistically compatible 3D morphology rather than a unique particle-by-particle reconstruction. Its physical relevance is assessed through a macrospin model incorporating the corresponding shape-anisotropy contributions. Application to magnetic fluid hyperthermia reveals a pronounced sensitivity of the predicted heating response to particle geometry and polydispersity. The approach provides a general framework for connecting conventional TEM measurements with morphology-dependent properties of nanoparticle ensembles.

</details>


### [28] [Wavelength-Dependent Photoluminescence Mapping of Depth-Dependent Degradation Under Thermal Stress in Phenethylammonium Tetrafluoroborate Treated Perovskite Solar Cells](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04714)
*Julisa Juarez,Akash Dasgupta,Alan Zhan,Lamtiur P. Samosir,David S. Ginger**

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work introduces multiwavelength excitation photoluminescence (PL) as a nondestructive technique to separate interfacial from bulk recombination losses in perovskite semiconductors, demonstrated on PEABF4-treated stacks during thermal aging.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-time degradation studies in perovskite semiconductors are challenging because conventional methods cannot decouple interfacial from bulk processes or require destructive analysis, creating a need for nondestructive, depth-sensitive characterization.

Method: Multiwavelength excitation photoluminescence (PL) spectroscopy combined with diffusion-recombination simulations, using phenethylammonium tetrafluoroborate (PEABF4) treatment as a model system to probe excitation-wavelength-dependent PL decay signatures linked to surface recombination velocity and bulk lifetime.

Result: PEABF4 passivates the ETL/perovskite interface, reducing interfacial recombination losses from fullerene deposition. Under thermal aging, however, PEABF4-treated stacks degrade faster, with recombination losses broadening from the ETL/perovskite interface into the absorber.

Conclusion: Multiwavelength excitation PL is established as a nondestructive, depth-sensitive platform for studying recombination losses during aging of perovskite films, supported by simulation-based interpretation of PL decay signatures.

Abstract: Studying real-time degradation in perovskite semiconductors remains an important challenge. Conventional methods often cannot separate interfacial and bulk processes or rely on destructive analysis. Here, we demonstrate multiwavelength excitation photoluminescence (PL) as a nondestructive approach to distinguish interface- and bulk-dominated recombination losses. Using phenethylammonium tetrafluoroborate (PEABF4) treatment as a model, we use PL to show that PEABF4 passivates the electron transport layer (ETL)/perovskite interface, reducing interfacial recombination losses from fullerene deposition. However, upon thermal aging, PEABF4-treated stacks degrade more rapidly, with recombination losses broadening from the ETL/perovskite interface into the absorber. Diffusion-recombination simulations support interpreting excitation-wavelength-dependent PL decay signatures consistent with changes in surface recombination velocity and bulk lifetime, providing a framework for depth-sensitive comparisons during aging. Collectively, these results further establish multiwavelength excitation PL as a platform for studying recombination losses during aging of perovskite films.

</details>


### [29] [Site-Specific Orbital Symmetry Breaking in Epitaxial Graphene on Silicon Carbide](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04760)
*Vinod K. Paidi,Yves Joly,Alex Taekyung Lee,Guillaume Radtke,Vlado K. Lazarov,Demie Kepaptsoglou,Quentin M. Ramasse,Matthieu Bugnet*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Atomic-scale EELS in an aberration-corrected STEM, combined with ab initio calculations, reveals that the epitaxial graphene buffer layer on SiC(0001) has a modified bonding environment: covalent C–Si bonding induces partial sp³ hybridization, quenches out-of-plane π states, and breaks in-plane orbital degeneracy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Epitaxial graphene on SiC is a leading platform for graphene electronics, and controlling its electronic transport requires understanding the electronic structure of the buffer (interfacial) layer at the atomic scale, which differs from free-standing graphene.

Method: Monochromated electron energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) in an aberration-corrected scanning transmission electron microscope (STEM) was used to probe the C K-edge fine structure of the buffer layer at atomic resolution. Ab initio calculations provided a site-resolved probe of orbital character to explain the spectral features.

Result: The C K-edge fine structure of the buffer layer differs distinctly from that of free-standing graphene, giving direct experimental evidence of a modified bonding environment. Covalent bonding between one-quarter of the buffer-layer carbon atoms and substrate silicon atoms induces partial sp³ hybridization, quenching out-of-plane π states and disrupting π-conjugation across the interface. Substrate-imposed symmetry breaking further lifts the in-plane orbital degeneracy.

Conclusion: An orbital-engineering framework based on π-state quenching and site-specific symmetry breaking provides a quantitative basis for tailoring graphene–substrate interfaces for next-generation low-energy transistor applications.

Abstract: The epitaxial graphene grown on SiC interface has been the subject of tremendous research efforts to control its electronic transport properties in the quest for graphene electronic applications. The electronic structure of the epitaxial graphene buffer layer on SiC(0001) is investigated at the atomic scale using monochromated electron energy loss spectroscopy (EELS) in an aberration-corrected scanning transmission electron microscope (STEM). The C $K$-edge reveals spectral fine structures distinctly different from free-standing graphene layer, providing direct experimental evidence of a modified bonding environment. Ab initio calculations providing a site-resolved probe of orbital character to elucidate the origin of these spectral features: covalent bonding between one-quarter of the buffer-layer carbon atoms and the substrate silicon atoms induces partial $\mathrm{sp}^3$ hybridization, quenching the out-of-plane $π$ states and disrupting $π$-conjugation across the interfacial layer. Substrate-imposed symmetry breaking further lifts the in-plane orbital degeneracy. This orbital-engineering framework, rooted in $π$-state quenching and site-specific symmetry breaking, establishes a quantitative basis for tailoring graphene-substrate interfaces toward next-generation low-energy transistor applications.

</details>


### [30] [Phenomenon-first problem formulation with language models in metal-organic frameworks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04866)
*Jihan Kim*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A phenomenon-first LLM framework decouples guest physics, host capabilities, and operating context to propose testable qualitative materials behaviors, yielding nine tractable candidates from 80 guest-host MOF combinations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Materials discovery pipelines typically presuppose a supplied objective. The paper aims to move LLM reasoning upstream, from executing given targets toward prospectively formulating qualitative phenomena worthy of investigation.

Method: An LLM receives separate descriptions of guest physics, host capabilities, and operating context, then proposes an unusual qualitative behavior together with its mechanism, distinguishing signature, and falsifier. Proposals were frozen before evaluation or materials selection. 80 guest-host combinations were tested in metal-organic frameworks, with a repeat run and a guest-only baseline.

Result: 57 proposals were generated, of which nine were computationally tractable. Structured formulation reproducibly broadened mechanistic exploration: original and repeat runs each covered 21 broad mechanism families versus eight for the guest-only baseline, despite fewer proposals. MC-010 showed reproducible hydration-driven reorientation of retained ethanol in experimentally reported MOFs; MC-015 showed a quantum-corrected reversal of H2 adsorption-region preference relative to the matched classical description.

Conclusion: Structured LLM reasoning can prospectively formulate qualitative materials phenomena, positioning the model upstream of objective execution in materials discovery.

Abstract: Here we introduce a phenomenon-first framework for materials discovery in which an LLM receives separate descriptions of guest physics, host capabilities and operating context and proposes an unusual qualitative behavior with its mechanism, distinguishing signature and falsifier. Across 80 guest-host combinations in metal-organic frameworks, the model generated 57 proposals that were frozen before evaluation or materials selection; assessment identified nine computationally tractable candidates. In a repeat and guest-only baseline, structured formulation reproducibly broadened mechanistic exploration: the original and repeat runs each covered 21 broad mechanism families versus eight for guest-only formulation despite fewer proposals. From the original run, MC-010 yielded reproducible hydration-driven reorientation of retained ethanol in experimentally reported MOFs, while MC-015 yielded a quantum-corrected reversal of H$_2$ adsorption-region preference relative to the matched classical description. These results show how structured LLM reasoning can move upstream from executing supplied objectives toward prospectively formulating qualitative materials phenomena.

</details>


### [31] [Identification and Structural Decomposition of Hidden Defect Configurations: A Case Study of Charged Oxygen Divacancies in HfO$_2$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04892)
*Hyunjin Lee,Yoon Kyeung Lee,Keun Heo,Shi Liu,Taehun Lee*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A DFT study of oxygen-divacancy complexes in HfO2 reveals hidden low-energy defect configurations whose mixed monoclinic/tetragonal/polar-orthorhombic local bonding reshapes surrounding atomic environments and alters kinetic branching between competing polymorphs.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Configurational searches have uncovered hidden defect structures in crystalline compounds, but how their local bonding arrangements modify the surrounding atomic environment is poorly understood. This work addresses that gap using HfO2, which hosts energetically competing monoclinic (M), tetragonal (T), and polar orthorhombic (PO) polymorphs.

Method: A local-distortion search with DFT relaxation under tetragonal-lattice confinement for the c-axis oxygen-divacancy complex (Tc) identifies a hidden family of low-energy configurations (Tc*). Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions (SOAP) descriptors are used for structural decomposition of the fully charged complex (2VO2+), complemented by independent analyses of Hf coordination and bond geometry. Transformation pathways to M and PO are compared via solid-state nudged elastic band calculations with cell-relaxed endpoints, both under and after release of the lattice constraint.

Result: The idealized Tc reference connects barrierlessly to the lowest-energy mixed-motif Tc* configuration, lowering energy by 18.4 meV/f.u. within the confined landscape. The fully charged complex exhibits mixed M-, T-, and PO-like character. After releasing the lattice constraint, barriers to M and PO increase from 6.9 and 10.1 meV/f.u. for the idealized reference to 26.9 and 15.3 meV/f.u. for the Tc*-derived structure, making PO the lower-barrier branch instead of M.

Conclusion: Structural decomposition links hidden defect configurations to changes in the surrounding atomic environment and to kinetic branching between competing polymorphs, with hidden mixed-motif configurations altering which polymorphic pathway is kinetically favored in HfO2.

Abstract: Extensive configurational searches have revealed hidden defect structures in crystalline compounds, yet how their local bonding arrangements modify the surrounding atomic environment remains less well understood. We examine this question in hafnium oxide (HfO$_2$), which hosts energetically competing monoclinic (M), tetragonal (T), and polar orthorhombic (PO) polymorphs. For the $c$-axis oxygen-divacancy complex ($T_c$), a local-distortion search with density-functional theory (DFT) relaxation under tetragonal-lattice confinement identifies a hidden family of low-energy configurations ($T_c^{*}$). For the fully charged complex ($2\mathrm{V}_{\mathrm{O}}^{2+}$), structural decomposition using Smooth Overlap of Atomic Positions (SOAP) descriptors reveals mixed M-, T-, and PO-like character, supported by independent analyses of Hf coordination and bond geometry. The idealized $T_c$ reference connects barrierlessly to the lowest-energy mixed-motif $T_c^{*}$ configuration, lowering the energy by $18.4$ meV/f.u. within the confined landscape. After releasing the lattice constraint, we compare transformation pathways to M and PO using solid-state nudged elastic band calculations with cell-relaxed endpoints. The respective barriers increase from $6.9$ and $10.1$ meV/f.u. for the idealized reference to $26.9$ and $15.3$ meV/f.u. for the $T_c^{*}$-derived structure. The larger increase for M makes PO, rather than M, the lower-barrier branch. This case study shows how structural decomposition connects hidden defect configurations with changes in the surrounding atomic environment and kinetic branching between competing polymorphs.

</details>


### [32] [Anisotropic mesoscale magnetic scattering across the spin-reorientation transition in TbMn6Sn6](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04903)
*Zhuang Xu,Fan Chen,Hui Cheng,Xinmin Wang,Taisen Zuo,He Cheng,Haitao Hu,Cuiping Zhang,Qian Zhao,Michel Kenzelmann,Jonathan S. White,Junying Shen,Xin Tong*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: SANS on bulk TbMn6Sn6 finds no long-range-ordered skyrmion lattice; instead, an anisotropic twofold low-Q magnetic scattering response evolves across the spin-reorientation regime, with distinct field and temperature dependence for H || b and H || c.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Real-space imaging of thin TbMn6Sn6 specimens has revealed helical stripes, biskyrmion-like textures, and skyrmion-bubble-like structures in the spin-reorientation regime, but whether these local textures form a bulk long-range-ordered skyrmion lattice was unresolved.

Method: Small-angle neutron scattering (SANS) was used to probe the mesoscale magnetism of a bulk TbMn6Sn6 single crystal, examining magnetic scattering under different field and temperature conditions, including zero field, 345 K with H || b, and 310 K with H || c near TSR.

Result: No sixfold SANS Bragg pattern indicating a long-range-ordered skyrmion lattice was observed. The dominant response was an anisotropic twofold low-Q scattering component. In zero field, a broad, horizontally elongated low-Q signal develops above TSR and weakens toward the paramagnetic state. At 345 K with H || b, the signal is diffuse and suppressed rapidly below about 700 Oe. At 310 K with H || c, near TSR, the response is more concentrated, line-shaped, and persists to higher fields.

Conclusion: Bulk TbMn6Sn6 exhibits an anisotropic mesoscale magnetic response across the spin-reorientation regime, consistent with stripe- or domain-related textures seen by real-space imaging, but the measurements do not uniquely identify their real-space morphology.

Abstract: TbMn6Sn6 is a centrosymmetric ferrimagnetic kagome metal that combines topological electronic states with strong magnetic anisotropy and a near-room-temperature spin-reorientation transition (SRT). Real-space imaging studies of thin specimens have revealed helical stripes, biskyrmion-like textures, and skyrmion-bubble-like structures in the spin-reorientation regime. Whether such local textures develop into a bulk long-range-ordered skyrmion lattice remains unresolved. Here we present small-angle neutron scattering (SANS) results probing the mesoscale magnetism of a bulk TbMn6Sn6 single crystal. We observe no sixfold SANS Bragg pattern indicative of a long-range-ordered skyrmion lattice under the explored conditions. Instead, the dominant magnetic response is an anisotropic twofold low-Q scattering component whose intensity distribution and field dependence evolve markedly across the spin-reorientation regime. In zero field, a broad and horizontally elongated low-Q signal develops above TSR and weakens as the system approaches the paramagnetic state. At 345 K with H || b, the signal is diffuse and is suppressed rapidly below about 700 Oe. By contrast, at 310 K with H || c, close to TSR, the response is more concentrated and line-shaped and persists to higher fields. The contrasts between these scans likely reflect the combined effects of rapidly evolving magnetic anisotropy near TSR and the field geometry. Our results therefore establish a bulk anisotropic mesoscale magnetic response across the spin-reorientation regime, consistent with stripe- or domain-related textures reported by real-space imaging, while not uniquely identifying their real-space morphology.

</details>


### [33] [Computing at the Edge Enabled by Indoor Photovoltaics and Ferroelectrics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05133)
*Robert L. Z. Hoye,Markus Hellenbrand,Nuno Estrócio,Austin M. Kay,Alice Scardina,Alessandro Mezzetti,Ignasi Fina,Jorge Íñiguez-González,Luís S. Marques,Francesco Matteucci,Quanxi Jia,Judith L. MacManus-Driscoll,Bert Offrein,Florencio Sánchez,Beatriz Noheda,George Koutsourakis,Marina Freitag,Gregory Burwell,Giulia Grancini,Jose P. B. Silva*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This perspective argues that distributed intelligence (edge computing) powered by high-performance indoor photovoltaics and (anti)ferroelectric materials for energy storage and neuromorphic computing can offer a more sustainable alternative to energy-intensive centralized AI.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The rapid, pervasive growth of AI hosted in large data centers strains electricity and water supplies, which are increasingly precious. Centralized computation entails substantial communication energy costs, and edge computing/distributed intelligence has been held back by the lack of reliable local energy supplies matching computational energy requirements.

Method: A perspective analysis examining the requirements of indoor photovoltaics (IPVs) for distributed intelligence, assessing the extent to which emerging materials meet these requirements, identifying current gaps, and proposing how they could be addressed—highlighting (anti)ferroelectric materials for electrostatic energy storage and low-power in-memory neuromorphic computing.

Result: Advances in high-performance indoor photovoltaics for local energy harvesting, combined with computational cost reductions via neuromorphic or in-memory computing devices, create opportunities to overcome the local energy supply bottleneck. (Anti)ferroelectric materials are argued to surpass bottlenecks for both electrostatic energy storage and low-power in-memory neuromorphic computing.

Conclusion: Co-creation between energy harvesting, storage, and computing is essential for making distributed intelligence a reliable and more sustainable alternative to centralized AI.

Abstract: The exponential, pervasive rise of artificial intelligence (AI), hosted in large data centres, will test the limits of the underpinning infrastructure, including electricity and water supplies, which are becoming increasingly precious resources. Rather than performing all AI computations in centralized servers, for many functionalities, these can be performed locally across a network of billions of small, autonomous nodes, saving substantial energy costs associated with communication. This alternative paradigm is known as edge computing, or distributed intelligence (DI), but has been held back by the lack of availability of reliable local energy supplies matching the energy requirements of the computational infrastructure. Opportunities to address this challenge are emerging with rapid advances in high-performance indoor photovoltaics (IPVs) for local energy harvesting, as well as reductions in computational cost through neuromorphic or in-memory computing devices. In this perspective, we discuss the requirements of IPVs for DI, the extent to which emerging materials fulfil these requirements, and how current gaps could be addressed. We make the case that (anti)ferroelectric materials can surpass bottlenecks for both electrostatic energy storage and low-power in-memory neuromorphic computing. The core theme of this perspective is that co-creation between energy harvesting, storage and computing is essential for making DI a reliable and more sustainable alternative to centralized AI.

</details>


### [34] [Sliding and stacking thin homo-monolayers from energy landscapes and combinatorics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05363)
*Jose D. Mella,Jose Luis Cabellos,Bernardo S. Mendoza,Salvador Barraza-Lopez*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A DFT-based combinatorial "divide and conquer" framework enumerates all possible stacking configurations of arbitrary numbers of identical monolayers, validated on non-moiré SnSe, and explains experimental second-harmonic optical responses.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The relationship between stacking order and function in layered materials is critical: knowing all possible ways to stack and slide identical monolayers enables the design of functionalities such as nonlinear optical responses and ferroelectricity. Furthermore, mounting experimental evidence indicates that few-monolayer and bulk samples exhibit differing stacking configurations, motivating the need for a general framework to determine stacking configurations.

Method: Using density functional theory (DFT), ground-state and metastable non-moiré SnSe stacks were first identified. These were then employed to validate a "divide and conquer" combinatorial approach for determining the most probable stacking configurations in materials with an arbitrary number of identical monolayers. Finite-temperature effects were also investigated, despite the granularity of available data.

Result: A small number of point groups were found to account for all possible stacks, which explains experimental second-harmonic optical responses. The approach was validated on non-moiré SnSe stacks.

Conclusion: This comprehensive framework readily generalizes to provide a predictive description of arbitrary metastable few-homomonolayer stacks.

Abstract: Structure leads to function: Knowing all possible ways to stack and slide identical monolayers enables the design of functionalities such as nonlinear optical responses and ferroelectricity in layered materials. Besides, mounting experimental evidence points to differing stacking configurations in few-monolayer and bulk samples. To exemplify the core concepts for stacking arbitrary monolayers, ground-state and metastable non-moiré SnSe stacks were first identified using density functional theory and employed to validate a ``divide and conquer'' combinatoral approach for determining the most probable stacking configurations in materials with an arbitrary number of identical monolayers. A small number of point groups accounts for all possible stacks, which explains experimental second-harmonic optical responses. Finite-temperature effects are also studied despite the granularity of available data. This comprehensive framework readily generalizes to provide a predictive description of arbitrary metastable few-homomonolayer stacks.

</details>


### [35] [A Scale-Separated Full-Field Monopole Method for Precipitation Modelling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05436)
*C. W. Sinclair*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: A scale-separated monopole method using Ewald decomposition is developed to predict precipitation when particle-scale diffusion fields coexist with long-wavelength solute variations, validated on Al–Sc.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Direct quasistatic monopole formulations fail when spatially heterogeneous particle populations generate slowly relaxing long-wavelength concentration fields, so a method bridging particle-level kinetics and mesoscale solute redistribution is needed.

Method: Precipitates are represented by monopole Green's-function solutions. The direct quasistatic formulation is validated against transient finite-difference calculations and a Kampmann–Wagner numerical model. An Ewald decomposition separates the diffusion field by spatial scale: screened particle-scale fields are treated quasistatically, while the complementary long-range field evolves by transient diffusion.

Result: The direct formulation is validated but becomes inappropriate for heterogeneous particle populations with slowly relaxing long-wavelength fields. The hybrid Ewald-based formulation retains explicit particle-level kinetics while allowing mesoscale solute redistribution on its physical timescale. Applied to Al–Sc, a transient depletion field from localized precipitation suppresses subsequent nucleation and produces persistent spatial variations in precipitate number density and volume fraction.

Conclusion: Scale separation via Ewald decomposition enables efficient full-field precipitation prediction, coupling explicit particle-level growth and dissolution with physically timed mesoscale solute transport.

Abstract: A scale-separated monopole method is developed for full-field prediction of precipitation under conditions where particle-scale diffusion fields coexist with solute variations over much larger distances. Precipitates are represented by monopole Green's-function solutions, providing a particle-based description of interacting particle growth and dissolution. The direct quasistatic formulation is validated against transient finite-difference calculations and a conventional Kampmann--Wagner numerical model, but becomes inappropriate when spatially heterogeneous particle populations generate slowly relaxing long-wavelength concentration fields. An Ewald decomposition is therefore used to separate the diffusion field by spatial scale. Screened particle-scale fields are treated quasistatically, while the complementary long-range field evolves by transient diffusion. The resulting hybrid formulation retains explicit particle-level precipitation kinetics while allowing mesoscale solute redistribution to evolve on its physical timescale. Application to Al--Sc demonstrates that a transient depletion field generated by localized precipitation can suppress subsequent nucleation and produce persistent spatial variations in precipitate number density and volume fraction.

</details>


### [36] [Deconvoluting Mechanisms of Radiation-Induced Grain Boundary Segregation in Dilute Ni-X (X = Fe, Cr, Mn) Alloys via Ion Irradiation and Phase-field Modeling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05849)
*Sohail Shah,Sourabh Bhagwan Kadambi,Anshul Kamboj,Benjamin Diaz,Xinchang Zhang,Jana Howard,Michael Moorehead,Boopathy Kombaiah,Lin Shao,Mukesh Bachhav*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Systematic Ni²⁺ irradiations of dilute Ni-Fe, Ni-Cr, and Ni-Mn alloys (500–1000 K, 2 dpa) combined with STEM-EDS and rate-theory phase-field modeling reveal that GB segregation is governed by distinct, solute-specific mechanisms: Fe depletes via the inverse Kirkendall effect, Cr segregation arises from a competition between SIA- and vacancy-mediated transport, and Mn enriches driven by thermodynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Radiation-induced GB composition redistribution in Ni-based structural alloys threatens the long-term deployment of advanced nuclear reactors, yet the mechanisms controlling solute-specific segregation remain poorly understood because defect-mediated transport, thermodynamic driving forces, and competing microstructural sinks act simultaneously under irradiation.

Method: Dilute Ni-Fe, Ni-Cr, and Ni-Mn binary alloys were irradiated with Ni²⁺ ions at 500, 800, and 1000 K to 2 dpa. GB chemistry was characterized by STEM-EDS, and segregation behavior was interpreted using rate-theory phase-field modeling informed by atomistic transport data. Depth-dependent characterization along individual GBs, spanning irradiated and unirradiated regions, was used to separate radiation-induced from thermodynamic contributions.

Result: Fe depletes at GBs at all temperatures via preferential vacancy-solute exchange (inverse Kirkendall effect). Cr segregation reflects a temperature-dependent competition between SIA-mediated transport toward GBs and vacancy-mediated transport away from GBs, producing near-zero net segregation at 500 K and depletion at higher temperatures. Mn shows distinct behavior dominated by thermodynamic segregation, driving local GB enrichment. Void formation at 800 K and irradiation-induced GB migration at 800 and 1000 K further suppress and redistribute solutes.

Conclusion: Interpreting GB chemistry evolution under irradiation requires concurrent treatment of non-equilibrium kinetic transport, thermodynamic driving forces, bulk sink effects, and GB migration. The unirradiated region along the same GB serves as a valid internal thermodynamic reference for isolating radiation-induced contributions.

Abstract: Radiation-induced composition redistribution at grain boundaries (GBs) in Ni-based structural alloys poses a critical challenge for the long-term deployment of advanced nuclear reactors. The mechanisms governing solute-specific segregation remain poorly understood because defect-mediated transport, thermodynamic driving forces, and competing microstructural sinks can all contribute under irradiation. We perform systematic Ni2+ ion irradiations on dilute Ni-Fe, Ni-Cr, and Ni-Mn binary alloys at 500, 800, and 1000 K to 2 dpa and characterize GB chemistry using STEM-EDS. The segregation behavior is interpreted using rate-theory phase-field modeling informed by atomistic transport data. We find that Fe depletes at GBs across all temperatures via preferential vacancy-solute exchange (inverse Kirkendall effect). Cr segregation reflects a temperature-dependent competition between self-interstitial atom (SIA)-mediated transport toward GBs and vacancy-mediated transport away from GBs, yielding near-zero net segregation at 500 K and depletion at higher temperatures. In contrast, Mn exhibits distinct behavior dominated by thermodynamic segregation that drives local enrichment at the GB. We leverage depth-dependent characterization along the same GB, spanning irradiated and unirradiated regions, to separate radiation-induced and thermodynamic contributions and validate the unirradiated region as an internal thermodynamic reference. Void formation at 800 K and irradiation-induced GB migration at 800 and 1000 K further add mechanistic complexity by suppressing and redistributing the solute. Our combined experimental and computational assessments show that interpreting GB chemistry evolution under irradiation requires concurrent treatment of non-equilibrium kinetic transport, thermodynamic driving forces, bulk sink effects, and GB migration.

</details>


### [37] [Geometrical Confinement of Magnetic Domains and Domain Walls on Two-Dimensional Cylindrical-Shaped Ferromagnetic Surfaces](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05970)
*Gabriel Martínez-Carracedo,Aurelio Hierro-Rodríguez,Amador García-Fuente,Jaime Ferrer*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper analytically and numerically studies domain wall (DW) confinement and interaction in ferromagnetic van der Waals nanotubes, showing that transverse curvature compresses DW width and that two interacting DWs form a bound state with non-monotonic spacing versus tube perimeter.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rolling 2D vdW magnetic monolayers into cylindrical nanotubes offers a way to confine magnetic domain walls free of edge-pinning effects, motivating a systematic understanding of how curvature governs DW confinement and DW-DW interactions in quasi-1D spintronic architectures.

Method: Analytical Heisenberg model on a 2D rectangular lattice to derive the azimuthal exchange energy penalty from transversal curvature; validation against atomistic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (LLG) spin dynamics simulations; extension to realistic CrSBr (CSB) nanotubes with micromagnetic simulations of two interacting DWs.

Result: Transversal curvature introduces an azimuthal exchange energy penalty that intrinsically compresses DW width, with excellent quantitative agreement between the analytical model and atomistic LLG simulations. In CSB nanotubes, two DWs form a bound state from the balance of long-range dipolar attraction and short-range exchange repulsion, and the inter-wall distance varies non-monotonically with tube perimeter.

Conclusion: vdW magnetic nanotubes are robust platforms for stable domain confinement in quasi-one-dimensional spintronic architectures.

Abstract: The rolling of two-dimensional (2D) van der Waals (vdW) magnetic monolayers into cylindrical architectures provides an ideal platform for confining magnetic domain walls (DWs) without edge-pinning effects. Here, we present a combined analytical and numerical investigation of DW confinement and interaction in ferromagnetic (FM) nanotubes. Using an analytical Heisenberg model on a 2D rectangular lattice, we show that transversal curvature introduces an azimuthal exchange energy penalty that intrinsically compresses the DW width. This model demonstrates excellent quantitative agreement with atomistic Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert (LLG) spin dynamics simulations. By extending our study to realistic CrSBr (CSB) nanotubes, we model a bound state of two interacting DWs arising from a balance between long-range dipolar attraction and short-range exchange repulsion. Micromagnetic simulations reveal a non-monotonic evolution of the inter-wall distance with the tube perimeter. These findings highlight vdW magnetic nanotubes as robust platforms for stable domain confinement in quasi-one-dimensional spintronic architectures.

</details>


### [38] [Compressed magnetic Moment Tensor Potentials via low-rank matrix and tensor factorizations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06158)
*Alexey S. Kotykhov,Alexander V. Shapeev,Maxim Rakhuba,Ivan S. Novikov*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: The paper proposes a parameter-reduced magnetic Moment Tensor Potential (mMTP) using matrix and tensor decompositions, achieving 1.5-3x parameter reduction without loss of accuracy, validated on Fe-Al and CrN systems.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetic machine-learning potentials like mMTP are accurate but parameter-heavy, which increases computational and memory cost. Reducing parameters while retaining predictive fidelity is desirable for practical large-scale simulations.

Method: The authors apply various matrix and tensor decomposition techniques to compress the mMTP model, reducing the number of parameters. They benchmark the compressed potentials on magnetic, structural, and vibrational properties and in molecular dynamics simulations of Fe-Al and CrN.

Result: Parameter count is reduced by roughly a factor of 1.5-3 with no degradation on the validation set. Simulation results from the compressed mMTP are numerically consistent with the original uncompressed model and agree with DFT calculations and experimental data.

Conclusion: Matrix/tensor decomposition enables effective compression of mMTP, yielding substantial parameter savings while preserving accuracy, making the potential more efficient for magnetic-material simulations.

Abstract: We propose a parameter-reduced version of magnetic Moment Tensor Potential (mMTP) based on various matrix and tensor decompositions. The resulting compressed magnetic machine-learning potential reduces the number of parameters by a factor of approximately 1.5-3 without compromising predictive performance on the validation set. We evaluate the performance of the compressed potentials for magnetic, structural, and vibrational properties, as well as in molecular dynamics simulations of Fe-Al and CrN systems. We demonstrate that the simulation results obtained with the compressed mMTP are numerically consistent with those of the original uncompressed model and are in agreement with density functional theory calculations and experimental data.

</details>


### [39] [State aligned graph wavelet fingerprints of electron trapping in amorphous Si$_3$N$_4$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06173)
*Jonathon Cottom,Emilia Olsson*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: State-aligned graph-wavelet fingerprints resolve coexisting charge-trap mechanisms in a-Si3N4 by linking electronic identity to multiscale network reorganisation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Classifying charge traps in amorphous materials requires resolving the electronic state and the network response together, since these are coupled and cannot be treated independently.

Method: Develops state-aligned graph-wavelet fingerprints and applies them to 415 pairs of relaxed neutral and negatively charged a-Si3N4 cells, aligning the trap state before and after localisation to connect electronic identity with multiscale structural fingerprints.

Result: Charge-driven conversion causes substantial reorganisation across graph scales and increases fingerprint amplitude. Fingerprint change identifies each site's transformation, occupation-induced level shift tracks localisation gain, and graph deformation tracks stabilisation. Charge-induced defect formation dominates the deepest spectral-trap decile: 69% form at sites whose Si-N bond breaks on capture, although this route accounts for 44.2% of classified traps.

Conclusion: The fingerprints unify electronic identity, multiscale network reorganisation, and energetic stabilisation into a quantitative framework for resolving charge trapping in amorphous materials.

Abstract: Classifying charge traps in amorphous materials requires the electronic state and the network response to be resolved together. We develop state-aligned graph-wavelet fingerprints and apply them to electron trapping in a-Si$_3$N$_4$, where intrinsic-polaronic trapping, coordination-driven trapping, and charge-driven conversion coexist within one ensemble. Across 415 pairs of relaxed neutral and negatively charged cells, alignment of the trap state before and after localisation connects electronic identity to multiscale structural fingerprints. Charge-driven conversion produces substantial reorganisation across graph scales and increases the fingerprint amplitude. The fingerprint change identifies the transformation each site undergoes, while the occupation-induced level shift tracks localisation gain and graph deformation tracks stabilisation. Charge-induced defect formation dominates the deepest spectral-trap decile: 69\% form at sites whose Si--N bond breaks on capture, although this route accounts for 44.2\% of the classified traps. The fingerprints unify electronic identity, multiscale network reorganisation and energetic stabilisation in a quantitative framework for resolving charge trapping in amorphous materials.

</details>


### [40] [Influence of Electrode Thickness and Electrolyte Composition on SEI Formation and Cycle Stability in Binder-Free Hard Carbon Anodes for Na-Ion Batteries](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06253)
*Tien-Nguyen-Khoi Trinh,Adrian Beda,Samar Hajjar-Garreau,Cyril Vaulot,Cam{é}lia Matei Ghimbeu*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Thickness and macroporosity of hard carbon self-supported electrodes (HC SSEs) strongly govern SEI formation and cycling stability; the FEC additive enables stable cycling for most electrodes except very thick ones.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Hard carbon self-supported electrodes offer a binder-, solvent-, conductive-carbon-, and current-collector-free alternative to classical anodes for sodium-ion batteries, but the influence of electrode thickness, macroporosity, and electrolyte additives on SEI formation and electrochemical performance remains insufficiently understood.

Method: Three cellulose-based filter paper precursors of different thicknesses were pyrolyzed at 1500 °C to yield HC SSEs, which were compared with an HC SSE from commercial carbon fabric. Structural, morphological, porosity, and surface chemical properties were characterized, and electrochemical tests were performed in NaPF6 electrolyte with and without fluoroethylene carbonate (FEC) additive.

Result: High initial Coulombic efficiency (up to 93%) and high reversible capacity (up to 328 mAh g−1 at 37.2 mA g−1) were achieved. Without FEC, the thinnest electrode (79 μm) reached a maximum of 50 cycles, while electrodes >185 μm faded rapidly (after ≈1–5 cycles). With FEC, fading was mitigated and 100 cycles were reached for all electrodes except the commercial one (5 cycles) due to its 1042 μm thickness. The FEC-derived thin, homogeneous, inorganic-rich SEI containing more NaF is more stable, protective, and electronically conductive.

Conclusion: Appropriate structural, morphological, porosity, and surface chemical properties are essential for high ICE and reversible capacity, whereas cycle stability is greatly impacted by electrode thickness/macroporosity and the electrolyte additive. FEC-induced inorganic-rich SEI markedly improves cycling stability, though extreme thickness remains detrimental.

Abstract: Hard carbon self-supported electrodes (HC SSEs) have gained significant interest as an alternative to classical binder-based anode materials for sodium-ion batteries (SIBs), eliminating the need for binders, solvents, conductive carbon, and current collectors. In this work, we investigate the impact of HC thickness and macroporosity, as well as the presence/absence of an electrolyte additive, on the formation of the solid electrolyte interphase (SEI) and electrochemical performance. Three cellulose-based filter paper (FP) precursors with different thicknesses were used to prepare HC SSEs through a pyrolysis process at 1500 {\textdegree}C, and then these were compared with an HC SSE obtained from commercial carbon fabric. The results highlight the importance of appropriate structural, morphological, porosity, and surface chemical properties of the materials to achieve high initial Coulombic efficiency (up to 93%) and high reversible capacity (up to 328 mAh g -1 at 37.2 mA g -1 ). However, the cycle stability was greatly impacted by the electrode thickness/macroporosity and the electrolyte additive. In NaPF6 without fluoroethylene carbonate (FEC) additive, the maximum number of cycles (50) was obtained for the thinnest electrode (79 $μ$m), while rapid fading (after $\approx$1-5 cycles) was observed for electrode thicknesses >185 $μ$m. By using the FEC additive, the fading is mitigated and cycling stability (100 cycles) is reached for all the electrodes, except the commercial one (5 cycles), due to its highest thickness (1042 $μ$m). The improvement in stability could be explained by the buildup of a thin homogeneous inorganic-rich SEI containing more NaF in the presence of FEC, which is more stable, protective, and electronically conductive.

</details>


### [41] [The phonomagnet: Spontaneous order of phonon angular momentum](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06395)
*Maike Fahrensohn,R. Matthias Geilhufe*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: Phonons can spontaneously order their angular momentum to generate a distinct "phonomagnetism" via spin-phonon angular-momentum coupling, with VI3 identified as a candidate phonomagnet.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnetism is conventionally attributed to collective ordering of electronic spin and orbital angular momentum. While other quasiparticles interact with magnetism, they have not been considered a primary source of it, leaving unexplored possibilities for novel magnetic phenomena.

Method: The authors develop a theory of phonomagnetism emerging from spin-phonon angular-momentum coupling, which produces an effective exchange interaction between local ionic angular momenta. They show the interaction strength depends on spin susceptibility and derive phononic analogues of spin models (phonon Heisenberg, phonon Γ-model, phonon Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions). First-principles phonon calculations are combined with Raman spectroscopy data.

Result: A phonomagnetic phase is predicted near a ferromagnetic critical point. VI3 is identified as a candidate phonomagnet, providing a microscopic framework for the weak magnetic anomaly observed above its bulk ferromagnetic transition.

Conclusion: Phonons can generate a distinct form of magnetism through spontaneous angular-momentum ordering, opening avenues for new platforms for information storage and manipulation on picosecond timescales.

Abstract: Magnetism is a fundamental property of matter, typically arising from collective ordering of electronic spin and orbital angular momentum. Other quasiparticles interact with magnetism. However, they have not been considered its primary source. Here we show that phonons can generate a distinct form of magnetism through spontaneous ordering of their angular momentum. This phonomagnetism emerges from spin-phonon angular-momentum coupling, producing an effective exchange interaction between local ionic angular momenta. We show that the interaction strength depends on spin susceptibility, with a phonomagnetic phase predicted near a ferromagnetic critical point. Our theory also reveals phononic analogues of established spin models, including phonon Heisenberg, phonon $Γ$-model, and phonon Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions. Combining first-principles phonon calculations with recent data from Raman spectroscopy, we identify VI$_3$ as a candidate phonomagnet, providing a microscopic framework for interpreting the weak magnetic anomaly reported above its bulk ferromagnetic transition. These findings open avenues for new platforms for information storage and manipulation on picosecond timescales.

</details>


### [42] [Quantum spectral thermodynamics and active learning enable million-scale exploration of high-entropy ceramics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06467)
*Jie Sun,Yiheng Shen*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This work introduces a quantum spectral thermodynamic framework linking disorder-induced phonon spectral broadening to free energy, combined with uncertainty-guided active learning that screens 7.7 million high-entropy ceramic configurations at DFT fidelity with 10^5-fold acceleration, revealing an intrinsic vibrational stabilization mechanism and a ~12 at.% solute threshold governing strengthening versus softening.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding phase stability and navigating the vast compositional spaces of multicomponent solids are central, unresolved challenges in solid-state chemistry, largely because conventional approaches rely on ideal configurational entropy and cannot tractably explore millions of compositions at DFT accuracy.

Method: A quantum spectral thermodynamic framework that connects interaction-induced phonon spectral broadening (phonon self-energy effects from chemical disorder) to free energy, paired with an uncertainty-guided active-learning workflow that explores 7.7 million high-entropy ceramic configurations at density-functional-theory fidelity.

Result: The workflow achieves a 10^5-fold acceleration. Phonon self-energy effects from chemical disorder supply an intrinsic vibrational contribution to thermodynamic stabilization beyond ideal configurational entropy, compensating unfavorable mixing enthalpies and suppressing phase separation. A robust ~12 at.% solute threshold separates strengthening from softening regimes, linked to filling of metal-carbon antibonding states. Chemical disorder also yields high-temperature mechanical stiffness combined with low thermal conductivity and anomalous temperature-dependent lattice heat transport.

Conclusion: The study establishes a quantum spectral foundation for connecting many-body interactions to thermodynamics and phase stability, and offers a scalable framework for exploring previously inaccessible multicomponent chemical spaces.

Abstract: Understanding phase stability and navigating vast compositional spaces in multicomponent solids remain central challenges in solid-state chemistry. Here, we develop a quantum spectral thermodynamic framework connecting interaction-induced phonon spectral broadening to free energy, alongside an uncertainty-guided active-learning workflow that explores 7.7 million high-entropy ceramic configurations at density-functional-theory fidelity, achieving a $10^5$-fold acceleration. We show that phonon self-energy effects arising from chemical disorder provide an intrinsic vibrational contribution to thermodynamic stabilization beyond ideal configurational entropy, compensating unfavorable mixing enthalpies and suppressing phase separation. Across the chemical space, we uncover a robust ~12 at.% solute threshold separating strengthening and softening regimes, associated with the filling of metal-carbon antibonding states. Chemical disorder further enables an unusual combination of high-temperature mechanical stiffness and low thermal conductivity, together with anomalous temperature-dependent lattice heat transport. This work establishes a quantum spectral foundation for connecting many-body interactions to thermodynamics and phase stability, while providing a scalable framework for exploring previously inaccessible multicomponent chemical spaces.

</details>


### [43] [Strain tunability of electron and hole g-factors in CsPb$X_3$: bulk and nanocrystals](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06696)
*Aleksander Cząstkiewicz,Paweł Scharoch,Krzysztof Gawarecki*

Main category: cond-mat.mtrl-sci

TL;DR: This paper theoretically studies electron and hole g-factors in inorganic lead-halide perovskites using an 18-band k·p model, examines strain dependence, and shows bulk g-factors are highly tunable (even through zero) while nanocrystal g-factors are less strain-sensitive due to quantum confinement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding and controlling carrier g-factors in lead-halide perovskites is important for spintronic and optoelectronic applications, motivating a detailed theoretical investigation of how strain and confinement affect these quantities.

Method: The authors employ an 18-band k·p model with parameters fitted to DFT band structures for the reference cubic phase. They use linear response theory to compute g-factors and their strain dependence, and perform real-space calculations for perovskite nanocrystals.

Result: Bulk electron and hole g-factors can be tuned over a wide range by external strain, in some cases passing through zero. In contrast, carrier g-factors in CsPbX3 nanocrystals are considerably less sensitive to strain due to quantum confinement.

Conclusion: Strain provides an effective means to engineer bulk g-factors in inorganic lead-halide perovskites, but quantum confinement in nanocrystals substantially suppresses this strain tunability.

Abstract: In this paper, we theoretically study the electron and hole $g$-factors in inorganic lead-halide perovskites for the reference cubic phase. We employ an 18-band $k \cdot p$ model and determine its parameters using target band structures obtained from DFT calculations. We use the linear response theory to calculate the $g$-factors and investigate their dependence on external strain. We then perform real-space calculations of the electron and hole $g$-factors in perovskite nanocrystals. We show that the bulk $g$-factors can be tuned over a wide range by external strain. In some cases, strain can be used to tune the $g$-factor through zero. By contrast, the carrier $g$-factors in CsPb$X_3$ nanocrystals are considerably less sensitive to strain due to quantum confinement.

</details>

### [44] [Ground-state properties of the two-dimensional SWAP spin-glass ensemble](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05046)
*Alexander K. Hartmann,Leticia F. Cugliandolo,Marco Tarzia*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: The paper studies a SWAP ensemble for Ising spin-glasses with variable spin lengths scaled by Δ∈[0,2], proving that slow annealing can find true ground states for 2D systems up to 1024² spins when Δ is medium or large, though at the cost of the realizations becoming ferromagnetic.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The SWAP algorithm has proven effective for glassy poly-disperse hard-sphere systems, motivating its adaptation to Ising spin-glasses through a variable-length spin ensemble. The study aims to determine whether annealing in this SWAP ensemble can reliably reach ground states of the associated Ising Hamiltonian and to assess how the length-scale parameter Δ affects the glassy nature of the resulting disorder realizations.

Method: The authors employ an annealing schedule on the SWAP ensemble, where spin lengths vary with scale Δ∈[0,2] and can be exchanged as in the SWAP algorithm; the spin lengths are incorporated into the bonds. They test the protocol on two-dimensional systems up to L²=1024² spins and validate against exact ground states computed via graph matching algorithms. They characterize disorder realizations by computing exact zero-temperature domain-wall energies using periodic and anti-periodic boundary conditions.

Result: For slow enough annealing, ground states of the corresponding Ising Hamiltonian are obtained with high probability, verified for 2D systems up to 1024² spins. However, for medium or large Δ (e.g., Δ=1), the realizations become ferromagnetic when annealing is slow enough, losing their zero-temperature spin-glass property. For small Δ, the realizations remain glassy, but SWAP annealing struggles to find true ground states.

Conclusion: SWAP annealing can reliably find ground states in the variable-length Ising spin-glass ensemble, but only under a trade-off: sufficiently large Δ that enables success also drives the disorder realizations ferromagnetic, erasing the spin-glass property; small Δ preserves glassiness but defeats the annealing's ability to locate true ground states.

Abstract: We study the recently introduced SWAP ensemble for Ising spin-glasses, where the spins obtain varying lengths, with length scale $Δ\in [0, 2]$. The lengths can be exchanged in the spirit of the SWAP algorithm for glassy poly-disperse hard-sphere systems. Using an annealing schedule, if the annealing is slow enough, ground states of the corresponding Ising Hamiltonian, where the spin lengths are incorporated into the bonds, can be obtained with high probability. We prove this statement for two-dimensional systems with sizes of up to $L^2=1024^2$ spins by comparison with exact ground states obtained by using graph matching algorithms. In particular, we analyze the nature of the obtained realizations of the disorder by calculating exact zero-temperature domain-wall energies, from exact ground state calculations using periodic and anti-periodic boundary conditions. For medium or large values of $Δ$, e.g. $Δ=1$, the realizations turn ferromagnetic if the annealing is slow enough, i.e., they lose their zero-temperature spin-glass property. If $Δ$ is small, the realizations remain glassy, but the SWAP annealing does not easily find a true ground state.

</details>


### [45] [Disorder-driven loss of crystalline coherence in two-dimensional electron solids: Quantum Hartree-Fock and classical molecular dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06371)
*Sankar Das Sarma,Haining Pan*

Main category: cond-mat.dis-nn

TL;DR: Quenched disorder weakens crystalline correlations and shrinks the coherence length in 2D electron solids, consistent with Imry-Ma/Larkin physics, while simultaneously stabilizing the resulting amorphous solid against thermal rearrangement.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how quenched (frozen-in) disorder affects the structural order of two-dimensional electron solids, addressing the fundamental question of how random pinning destroys long-range translational (crystalline) order.

Method: The authors combine Hartree-Fock theory with classical molecular dynamics simulations, tuning the disorder strength and measuring crystalline correlations, structure factor broadening, and coherence length.

Result: Increasing disorder weakens crystalline correlations, broadens the structure factor, and reduces the coherence length down toward the pristine lattice spacing. Classical simulations show that stronger pinning raises the temperature required for particle rearrangement even after crystalline order has vanished.

Conclusion: The observed behavior is consistent with the Imry-Ma and Larkin picture, in which random pinning disrupts translational order beyond a finite length scale. Disorder thus suppresses structural coherence while conversely stabilizing an amorphous solid against thermal rearrangement.

Abstract: We investigate the effects of quenched disorder on two-dimensional electron solids using Hartree-Fock theory and classical molecular dynamics. Increasing disorder weakens crystalline correlations, broadens the structure factor, and reduces the measured coherence length toward the pristine lattice spacing. This evolution is consistent with the Imry-Ma and Larkin picture: random pinning disrupts translational order on sufficiently long scales, and stronger disorder reduces the distance over which crystalline organization survives. Classical simulations further show that stronger pinning raises the temperature for particle rearrangement even after crystalline order has disappeared. Disorder thus suppresses structural coherence while stabilizing an amorphous solid against thermal rearrangement.

</details>

### [46] [A Novel Iterative-Fitting Approach for the Thermal Characterization of Thin Fibers](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03933)
*William Spirnock,Alison Hake,Dihui Wang,Timothy Sullivan,Dana He,Heng Ban*

Main category: physics.app-ph

TL;DR: A two-measurement transient electrothermal technique (TET) determines thermal conductivity, diffusivity, volumetric heat capacity, and effective surface emissivity of thin fibers/films, correcting radiative heat-loss errors via an analytical model with sensitivity-guided multiparameter fitting.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional TET accuracy declines when radiative heat losses are significant and surface emissivity is unknown—conditions common at the microscale. Existing analyses either neglect these effects or rely on separate measurements that introduce sequential (propagated) errors.

Method: An analytical model incorporating thermal radiation and variable heat generation enables sensitivity-guided, multiparameter fitting. Two forms of the temperature solution are used: one yields thermal conductivity and diffusivity, the other thermal conductivity and volumetric heat capacity. An iterative fitting procedure recovers intrinsic properties corrected for environmental effects, reducing sample count and measurement time.

Result: Measurements of amorphous SiO2 optical fiber and Hi-Nicalon Type S SiC fiber agreed with literature values to within ~10% average error across all samples. Experimental and numerical uncertainty analyses quantify measurement variability and relate it to sample geometry.

Conclusion: The method simultaneously resolves multiple intrinsic thermal properties, corrects radiative-loss errors, and offers quantitative guidance for sample geometry selection.

Abstract: The transient electrothermal technique (TET) is widely used to measure the thermal properties of thin fibers and films, but its accuracy declines when radiative heat losses are significant and surface emissivity is unknown. These conditions are difficult to avoid at the microscale, while conventional analyses either neglect them or rely on separate measurements that introduce sequential errors. We present a TET implementation that determines multiple thermal properties and effective surface emissivity from only two transient measurements. An analytical model incorporating thermal radiation and variable heat generation supports sensitivity-guided, multiparameter fitting. Two forms of the temperature solution yield thermal conductivity and diffusivity, and thermal conductivity and volumetric heat capacity, respectively. An iterative fitting procedure then recovers intrinsic properties corrected for environmental effects while reducing the required sample count and measurement time. Measurements of amorphous silicon dioxide (SiO2) optical fiber and Hi-Nicalon Type S silicon carbide (SiC) fiber agreed with literature values to within an average error of approximately 10% across all samples. Experimental and numerical uncertainty analyses quantify measurement variability and relate it to sample geometry. This method simultaneously resolves multiple intrinsic thermal properties, corrects radiative-loss errors, and provides quantitative guidance for sample geometry selection.

</details>

### [47] [Nonlinearity-induced Topological Phase Transitions in One-dimensional Systems with Chiral Symmetry: A Real-Space Treatment](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04984)
*Liu Bi Tao,Shilpi Roy,Jiangbin Gong*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper studies whether nonlinearity-induced topological phase transitions persist under quasiperiodicity, using a nonlinear Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain in real space. It finds the transition behavior depends strongly on quasiperiodic potential strength, and warns that boundary localization of nonlinear eigenstates is not a reliable signature of topological edge states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonlinearity is known to induce topological phase transitions in periodic systems, but it is unclear whether such transitions survive in the absence of translational symmetry. Quasiperiodicity offers a natural testbed to address this question directly in real space.

Method: Using the periodic SSH chain as a reference, they introduce a quasiperiodic potential and nonlinearity, and analyze the nonlinear eigenstates in real space to track how nonlinearity drives topological phase transitions as a function of quasiperiodic potential strength.

Result: The nature of the nonlinearity-driven transition depends strongly on quasiperiodic potential strength: weak quasiperiodicity supports a trivial-to-topological transition as nonlinearity increases; the intermediate regime exhibits no topological phase transition; and under strong quasiperiodicity, pronounced boundary localization appears well before any nonlinearity-induced topological transition.

Conclusion: Nonlinearity and quasiperiodic potential interact nontrivially in inducing topological phase transitions, and boundary localization of nonlinear eigenstates alone should not be taken as a clear signature of topological edge states.

Abstract: Nonlinearity can induce topological phase transitions in periodic systems. It is hence of considerable interest to examine if such transitions persist in the absence of translational symmetry. We address this question directly in real space, using the periodic Su-Schrieffer-Heeger (SSH) chain as a reference for understanding the effect of quasiperiodicity. How nonlinearity drives a chain towards a topological phase transition is found to depend strongly on the strength of the quasiperiodic potential.
