As the nonlinearity strength increases, we find that weak quasiperiodicity supports a transition from a trivial to a topological phase, whereas the intermediate regime does not have a topological phase transition. Under strong quasiperiodicity, pronounced boundary localization can develop well before a nonlinearity-induced topological transition. Our results show how nonlinearity may interplay with a quasiperiodic potential in inducing topological phase transitions and that boundary localization of nonlinear eigenstates alone may not be taken as a clear signature of topological edge states.

</details>


### [48] [Magnon chiral damping beyond the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03766)
*Darpa Narayan Basu,Subhadip Ghosh,Peter M. Oppeneer,Alexander Mook,Ritwik Mondal*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper offers an alternative explanation for magnon chiral damping via linear spin-wave theory and antisymmetric Gilbert damping in the LLG equation, showing that antisymmetric Gilbert damping produces nonreciprocal magnon damping linear in k, independent of Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Magnon chiral damping (damping asymmetric with respect to the magnon wave vector) has conventionally been attributed solely to antisymmetric Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya spin interactions. The authors seek an alternative mechanism, questioning whether this nonreciprocity necessarily requires DMI or can instead originate from antisymmetric Gilbert damping.

Method: The authors use linear spin-wave theory combined with an antisymmetric Gilbert damping tensor introduced within the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation of motion. They first analyze a minimal single-sublattice ferromagnet on a rectangular lattice, examining scalar nonlocal and antisymmetric Gilbert damping, then extend the analysis to a two-sublattice system with cross-sublattice Gilbert damping, including cases with and without Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions.

Result: The effective magnon damping is found to be independent of system energy. Scalar nonlocal Gilbert damping makes the effective damping parameter α_eff wave-vector dependent, acquiring a quadratic k-dependence in the long-wavelength limit. The antisymmetric component of the Gilbert damping tensor yields asymmetric (nonreciprocal) magnon damping that is linear in k in the long-wavelength limit, consistent with recent experiments. In the two-sublattice system, cross-sublattice antisymmetric Gilbert damping generates nonreciprocal magnon damping even with DMI parametrically set to zero, and this nonreciprocity is roughly an order of magnitude larger than that from DMI.

Conclusion: Magnon chiral damping can arise from antisymmetric Gilbert damping rather than Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions alone. Antisymmetric Gilbert damping provides an alternative, and even dominant (by roughly an order of magnitude), mechanism for asymmetric spin-wave relaxation.

Abstract: Magnon chiral damping denotes damping that is asymmetric with respect to the magnon wave vector. Such nonreciprocity has been attributed to antisymmetric Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya spin interactions. Here, we provide an alternative theoretical explanation of magnon chiral damping using linear spin-wave theory and antisymmetric Gilbert damping introduced within the Landau-Lifshitz-Gilbert equation of motion. First, employing a minimal model of a single sublattice ferromagnet on a rectangular lattice, we show that the effective magnon damping is independent of the system's energy, including contributions from Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions. Considering scalar nonlocal Gilbert damping, the effective damping parameter $α_{\rm eff}$ becomes wave-vector dependent. In the long-wavelength limit, $α_{\rm eff}$ acquires a quadratic dependence on $\bf{k}$. Furthermore, the antisymmetric component of the Gilbert damping tensor gives rise to asymmetric (nonreciprocal) magnon damping. We find the asymmetry to be linear in $\bf{k}$ in the long-wavelength limit, consistent with recent experiments. Extending the analysis to a two-sublattice system, we consider cross-sublattice Gilbert damping. In the presence of only scalar Gilbert damping, broken reciprocity in magnon relaxation arises from Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions. In contrast, the cross-sublattice antisymmetric component of the Gilbert damping tensor can generate nonreciprocal magnon damping even if the Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction is parametrically set to zero, providing an alternative mechanism for asymmetric spin-wave relaxation. Notably, the nonreciprocity induced by antisymmetric Gilbert damping is roughly an order of magnitude larger than that arising from Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interactions.

</details>


### [49] [From Dirac surface response to Casimir forces in Bumblebee-CFJ electrodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03768)
*F. C. E. Lima*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The Casimir interaction between planar massive Dirac materials in a Lorentz-violating Bumblebee-CFJ vector background is studied via the Lifshitz formalism using the one-loop polarization tensor as surface response. Repulsive regimes arise from CFJ-induced TE-TM mode mixing rather than Hall conductivity, while the Dirac mass suppresses the interaction and alters its large-distance scaling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper investigates whether repulsive Casimir regimes in Dirac materials can originate from Lorentz-violating backgrounds rather than conventional parity-odd (Hall-like) conductivity, and how a Dirac mass and the CFJ parameter jointly control the sign and scaling of the interaction.

Method: The renormalized one-loop polarization tensor of Dirac quasiparticles is employed as a nonlocal surface response within the Lifshitz scattering formalism, treating the Bumblebee-CFJ vector sector in a normally oriented Lorentz-violating background.

Result: For the normally oriented Lorentz-violating background the surface response remains parity-even, so repulsion is not driven by Hall-like conductivity; instead it stems from CFJ-induced TE-TM bulk mode mixing combined with the spatially dispersive response of the Dirac sheets. The Dirac mass suppresses the interaction and modifies its large-distance scaling, while the CFJ parameter drives transitions between attractive and repulsive regimes.

Conclusion: Lorentz violation via the Bumblebee-CFJ sector provides a distinct, parity-even mechanism for tuning the Casimir force between massive Dirac materials, with the CFJ parameter and Dirac mass serving as independent knobs for controlling sign and distance scaling of the interaction.

Abstract: We examine the Casimir interaction between planar massive Dirac materials coupled to a Lorentz-violating Bumblebee-CFJ vector sector. The standard renormalized one-loop polarization tensor of the Dirac quasiparticles is used as the nonlocal surface response in the Lifshitz scattering formalism. For the normally oriented Lorentz-violating background, the surface response remains parity-even. Hence, the repulsive regimes do not originate from a Hall-like conductivity. Instead, they arise from the CFJ-induced mixing of TE and TM bulk modes, combined with the spatially dispersive response of the Dirac sheets. Furthermore, we show that the Dirac mass suppresses the interaction and changes its large-distance scaling, whereas the CFJ parameter drives transitions between attractive and repulsive regimes.

</details>


### [50] [Surface scalar plasmons on curved interfaces: a venue for analogue gravity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03813)
*Matheus E. Pereira*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper shows that curvature of a conductor-dielectric interface modifies scalar surface plasmon (SSP) propagation via a geometric potential, potentially yielding vanishing effective mass, and computes quasinormal modes in an analogue black hole geometry.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The authors ask whether the geometry of a curved conductor-dielectric interface can modify the effective mass and propagation of scalar surface plasmons, seeking a geometric handle on plasmon transport and localization.

Method: In the hydrodynamic limit, SSPs are treated as density perturbations of a semiclassical jellium electron gas. A thin-layer dimensional reduction procedure is used to derive a massive Klein-Gordon equation for SSPs on the curved interface, including a geometric potential. The formalism is then applied to a known analogue black hole configuration to determine quasinormal mode frequencies.

Result: The derived Klein-Gordon description contains a geometric potential, and under specific conditions this potential permits SSP propagation with vanishing effective mass. For the analogue black hole configuration, the corresponding quasinormal mode frequencies are obtained.

Conclusion: Interface curvature acts as a geometric control parameter for plasmon propagation and localization, suggesting new possibilities for plasmon transport and analogue gravity experiments using surface plasmons.

Abstract: We investigate whether the geometry of a curved conductor--dielectric interface can modify the effective mass and propagation of scalar surface plasmons (SSPs). In the hydrodynamic limit, we describe SSPs as density perturbations in a semiclassical jellium electron gas. Using a thin--layer dimensional reduction procedure, we derive a massive Klein--Gordon equation for SSPs on the curved interface with a geometric potential. We find that under specific conditions, the geometric potential allows SSP propagation with a vanishing effective mass. We then apply the formalism to a well known analogue black hole configuration, determining the corresponding quasinormal mode frequencies. Our results show that interface curvature provides a geometric handle on plasmon propagation and localization, suggesting new possibilities for plasmon transport and analogue gravity experiments with surface plasmons.

</details>


### [51] [Giant Conductance Enhancement in Graphene Nanoscrolls via Interlayer-Coupled States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05881)
*Jia-Cheng Li,Ying-Je Lee,Yu An Cheng,Yu-Hsiang Fu,Xuan-Fu Huang,Yu-Jie Zhong,Ion Cosma Fulga,Carmine Ortix,Ching-Hao Chang*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Rolling a graphene nanoribbon into a few-turn nanoscroll (1.0-1.2 turns) boosts longitudinal conductance by nearly twentyfold, an effect driven by interlayer hopping between open boundaries that converts localized edge states into 1D-like conducting channels and shifts the Fermi energy.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Graphene nanoscrolls possess a unique radial superlattice geometry that enables transport behaviors distinct from flat graphene, motivating investigation of how scroll geometry governs electronic transport.

Method: Theoretical calculations demonstrating that rolling a graphene nanoribbon into a few-turn nanoscroll (1.0-1.2 turns) triggers conductance enhancement via interlayer hopping between open boundaries.

Result: A giant order-of-magnitude (nearly twentyfold) enhancement of longitudinal conductance is found; localized edge states transform into one-dimensional-like conducting channels and a significant Fermi energy shift occurs. Conductance scales inversely with turn number.

Conclusion: Graphene nanoscrolls constitute a platform for geometry-driven quantum transport and offer a tunable route to engineering nanoarchitectural electronic states.

Abstract: Graphene nanoscrolls form a unique radial superlattice whose geometry enables transport behaviors distinct from their flat counterparts. We theoretically demonstrate that rolling a graphene nanoribbon into a few-turn nanoscroll (1.0-1.2 turns) triggers a giant enhancement in longitudinal conductance by an order of magnitude (nearly twentyfold). We show that this effect stems from interlayer hopping between open boundaries, which not only transforms localized edge states into one-dimensional-like conducting channels and also induces a significant Fermi energy shift. Our results reveal an inverse scaling of conductance with turn number, establishing GNSs as a platform for geometry-driven quantum transport and offering a tunable approach to engineering nanoarchitectural electronic states.

</details>


### [52] [Integrating spin glass dynamics into nanomechanical resonators](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03891)
*S. Y. Grebenchuk,T. M. Savilov,Y. W. Ho,A. K. Grebenko,M. Kravtsov,J. M. Wolf,D. A. Litvinov,Z. Yang,P. Cai,S. Ramachandran,R. Duan,L. Zheng,B. Özyilmaz,G. Eda,E. J. G. Santos,M. Koperski,K. S. Novoselov,M. Šiškins*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper integrates spin-glass magnetism into nanoelectromechanical systems (NEMS) using thin exfoliated NbFeTe₂ films, transducing slow glassy magnetic domain relaxation into mechanical resonance frequency shifts.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spin glasses exhibit history-dependent, slow-relaxation dynamics that are hard to harness in functional devices due to their non-equilibrium nature. Two-dimensional van der Waals spin-glass magnets could overcome this by enabling controlled magneto-mechanical coupling.

Method: Thin exfoliated NbFeTe₂ films were integrated into membrane resonators. Cryogenic magnetic force microscopy characterized the magnetic behavior, and the films' glassy domain relaxation was coupled to mechanical strain to shift resonance frequency.

Result: A robust spin-glass state persists down to few-tens-of-nanometer-thick layers, with hour-scale macroscopic magnetic relaxation tied to glassy reorganization of magnetic domain clusters. This relaxation is transduced into strain, measurably shifting the mechanical resonance frequency.

Conclusion: The work provides a functional nanomechanical readout of spin-glass dynamics and establishes a versatile platform for integrating frustrated magnetism into NEMS.

Abstract: Spin glasses are non-equilibrium magnetic systems with pronounced slow relaxation, ageing, and chaotic, synaptic-like properties, whose unique history-dependent dynamics have remained difficult to harness in functional devices. Two-dimensional van der Waals spin-glass magnets offer a route to overcome this limitation by allowing controlled coupling between magnetic and mechanical degrees of freedom. Here, we demonstrate the integration of a spin-glass functionality in nanoelectromechanical systems (NEMS) using thin exfoliated NbFeTe$_2$ films, which establishes a robust spin-glass state down to a few tens of nanometer-thick layers. Using cryogenic magnetic force microscopy, we reveal characteristic slow, hour-scale macroscopic magnetic relaxation, associated with glassy reorganisation of its magnetic domain clusters. Incorporation of thin NbFeTe$_2$ into membrane resonators allows the transduction of this glassy magnetic domain relaxation into strain, shifting their mechanical resonance frequency. This provides a functional nanomechanical readout of spin-glass dynamics and establishes a versatile platform for integrating frustrated magnetism into NEMS.

</details>


### [53] [Escaping the Composite Fermi Sea: An Incompressible State at Half Filling in Graphene's Lowest Landau Level](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03921)
*Domagoj Perkovic,Ziwei Wang,Steven Herbert Simon*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work provides the first quantitative energetic study of incompressible even-denominator FQH states at zeroth Landau-level crossings in monolayer/trilayer graphene, identifying Landau-level mixing (screening) plus ultra-short-range interactions as the mechanism stabilizing a strongly paired composite-fermion phase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Experiments observe incompressible even-denominator fractional quantum Hall states at narrow parameter regions near zeroth Landau-level crossings in monolayer and trilayer graphene, which is surprising since at half filling the Coulomb interaction is expected to favor a compressible composite Fermi liquid. A quantitative understanding of the candidate incompressible phases and the mechanisms stabilizing them is lacking.

Method: Exact diagonalization and infinite density-matrix renormalization group (iDMRG) are used first to study ultra-short-range corrections to the Coulomb interaction from finite layer separation, lattice-scale effects, and electron-phonon coupling. Landau-level mixing is then incorporated via screening within the random-phase approximation (RPA).

Result: A sufficiently strong attractive short-range interaction drives the composite Fermi liquid first into a weakly paired d-wave pseudospin-singlet state, then into a strongly paired state. However, microscopic estimates of these short-range corrections are too small to stabilize either phase on their own. Screening substantially reshapes the effective interaction, suppresses the weak-pairing regime, and strongly enhances the tendency toward strong pairing. For a realistic range of microscopic parameters, the strongly paired phase is stabilized over a substantial fraction of that range.

Conclusion: The interplay of Landau-level mixing and ultra-short-range interactions is a plausible microscopic mechanism for the experimentally observed incompressible states in monolayer and trilayer graphene, pointing toward a strongly paired composite-fermion phase as their candidate ground state.

Abstract: Recent experiments in monolayer and trilayer graphene have observed incompressible even-denominator fractional quantum Hall states within narrow regions of parameter space associated with crossings of zeroth Landau levels. This is surprising because at half filling the Coulomb interaction in the zeroth Landau level is expected to favour a compressible composite Fermi liquid. Here we provide the first quantitative energetic study of the candidate incompressible phases and of the microscopic mechanisms that can stabilise them near such Landau-level crossings. Using exact diagonalization and infinite density-matrix renormalisation group, we first study ultra-short-range corrections to the Coulomb interaction arising from finite layer separation, lattice-scale effects, and electron-phonon coupling. We find that a sufficiently strong attractive short-range interaction drives the composite Fermi liquid first into a weakly paired \(d\)-wave pseudospin-singlet state and subsequently into a strongly paired state. However, microscopic estimates of these short-range corrections are far too small to stabilise either incompressible phase. We then incorporate Landau-level mixing through screening within the random-phase approximation. Screening substantially reshapes the effective interaction, suppresses the weak-pairing regime, and strongly enhances the tendency toward strong pairing. For a realistic range of microscopic parameters, the strongly paired phase is stabilised over a substantial fraction of that range. Our results identify the interplay of Landau-level mixing and ultra-short-range interactions as a plausible microscopic mechanism for the experimentally observed incompressible states in monolayer and trilayer graphene and point toward a strongly paired composite-fermion phase as their candidate ground state.

</details>


### [54] [Exchange-controlled quantum beats and entanglement in an exciton--bimodal-cavity system](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04111)
*Cesar E. P. Villegas,Victor P. Brasil,Pedro A. S. Contri,Kevin Lizárraga,Jonas Maziero*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A two-exciton system in a bimodal cavity with exciton-exciton exchange is analyzed via dressed-state transformations and Löwdin partitioning, yielding effective Hamiltonians with three time scales. Applied to graphene nanoribbon excitons parameterized from first principles, the model shows long-lived entanglement in small/moderate-exchange regimes and near-complete population transfer in the large-exchange regime, providing analytical design rules.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding and controlling coherent dynamics and entanglement in exciton-cavity platforms is important for quantum technologies. A system with two excitons coupled to a bimodal cavity and an exciton-exciton exchange interaction introduces competing channels, and a clear analytical framework linking the exchange interaction, cavity detuning, and light-matter coupling to experimentally relevant observables is needed.

Method: The authors study a two-exciton system coupled to a bimodal optical cavity including exciton-exciton exchange. They apply dressed-state transformations and Löwdin partitioning to derive effective Hamiltonians, identifying three dynamical time scales: local polaritonic oscillations, long-period dressed-state beats, and direct excitonic exchange. The framework is applied to graphene nanoribbon excitons, using first-principles excitonic energies, transition dipoles, and lifetimes to parameterize the model.

Result: In the small- and moderate-exchange regimes, the dynamics are multiscale, and the beat channel sustains sizable quantum entanglement over long dissipative time windows. In the large-exchange regime, the excitonic Hamiltonian dominates, driving nearly complete population transfer between excitonic states over many coherent cycles. Analytical expressions relate the beat period, transfer amplitude, and coherent-cycle number to the exchange interaction, cavity detuning, and light-matter coupling.

Conclusion: The work provides a controllable route for engineering long-lived entanglement and coherent excitonic operations in exciton-bimodal-cavity systems, with analytical design rules connecting key dynamical quantities to system parameters.

Abstract: We theoretically demonstrate tunable population transfer and entanglement generation in a two-exciton system coupled to a bimodal optical cavity, including an exciton-exciton exchange interaction. By using dressed-state transformations and Löwdin partitioning, we derive effective Hamiltonians that identify three dynamical time scales: local polaritonic oscillations, long-period dressed-state beats, and direct excitonic exchange. We apply the framework to graphene nanoribbon excitons, using excitonic energies, transition dipoles, and lifetimes obtained from first-principles calculations to parameterize the model. In the small- and moderate-exchange regimes, the dynamics become multiscale, and the beat channel sustains sizable quantum entanglement over long dissipative time windows. In the large-exchange regime, the excitonic Hamiltonian dominates and drives nearly complete population transfer between excitonic states over many coherent cycles. The resulting analytical expressions provide direct design rules connecting the beat period, transfer amplitude, and coherent-cycle number to the exchange interaction, cavity detuning, and light--matter coupling. Our results establish a controllable route for engineering long-lived entanglement and coherent excitonic operations in exciton--bimodal-cavity systems.

</details>


### [55] [Micromagnet engineering for enhanced addressability of donor-cluster arrays in silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04584)
*Hao Wang,Baolong Zhao,Shihang Zhang,Ziliang Jin,Huan Shu,Peihao Huang,Guanyong Wang,Mingchao Duan,Tianluo Pan,Tao Xin,Guangchong Hu,Zhen Tian,Dapeng Yu,Yu He*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Integrating STM-defined phosphorus donor-cluster arrays with on-chip micromagnets yields a scalable silicon quantum processor architecture supporting over 120 addressable atomic spin qubits with reduced fan-out overhead.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Phosphorus donors in silicon are promising for fault-tolerant quantum computing due to long coherence times and CMOS compatibility, but scaling is bottlenecked by the independent addressability of individual spin qubits.

Method: The authors propose an architecture combining STM-defined phosphorus donor-cluster arrays with on-chip micromagnets, using micromagnetic simulations to optimize micromagnet geometry and create magnetic field gradients for frequency multiplexing. Magnetic measurements are used to validate the design.

Result: Micromagnets generate magnetic field gradients that provide a broader addressable frequency range than hyperfine-interaction-only addressing, are compatible with customized array designs, enable integration of over 120 atomic spin qubits, and allow frequency multiplexing with a single microwave antenna, reducing fan-out addressing line overhead; magnetic measurements generally align with simulations.

Conclusion: The proposed design establishes a critical foundation for scalable silicon quantum computing with atomic qubits.

Abstract: Phosphorus donors in silicon are one of the promising platforms for fault-tolerant quantum computing, owing to their long coherence times and compatibility with complementary metal-oxide-semiconductor (CMOS) technology. However, a key bottleneck for scaling this platform lies in the independent addressability of individual spin qubits. Here, we present a scalable quantum processor architecture that integrates scanning tunneling microscope (STM)-defined phosphorus donor-cluster arrays with on-chip micromagnets. By generating magnetic field gradients, the micromagnets enable a broader addressable frequency range compared to the addressing scheme based solely on variations in hyperfine (HF) interactions, which arise from the randomness of intra-cluster configurations. Through micromagnetic simulations to optimize micromagnet geometry, we demonstrate that these structures are compatible with customized array designs, facilitating the integration of over 120 atomic spin qubits and enabling frequency multiplexing with a single microwave antenna, which greatly reduces the overhead of fan-out addressing lines. This design is further validated via magnetic measurements, which generally align with our micromagnetic simulations. These results establish a critical foundation for the development of scalable silicon quantum computing with atomic qubits.

</details>


### [56] [Spatiotemporal imaging of microwave magnetic fields via magnonic coherent splitting](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04618)
*C. K. Wei,Z. J. Chen,J. T. Song,S. H. Ma,W. H. Liu,J. H. Wu,Z. W. Huang,Jinwei Rao,Wei Lu,Bimu Yao*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper introduces an optics-free, room-temperature microwave magnetic-field imaging method based on magnonic coherent splitting in yttrium iron garnet (YIG), achieving 58 pT/√Hz sensitivity and 130-ns temporal response for spatiotemporal field mapping in high-frequency circuits.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Spatiotemporal microwave magnetic-field imaging is essential for understanding current flow in high-frequency circuits and nonequilibrium spin dynamics, but existing probes rarely combine calibrated spectral readout, optics-free operation, and transient mapping at room temperature simultaneously.

Method: The approach exploits ferrimagnetic order in yttrium iron garnet (YIG), where a pump-induced magnon mode coherently couples and converts the target magnetic-field amplitude into a spectral splitting read out entirely in the microwave domain. By sampling this calibrated splitting as a function of position and delay, spatiotemporal field maps are reconstructed. Continuous-wave measurements provide calibrated, phase-resolved readout, while time-resolved frequency-comb spectroscopy extends the scheme to transient fields.

Result: The continuous-wave mode achieves a sensitivity of 58 pT/√Hz and recovers phase information across various powers. Combined with time-resolved frequency-comb spectroscopy, the method reconstructs transient fields with a 130-ns response time. Coplanar-waveguide imaging confirms magnetic selectivity of the mode-splitting readout, with measured maps matching simulated magnetic-field distributions. Applied to a microwave amplifier, the reconstruction resolves nonuniform switching dynamics and detects downstream field suppression caused by an open-contact fault.

Conclusion: Magnonic coherent splitting offers a scalable, optics-free route for imaging spatiotemporal field evolution in functional devices, combining calibrated spectral readout with room-temperature, transient-capable operation.

Abstract: Spatiotemporal microwave magnetic-field imaging reveals current flow in high-frequency circuits and nonequilibrium spin dynamics, yet probes rarely combine calibrated spectral readout, optics-free operation and transient mapping at room temperature. Here ferrimagnetic order in yttrium iron garnet supports coherent coupling from a pump-induced magnon mode, converting target-field amplitude into a spectral splitting with all-microwave readout. Sampling the calibrated splitting over position and delay reconstructs spatiotemporal imaging of magnetic fields. Continuous-wave measurement reaches a sensitivity of 58 pT/$\sqrt{\mathrm{Hz}}$ and recovers phases across various powers. Combined with time-resolved frequency-comb spectroscopy, the method reconstructs transient fields with a 130-ns response time. Coplanar-waveguide imaging validates the magnetic selectivity of the mode-splitting readout, where measured maps agree with simulated magnetic-field distribution. In a microwave amplifier, our reconstruction resolves nonuniform switching dynamics and detects downstream field suppression from an open-contact fault. Magnonic coherent splitting provides a scalable route for optics-free imaging of spatiotemporal field evolution in functional devices.

</details>


### [57] [Electron-phonon control of obstructed and quantum spin Hall topology in an altermagnet](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04684)
*Kuntal Bhattacharyya,Bilal Tanatar,Saurabh Basu*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work theoretically shows that electron-phonon coupling can switch a d-wave altermagnet embedded in a QSH insulator between an obstructed atomic insulator and first-order topological phases, enabling phonon-tuned spintronic functionality.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Altermagnets host nonrelativistic spin-split bands with zero net magnetization, offering a platform to study interplay between magnetism and topology. A controllable mechanism to switch between topologically distinct insulating phases in such systems is desirable for phonon-tuned spintronics.

Method: Embed a d_{x^2-y^2}-wave altermagnet in a BHZ-type QSH insulator and analyze the resulting phases. Introduce electron-phonon coupling (EPC) to examine how polaronic dressing reorganizes Wannier centers and effective edge topology.

Result: Altermagnetic order beyond a critical strength reconstructs the parent QSH state into an obstructed atomic insulator with nontrivial bulk polarization, quantized fractional corner charges, in-gap boundary states, and vanishing spin Chern number. Sufficiently strong EPC weakens the altermagnetism-induced OAI phase and restores helical QSH edge modes, eventually driving the system into a trivial insulator.

Conclusion: A controllable switching mechanism between topologically distinct insulating phases in altermagnets is uncovered, establishing a route toward phonon-tuned spintronic functionalities in unconventional magnetic platforms.

Abstract: We theoretically demonstrate control over the interplay between obstructed atomic insulating and first-order topological phases via electron-phonon coupling (EPC) in a $d$-wave altermagnet (AM) that hosts nonrelativistic spin-split bands with zero net magnetization. By embedding a $d_{x^{2}-y^{2}}$-wave AM in a quantum spin Hall (QSH) insulator, we show that an altermagnetic order beyond a critical strength reconstructs the parent QSH state of a Bernevig-Hughes-Zhang (BHZ)-type topological insulator (TI) into an obstructed atomic insulator (OAI), characterized by nontrivial bulk polarization with quantized fractional corner charges, accompanied by characteristic in-gap boundary states, while the spin Chern number vanishes. Upon incorporating sufficiently strong EPC, the polaronic dressing reorganizes the Wannier centers and the associated effective edge topology, thereby weakening the altermagnetism-induced OAI phase and restoring the helical QSH edge modes. Further, EPC eventually drives the system into a trivial insulator. By uncovering a controllable switching mechanism between topologically distinct insulating phases in AMs, our study establishes a direct route toward phonon-tuned spintronic functionalities in unconventional magnetic platforms.

</details>


### [58] [Anisotropic Friedel oscillations and isoperimetric transport in two-dimensional free lattice Fermi seas](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04724)
*Guillermo R. Zemba*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper explores two physical consequences of the topological classification of 2D Fermi seas based on flat orbifolds R^2/Γ: (1) a directional anisotropy and Fraunhofer-like diffraction pattern in the single-particle density matrix within the mesoscopic window, and (2) a geometric correspondence between momentum-space packing and electrical transport, yielding σ_xx ∝ 1/√I.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Recent topological classification of 2D Fermi seas for free particles relies on flat orbifolds R^2/Γ of discrete symmetry Γ. The authors aim to uncover the physical consequences of this classification, specifically regarding real-space density behavior and transport properties.

Method: The study examines the spatial density inside the mesoscopic window bounded by Fermi and thermal lengths, analyzing the single-particle density matrix for directional anisotropy. It treats p4m and p6m crystallographic flat orbifolds as examples. For transport, it uses geometric measure theory to derive a mean-field expression for longitudinal conductivity σ_xx as a function of the isoperimetric parameter I of the occupied orbifold domain.

Result: The single-particle density matrix exhibits sharp directional anisotropy and a real-space diffraction (Fraunhofer-like) pattern, revealing point-group symmetries of the background lattice Γ via anisotropic Friedel oscillations. The conductivity satisfies σ_xx ∝ 1/√I, and the formula reproduces vanishing conductivity (σ_xx → 0) for geometric insulators.

Conclusion: The isoperimetric parameter I is promoted as an efficient descriptor for topological and geometric phases in conductors, linking momentum-space packing to electrical transport.

Abstract: Two physical consequences of the recent topological classification of 2d Fermi seas for free particles based on flat orbifolds $\mathbb{R}^2/Γ$ of discrete symmetry $Γ$, are explored. The first is an study of the spatial density behavior inside the mesoscopic window enclosed by the Fermi and (infinite) thermal lengths. It is shown that the single-particle density matrix exhibits a sharp directional anisotropy, encoding a real space diffraction pattern, {\it i.e.}, a Fraunhofer-like diagram of the Fermi sea, that reveals the point-group symmetries of the background lattice $Γ$ in the form of anisotropic Friedel oscillations. As examples, the special cases of the $p4m$ and $p6m$ crystallographic flat orbifolds are treated in some detail. The second physical consequence is the establishment of an explicit geometric correspondence between momentum-space packing and electrical transport. A mean-field expression for the longitudinal electrical conductivity $σ_{xx}$ as a function of the {\it isoperimetric parameter} $\cal{I}$ of the occupied orbifold domain by utilizing geometric measure theory is presented, satisfiying the scaling law $ σ_{xx} \propto 1/\sqrt{\cal{I}}$. The topological transport formula naturally incorporates the vanishing conductivity ($σ_{xx} \to 0$) of the previously described ``geometric insulators,'' promoting the isoperimetric parameter as an efficient descriptor for topological and geometric phases in conductors.

</details>


### [59] [Multi-bit Ferroelectric-NAND for High-throughput Massive Database Search](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05002)
*Prasanna Venkatesan,Tanvir H. Pantha,Po-Kai Hsu,Sumukh Pinge,Zheyu Li,Chinsung Park,Priyankka Ravikumar,Hari Jayasankar,Lance Fernandes,Weihong Xu,Zihan Xia,Flavio Ponzina,Keming Fan,Amrit Garlapati,Huy Tran,Taeyoung Song,Mengkun Tian,Hang Chen,Winston Chern,Kijoon Kim,Kwangyou Seo,Suhwan Lim,Kwangsoo Kim,Wanki Kim,Daewon Ha,Duygu Kuzum,Shimeng Yu,Mingu Kang,Tajana Rosing,Sourav Dutta,Asif Khan*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This paper demonstrates that ferroelectric NAND (FE-NAND) memory can serve as an in-storage compute platform for hyperdimensional computing, achieving high search accuracy even at TLC/QLC levels and delivering ~1,000x speedup and >10,000x energy efficiency gains on terabyte-scale proteomics search tasks.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Data-intensive applications such as proteomics and autonomous systems demand large-scale database search, but von Neumann architectures are fundamentally limited by memory bandwidth and energy constraints. Hyperdimensional computing is a robust, parallelizable alternative yet suffers from high memory demands, motivating a dense, energy-efficient in-situ compute memory solution.

Method: The authors fabricate quad-level FE-NAND strings with wide memory windows, disturb resilience, and robust retention. Using these planar strings as building blocks, they experimentally demonstrate in-situ multi-level cell (MLC) dot product operations at the single-cell level and, via experimentally calibrated physics-based simulations, demonstrate Hamming similarity calculations between reference and query hypervectors.

Result: The platform exploits the inherent error tolerance of HD computing to achieve >90% search accuracy even at high logic levels (TLC, QLC). Benchmarked on Open Modification Search (OMS) tasks in proteomics with TB-scale datasets, it shows nearly 1,000x speedup and over 10,000x energy efficiency improvement versus incumbent solutions.

Conclusion: These results establish FE-NAND as a viable in-storage compute architecture for large-scale, high-dimensional data processing.

Abstract: The growing demand for large-scale database search in data-intensive applications, ranging from proteomics to autonomous systems, has exposed fundamental limitations in von Neumann architectures due to memory bandwidth and energy constraints. Hyperdimensional (HD) computing offers a robust and parallelizable framework for such tasks, but its practical implementation remains challenged by high memory demands. Ultra-high-density, energy-efficient ferroelectric NAND (FE-NAND) memory provides a potential solution by enabling in-situ computation. We fabricate quad-level FE-NAND strings with wide memory windows, disturb resilience, and robust retention. Using these planar FE-NAND strings as building blocks, we experimentally demonstrate in-situ multi-level cell (MLC) dot product operations at the single-cell level and, using experimentally calibrated physics-based simulations, demonstrate Hamming similarity calculations between reference and query hypervectors (HV). This platform leverages the inherent error tolerance of HD computing to achieve >90% search accuracy even at high logic levels (TLC, QLC). When benchmarked on Open Modification Search (OMS) tasks in proteomics with TB-scale datasets, our system shows nearly 1,000x speedup and over 10,000x energy efficiency improvement compared to incumbent solutions. These results establish FE-NAND as a viable in-storage compute architecture for large-scale, high-dimensional data processing.

</details>


### [60] [New dimensions in magnetism and superconductivity via 3D nanoprinting with focused electron and ion beams](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05443)
*Claire Donnelly,Rosa Córdoba,Amalio Fernández-Pacheco*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This review discusses focused electron- and ion-beam induced deposition (FEBID/FIBID) as 3D nanopatterning techniques for superconducting and magnetic materials, covering mechanisms, capabilities, resolution limits, and the emergent physical phenomena accessible in 3D nanoscale architectures.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Extending nanoscale quantum materials into the third dimension enables geometric tuning of emergent properties that can emulate or surpass bulk physics, with implications for high-density technological integration. This requires nanofabrication techniques capable of producing complex 3D geometries while preserving functional quantum materials with emergent phenomena.

Method: The review examines FEBID and FIBID 3D nanopatterning of superconducting and magnetic materials. It addresses the underlying deposition mechanisms and the capabilities of these techniques, and analyzes physical phenomena accessible by considering technique resolution relative to the fundamental length scales of the materials.

Result: Recent advances in FEBID and FIBID have made 3D nanopatterning of functional materials in complex geometries increasingly feasible, enabling experimental realization of new phenomena and device prototyping. The review identifies emergent effects arising from 3D geometries in magnetic and superconducting nanostructures.

Conclusion: FEBID and FIBID 3D nanopatterning offers a viable route to geometrically tuned emergent phenomena in superconducting and magnetic nanostructures, and the review highlights perspectives for future development of these techniques.

Abstract: The extension of nanoscale quantum materials to the third dimension brings new possibilities to geometrically tune emergent properties, emulating or indeed going beyond the physics of bulk materials, with exciting implications for high-density technological integration. Realising such three-dimensional architectures requires nanofabrication techniques that not only enable complex 3D geometries, but also deliver functional quantum materials with emergent phenomena. Recent advances in focused electron- and ion-beam induced deposition (FEBID and FIBID) have made 3D nanopatterning of functional materials in complex geometries increasingly feasible, paving the way for the experimental realisation of new phenomena and device prototyping. In this review, we discuss the use of FEBID and FIBID 3D nanopatterning of superconducting and magnetic materials. We address the underlying mechanisms, and capabilities of these techniques, focusing on the physical phenomena that become accessible. Considering the resolution of the techniques in the context of the fundamental lengthscales of the materials, we discuss the emergent effects that arise due to three dimensional geometries in magnetic and superconducting nanostructures, and highlight perspectives for future developments of the techniques.

</details>


### [61] [From Nested Soliton Crystals to Skyrmion Crystals in Chiral Magnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05090)
*Sven Bjarke Gudnason,Muneto Nitta*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Exhaustive study of a chiral magnetic model with Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, in-plane anisotropy, and out-of-plane Zeeman field maps the phase diagram, revealing a chiral soliton lattice on a chiral soliton lattice as ground state, a smooth crossover to a skyrmion crystal, and excited composite solitons (anti-skyrmion coexisting with bimeron/anti-bimeron).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to systematically explore the parameter space of a chiral magnetic model combining Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, in-plane anisotropy, and an out-of-plane Zeeman field to understand its full phase behavior and identify novel magnetic textures.

Method: The authors perform an exhaustive parameter-space study of the chiral magnetic model incorporating Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, an in-plane anisotropy term, and an out-of-plane Zeeman magnetic field, mapping out the phase diagram and analyzing ground and excited states.

Result: A chiral soliton lattice hosted on a chiral soliton lattice is found as the ground state in a specific corner of the phase diagram. A smooth crossover (rather than a phase transition) occurs to the skyrmion crystal lattice phase. Excited composite soliton states are also discovered, including an anti-skyrmion coexisting with a bimeron or an anti-bimeron.

Conclusion: The model exhibits rich phase behavior with a hierarchical chiral soliton lattice ground state, a crossover (not a transition) to the skyrmion crystal, and exotic excited composite solitons, expanding the known landscape of chiral magnetic textures.

Abstract: We perform an exhaustive study of the parameter space of the chiral magnetic model with a Dzyaloshinskii-Moriya interaction, an in-plane anisotropy term and an out-of-plane Zeeman magnetic field. We map out the phase diagram for the model and find a chiral soliton lattice hosted on a chiral soliton lattice as the ground state in a particular corner of the phase diagram. There is a smooth crossover, as opposed to a phase transition, to the skyrmion crystal lattice phase. Finally, we also discover excited composite soliton states such as an anti-skyrmion coexisting with a bimeron or an anti-bimeron.

</details>


### [62] [Radio-frequency electron cascade in semiconductor quantum dots](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05206)
*Tara Murphy,Lorenzo Peri,Giovanni A. Oakes,Jacob F. Chittock-Wood,Ross C. C. Leon,Fabrizio Berritta,Amber Heskes,David Wise,Anasua Chatterjee,Ferdinand Kuemmeth,M. Fernando Gonzalez-Zalba*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work studies rf-driven correlated electron cotunneling in semiconductor quantum dots and develops a general theory of "rf electron-cascade readout," experimentally demonstrating enhanced dispersive readout in foundry-fabricated silicon devices.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Cotunneling connects charge states via virtual intermediate configurations, enabling transitions otherwise blocked by Coulomb blockade. Building on an earlier proof-of-principle, the authors aim to understand rf-driven correlated cotunneling and exploit it to improve dispersive quantum-dot readout fidelity.

Method: Theoretical modeling combined with experimental measurements in two different foundry-fabricated silicon devices. An external rf drive induces correlated cyclic single-electron transitions; a general theoretical framework is developed for rf electron-cascade readout using cotunneling in quantum-dot systems with three or more dots, extended to a gate-sensed device architecture.

Result: An rf drive induces correlated cyclic single-electron transitions, enhancing charge polarization of the system. The framework yields a relative signal enhancement in multi-dot (three or more) systems, and the experimental demonstration is extended to a gate-sensed device architecture in silicon.

Conclusion: The operating principles of rf electron-cascade readout are established, providing a route to increased dispersive readout fidelity in semiconductor quantum computing architectures.

Abstract: Electron cotunneling in semiconductor quantum dots is a higher-order tunneling mechanism that connects initial and final charge states through energetically inaccessible virtual intermediate configurations. In quantum-dot arrays, such processes can produce simultaneous charge motion across multiple tunnel junctions and enable transitions that would otherwise be suppressed by Coulomb blockade. Here, building on an earlier proof-of-principle demonstration, we investigate radio-frequency (rf)-driven correlated cotunneling and its application to dispersive quantum-dot readout. Using both theoretical modeling and experimental measurements in two different foundry-fabricated silicon devices, we show that an external rf drive can induce correlated cyclic single-electron transitions, resulting in enhanced charge-polarization of the system. We develop a general theoretical framework for rf electron-cascade readout, which exploits cotunneling events in quantum-dot systems with three or more dots to produce a relative signal enhancement, and extend its experimental demonstration to a gate-sensed device architecture. Our results establish the operating principles of rf electron-cascade readout and provide a route to increased dispersive readout fidelity in semiconductor quantum computing architectures.

</details>


### [63] [Fragmented and symmetry-restored condensates in a three-site Bose ring: exchange versus umklapp](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05469)
*A. Siddiki*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Exact diagonalization of up to N=400 bosons on a three-site ring reveals a first-order transition from a q=0 condensate to a fragmented/number-squeezed standing-wave state, with the outcome governed by umklapp scattering and the sign of the ±1 interaction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To clarify the nature of the ground-state transition in a few-mode bosonic system with density-assisted hopping, and to resolve whether the resulting state is genuinely fragmented (Penrose-Onsager) or a projected/squeezed condensate, distinguishing the roles of umklapp scattering and interaction sign.

Method: Model: N bosons on a three-site ring with nearest-neighbour hopping, opposite-sign density-assisted hopping, and on-site interaction, mapped to momentum modes q=0,±1 with a channel-dependent quartic vertex. Analyzed via exact diagonalization up to N=400, comparison to Gross-Pitaevskii standing-wave energy at leading order in N, and controlled variation of umklapp scattering (available since 2·(+1)≡−1 on three sites) and on-site interaction strength/sign; Nozières exchange argument invoked for fragmentation condition.

Result: As the density-assisted term grows it inverts the band, driving a first-order ground-state transition from the q=0 condensate to a state with ⟨n₊₁⟩=⟨n₋₁⟩≈N/2; the avoided-crossing gap closes faster than exponentially with N. Without umklapp, n₊₁−n₋₁ is conserved, eigenstates form a rotor tower whose lowest member is a fragmented Fock state, and fragmentation occurs exactly when the ±1 interaction is attractive (per Nozières exchange argument with reversed sign); otherwise a single current-carrying condensate forms. With umklapp, the standing wave pins to three positions, the three momentum sectors become exponentially degenerate in N, and coherent admixture of the q=0 mode stabilises the standing wave over a wider range.

Conclusion: The exact ground state with umklapp is a number-squeezed, momentum-projected standing-wave condensate rather than a robustly fragmented state; fragmentation requires switching off umklapp and having an attractive ±1 interaction, while umklapp-induced sector degeneracy plus q=0 admixture yields a stabilised standing-wave condensate.

Abstract: We study $N$ bosons on a three-site ring with nearest-neighbour hopping, density-assisted hopping of the opposite sign, and an on-site interaction, written equivalently as three momentum modes $q=0,\pm1$ with a channel-dependent quartic vertex. As the density-assisted term grows it inverts the band, and exact diagonalisation up to $N=400$ shows a first-order ground-state transition from the $q=0$ condensate to a state with $\avg{n_{+1}}=\avg{n_{-1}}\simeq N/2$; the avoided-crossing gap closes faster than exponentially with $N$. In its symmetric eigenstates this state is fragmented in the Penrose-Onsager sense, and at leading order in $N$ it has the energy of a Gross-Pitaevskii (GP) standing wave, as expected for a few-mode mean-field system. We distinguish two cases by switching off umklapp scattering, which is available because $2\cdot(+1)\equiv-1$ on three sites, and by varying the on-site interaction. Without umklapp, $n_{+1}-n_{-1}$ is conserved, the eigenstates form a rotor tower whose lowest member is a fragmented Fock state, and the transition leads to fragmentation exactly when the $\pm1$ interaction is attractive, as predicted by Nozières' exchange argument with the sign of the interaction reversed; otherwise the system forms a single current-carrying condensate. With umklapp, the standing wave is pinned to three positions, the three momentum sectors become degenerate exponentially in $N$, and coherent admixture of the $q=0$ mode stabilises the standing wave over a wider range; the exact ground state is then a number-squeezed, momentum-projected standing-wave condensate rather than a robustly fragmented state.

</details>


### [64] [Density Functional Theory for 2D Transition-Metal Dichalcogenides with Extended Hubbard Parameters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05496)
*Xue Li,Suad Alshammari,Igor Rozhansky,James G. McHugh,Vladimir Falko*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper adds on-metal (U) and metal-chalcogen (V) Hubbard corrections to the PBE functional to accurately model band-edge electronic structure in TMDs, optimizing U and V against experimental spin-orbit splittings.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Accurate modelling of the band-edge electronic structure of transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs) like MoS2 is essential for understanding their optical, electronic, and spintronic properties and enabling future applications. Standard PBE alone is insufficiently accurate.

Method: The authors supplement the standard PBE exchange-correlation functional with on-metal (U) and metal-chalcogen (V) Hubbard corrections, which control the orbital composition of band-edge states. For MoS2, MoSe2, WS2, and WSe2, they optimize the U and V parameters by comparison with experimentally measured spin-orbit splittings at the conduction and valence band edges.

Result: The optimal U and V choices also improve agreement of the computed band gap and the ferroelectric potential drop in rhombohedral bilayers with experimentally measured values.

Conclusion: This provides a highly efficient, scalable framework for quantitatively accurate, high-throughput calculations of monolayer and multilayer TMDs, suitable for implementation in 2D-materials databases as well as large-scale electronic and spintronic device modelling.

Abstract: Accurate modelling of the band-edge electronic structure of transition metal dichalcogenides (TMDs), such as MoS$_2$, is essential for understanding their optical, electronic and spintronic properties and enabling future applications. Here, we supplement the standard PBE exchange-correlation functional with on-metal ($U$) and metal--chalcogen ($V$) Hubbard corrections, which can control the orbital composition of band-edge states and consequently improve the quantitative accuracy of various properties. For MoS$_2$, MoSe$_2$, WS$_2$, and WSe$_2$, we optimise the $U$ and $V$ parameters by comparison with experimentally measured spin--orbit splittings at the conduction and valence band edges, and show that these optimal choices also improve agreement of the computed band gap, and ferroelectric potential drop in rhombohedral bilayers, with experimentally measured values. This provides a highly efficient, scalable framework for quantitatively accurate, high-throughput calculations of monolayer and multilayer TMDs, suitable for implementation in 2D-materials databases, as well as large-scale electronic and spintronic device modelling.

</details>


### [65] [Terahertz nanoscopy of quantum-geometric photovoltaics in bilayer graphene](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05620)
*Bogdan Borodin,Jiangxu Li,Xuanpu Zhang,Aleksei Karaborchev,Som Subhra Guria,Kenji Watanabe,Takashi Taniguchi,Roshan Krishna Kumar,Fan Zhang,Petr Stepanov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Cryogenic, polarization-sensitive terahertz nanoscopy resolves the bulk photovoltaic effect in dual-gated AB bilayer graphene, disentangling it from contact/interface photothermoelectric signals and probing quantum geometry and fine band structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The bulk photovoltaic effect (BPVE) links quantum geometry to nonlinear light-matter interactions, but conventional far-field measurements cannot separate BPVE from contact- and interface-induced photothermoelectric artifacts, obscuring the intrinsic response.

Method: Developed cryogenic, polarization-sensitive terahertz microscopy with deep-subwavelength resolution and applied it to dual-gated AB bilayer graphene; performed polarization-dependent and carrier-density/temperature-dependent measurements, comparing results with theory.

Result: Near resonance between the band gap and terahertz photon energy, enhanced optical conductivity and spatially resolved BPVE were observed. Polarization dependence showed a characteristic twofold angular symmetry and a distinct shift-vector direction set by crystalline symmetries and quantum geometry. BPVE evolution with carrier density and temperature revealed signatures of trigonal warping and electron-hole asymmetry, in agreement with theory.

Conclusion: Terahertz nanoscopy of the BPVE provides an optical lens into the interplay between band structure and quantum geometry in quantum materials, offering a sensitive probe of fine electronic structure.

Abstract: The bulk photovoltaic effect (BPVE) provides a direct link between quantum geometry and nonlinear light-matter interactions, yet conventional far-field measurements cannot disentangle BPVE from contact- and interface-induced photothermoelectric signals. Here we develop cryogenic, polarization-sensitive terahertz microscopy with deep-subwavelength resolution and apply it to dual-gated AB bilayer graphene. Near resonance between the band gap and terahertz photon energy, we observe enhanced optical conductivity and spatially resolved BPVE. Polarization-dependent measurements reveal a characteristic twofold angular symmetry and a distinct shift-vector direction, both governed by crystalline symmetries and quantum geometry. The evolution of BPVE with carrier density and temperature provides a sensitive probe of the fine electronic structure of bilayer graphene, revealing signatures of trigonal warping and electron-hole asymmetry. Agreement with theory demonstrates that the terahertz nanoscopy of BPVE established here offers an optical lens into the interplay between band and quantum geometry in quantum materials.

</details>


### [66] [Chirality-induced spin selectivity as a nonequilibrium effect: a unified test of competing mechanisms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05710)
*Swagata Acharya,Mark van Schilfgaarde*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: CISS signals in nonmagnetic, time-reversal-invariant conductors are strictly time-reversal odd, vanishing at equilibrium—so structural chirality alone cannot cause spin polarization. A universal selection rule is derived and verified to machine precision across four levels of nonequilibrium transport.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The origin of Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS)—spin polarization of electrons by nonmagnetic chiral systems in the absence of magnetic fields—has remained unsettled for two decades, lacking a fundamental, mechanism-independent criterion for when the effect can occur.

Method: The authors establish an exact selection rule for CISS and test it by evaluating leading theoretical mechanisms across four levels of nonequilibrium transport, ranging from coherent classical driving to fully non-Markovian quantum baths. They compare coherent versus incoherent chiral vibrations and assess the role of non-Markovian bath memory, decomposing the effect into chiral orbital angular momentum accumulation followed by spin-orbit coupling (SOC) conversion.

Result: All measurable CISS signals are time-reversal odd and vanish near equilibrium, confirming the selection rule to machine precision. Coherent chiral vibrations produce substantial collinear polarization (up to 10% across 0.5–6 THz) that reverses with handedness, whereas incoherent vibrations yield under 1%, with non-Markovian memory further suppressing the signal. CISS acts as a strong spin polarizer and a weaker spin filter (by an order of magnitude). Polarization grows with molecular length then saturates, matching DNA and peptide trends. Reversing the drive yields inverse-CISS, a handedness-locked charge-current pulse. Once driven, polarization magnitude scales with an effective SOC, making heavy atoms and curved light-atom backbones indistinguishable at equivalent effective SOC.

Conclusion: Structural chirality alone is insufficient for CISS; the effect requires nonequilibrium driving and is fundamentally time-reversal odd. Effective SOC governs the polarization magnitude, enabling a framework that separates the contributions of geometry, driving field, and molecular length to CISS.

Abstract: For two decades, the origin of Chirality-Induced Spin Selectivity (CISS), the spin polarization of electrons by nonmagnetic chiral systems without magnetic fields, has remained unsettled. Here, we establish a single exact rule defining the fundamental conditions for the effect: in nonmagnetic, time-reversal-invariant conductors, all measurable CISS signals are time-reversal odd and vanish near equilibrium, proving that structural chirality alone is insufficient. By evaluating leading theoretical mechanisms across four levels of nonequilibrium transport, from coherent classical driving to fully non-Markovian quantum baths, we verify this selection rule to machine precision. We show that coherent chiral vibrations generate substantial collinear polarization (up to 10 percent across 0.5-6 THz) that reverses with handedness, whereas incoherent vibrations yield under 1 percent, and non-Markovian bath memory further suppresses the signal. The chiral geometry first makes the electronic motion chiral by accumulating orbital angular momentum; spin-orbit coupling (SOC) then converts that into spin. CISS therefore acts as both a spin polarizer and a spin filter, the latter an order of magnitude weaker. The polarization grows with molecular length and then saturates, matching trends reported for DNA and peptides. Reversing the drive converts decaying spin into a handedness-locked charge-current pulse (inverse-CISS). We find that once the system is driven, the polarization magnitude scales with an effective spin-orbit coupling: making heavy atoms and curved light-atom backbones indistinguishable at equivalent effective SOC. This enables us to chart how geometry, driving field, and length separate those routes.

</details>


### [67] [Gate-tunable large second-order and third-order nonlinear Hall effect in topological insulator BiSbTeSe2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05713)
*Fengyi Guo,Shuai Zhang,Xiubing Li,Zheng Dai,Congcong Li,Yufan Ju,Ziqi Wang,Cheng Bin,Zhe Ying,Fengqi Song*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Large second- and third-order nonlinear Hall effects are demonstrated in dual-gated BiSbTeSe2 devices, both highly gate-tunable, with the second-order response exceeding prior topological insulator efficiencies and persisting to room temperature.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The nonlinear Hall effect (NLHE) is promising for next-generation electronic devices, making it essential to explore and engineer a strong and highly tunable NLHE.

Method: Fabrication and measurement of dual-gated BiSbTeSe2 devices, combined with scaling analysis to identify the microscopic origins of the second- and third-order NLHE.

Result: Both second- and third-order NLHEs are observed and are highly gate-tunable. The second-order NLHE shows higher generation efficiency than previous topological insulator systems and remains stable up to room temperature, while the third-order NLHE efficiency is comparable to prior reports. Scaling analysis attributes the second-order NLHE to skew scattering plus a term compatible with either the Berry curvature dipole or side jump, and the third-order NLHE to the Berry connection polarizability tensor and higher-order skew scattering.

Conclusion: Dual-gated BiSbTeSe2 provides a promising experimental platform for developing NLHE-based devices.

Abstract: The nonlinear Hall effect (NLHE) holds significant promise for next-generation electronic device applications, and it is essential to explore and engineer a strong and highly tunable NLHE. In this work, we report large second-order and third-order NLHEs in dual-gated BiSbTeSe2 devices. Both the second-order and third-order NLHE are highly gate-tunable. The second-order NLHE exhibits a higher generation efficiency than those reported in previous topological insulator systems, and it can be stably observed up to room temperature. Meanwhile, the generation efficiency of the third-order NLHE is comparable to previously reported values. The scaling analysis indicates that the second-order NLHE is consistent with contributions from skew scattering and a term compatible with either the Berry curvature dipole or side jump, whereas the third-order NLHE is consistent with contributions from the Berry connection polarizability tensor and higher-order skew scattering. Our work establishes a promising experimental platform for the development of NLHE-based devices.

</details>


### [68] [Current selects the helicity of a chiral phonon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05714)
*Swagata Acharya,Jerome Jackson,Mark van Schilfgaarde*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: In a biased chiral conductor, the electric current makes one chiral phonon helicity damp less than its time-reversal partner, converting current-induced Berry force into a net lattice helicity that grows linearly with bias and can condense into a self-sustained travelling wave; demonstrated in elemental tellurium via a quasi-ab initio open-quantum-system framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS) is strongly enhanced by chiral phonons of definite wavevector q, but near equilibrium the +q and -q helicities are equally populated as time-reversal partners, so their contributions cancel. A mechanism is needed to break this balance and select a single phonon helicity without external optical pumping.

Method: The authors analyze phonon kinetics in a biased chiral conductor and show that the current induces an asymmetric damping of the two helicities. The damping asymmetry is odd in current, odd under q->-q, invariant under a mirror reversing structural handedness, and equal mode-for-mode to the current-induced nonconservative Berry force on the phonon coordinate. A rate equation closes the phonon kinetics. The effect is demonstrated in elemental tellurium treated as an open quantum system (electronic Hamiltonian, phonon bath, electron-phonon coupling) in a quasi-ab initio framework: electronic Hamiltonian from vertex-corrected quasiparticle self-consistent GW (QSGW) with spin-orbit coupling; nuclear displacements from a machine-learned interatomic potential yielding chiral phonon modes at finite +/-q_z on the Gamma-A line; electron-phonon perturbation approximated by a frozen-phonon deformation potential.

Result: The current causes one phonon helicity to damp less than the other, producing a net lattice helicity: at any finite bias one helicity is preferentially populated, with no threshold, growing linearly with the current as V->0. Above a threshold, that helicity becomes a self-sustained coherent travelling wave. Reversing the current reverses the selection. The damping asymmetry equals the current-induced nonconservative Berry force on the phonon coordinate.

Conclusion: A bias alone, with no optical pump, can drive a current that selects a chiral phonon helicity in a chiral conductor, and this selection can condense into a self-sustained coherent travelling wave above a threshold. The effect is grounded in a quasi-ab initio open-quantum-system treatment of elemental tellurium and may provide a bias-controlled route to helicity-selective phonon states relevant to CISS.

Abstract: The emergence of spin-polarized electrons from nonmagnetic chiral molecules and crystals is called Chirality-induced spin selectivity (CISS). A chiral phonon of definite wavevector $q$ can strongly enhance it, but near equilibrium the two helicities $+q$ and $-q$ are equally populated: they are time-reversal partners and their contributions cancel. We show that in a biased chiral conductor the current causes the phonon of one helicity to damp less than the other. The damping asymmetry is odd in the current, odd under $q\rightarrow-q$, invariant under a mirror that reverses the structural handedness, and equal, mode for mode, to the current-induced nonconservative Berry force on the phonon coordinate. Closing the phonon kinetics with a rate equation turns the asymmetry into a net lattice helicity: at any finite bias one helicity is preferentially populated, with no threshold, growing linearly with the current as $V\rightarrow0$; above a threshold that helicity becomes a self-sustained coherent travelling wave. We demonstrate this effect in elemental tellurium, treated as an open quantum system (electronic Hamiltonian, phonon bath, and electron-phonon coupling) within a quasi-ab initio framework. The electronic Hamiltonian is supplied by a vertex-corrected quasiparticle self-consistent $GW$ (QSGW) potential with spin-orbit coupling; nuclear displacements are modeled with a machine-learned interatomic potential, which yields chiral phonon modes at finite $\pm q_z$ on the $Γ$-A line. The perturbation to the electronic Hamiltonian from a nuclear displacement is approximated by a frozen-phonon deformation potential. No optical pump is needed: a bias alone drives the current and selects the phonon helicity; reversing the current reverses the selection.

</details>


### [69] [Mirror-Constrained Optical Hall Rectification in Asymmetric Gold Gratings:\\ Edelstein Conversion and a Phase-Seeded Kerr Four-Wave Mixing Instability](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05761)
*Michael N. Leuenberger,Richard M. Osgood*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A symmetry-complete effective model explains transverse optical rectification in a 1D asymmetric Au grating: the required mirror-odd factor arises dynamically from phase-locked surface-plasmon-polariton quasinormal modes via a Kerr-driven pitchfork instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Transverse optical rectification was observed in a 1D asymmetric Au grating despite the ideal grating profile preserving xz-mirror symmetry. Since the transverse dc current Jy is mirror-odd while the p-polarized bilinears are mirror-even, a distinct mirror-odd factor is symmetry-required, and its physical origin needs to be identified and formalized.

Method: A symmetry-complete effective model combining the origin-independent cell invariant A = Im(h1^2 h2*), Drude-Edelstein spin polarization, and spin-orbit conversion. A dynamical realization of the mirror-odd factor is proposed for a finite-width device: a driven pump-like plasmonic mode at ky≈0 generates mirror-pair transverse surface-plasmon-polariton quasinormal modes (Kx, ±qy) through the same third-order susceptibility responsible for self- and cross-Kerr shifts. Four-wave mixing creates the pair and locks its phase sum; weak coherent backscattering renders the relative drive phase observable, and a small phase difference (from incident ky, spot displacement, edges, or disorder) unfolds a Kerr directional instability that selects its sign deterministically. Full three-mode equations, pump-only and saturated-state Bogoliubov analyses, and an imperfect-pitchfork reduction are derived.

Result: The dynamical population imbalance supplies the mirror-odd factor Ldyn = ληη in the structure-dependent A·L·⟨jx sy⟩_t side-jump-like channel, which vanishes for the centered symmetric grating because A = 0. The normalized numerical realization exhibits a robust supercritical pitchfork with an approximately one-half critical exponent and phase-reversed branch selection.

Conclusion: Transverse optical rectification in the asymmetric Au grating is governed by a combined cell-invariant and dynamical mirror-odd factor with a side-jump-like channel; the mirror-odd factor emerges deterministically via a Kerr directional (pitchfork) instability of phase-locked surface-plasmon-polariton pairs, providing a validated, symmetry-consistent description.

Abstract: Transverse optical rectification has been reported in a one-dimensional asymmetric Au grating even though an ideal profile $h(x)$ retains the $xz$ mirror $\Me:y\to-y$. Because $J_y$ is odd under $\Me$, whereas the $p$-polarized bilinears $|E_x|^2$, $|E_z|^2$, and $\mathrm{Re}(E_xE_z^*)$ are even, the transverse dc current requires a distinct mirror-odd factor $\Lamy$. We formulate a symmetry-complete effective model combining the origin-independent cell invariant $\Astr=\mathrm{Im}(h_1^2h_2^*)$, Drude--Edelstein spin polarization, and spin--orbit conversion. We then propose a dynamical realization of $\Lamy$ for a finite-width device. A driven pump-like plasmonic mode at $k_y\simeq0$ generates a mirror pair of transverse surface-plasmon-polariton quasinormal modes $(K_x,\pm q_y)$ through the same third-order susceptibility that produces self- and cross-Kerr shifts. Four-wave mixing creates the pair and locks its phase sum but, by itself, cannot change the pair-population difference. Weak coherent backscattering makes the relative drive phase observable; a small phase difference from incident $k_y$, spot displacement, edges, or disorder then unfolds a Kerr directional instability and deterministically selects its sign. The resulting population imbalance supplies the dynamical mirror-odd factor $\Ldyn=λ_ηη$ in the structure-dependent $\Astr\Lamy\langle j_xs_y\rangle_t$ side-jump-like channel, which vanishes for the centered symmetric grating because $\Astr=0$. We give the complete three-mode equations, the pump-only and saturated-state Bogoliubov problems, the imperfect-pitchfork reduction, and a validated normalized numerical realization. The normalized equations exhibit a robust supercritical pitchfork with an approximately one-half critical exponent and phase-reversed branch selection.

</details>


### [70] [Ferromagnetic Bloch Line as a Bead on a String](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05859)
*Jake Hoffmann,Oleg Tchernyshyov*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: A minimal model treats a Bloch line (meron) in a thin-film ferromagnet as a charged, massless gyroscopic bead on a string—a particle sliding along a linear domain wall—where the meron clamps the wall, fully reflects low-amplitude spin waves, and responds to fields and spin-transfer torques either via a force on the Bloch line or by driving the whole wall, matching simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper seeks a simple physical picture of Bloch lines in thin-film ferromagnets with strong easy-axis and intermediate in-plane anisotropies, aiming to explain how these topological objects move and interact with the surrounding domain wall and spin waves.

Method: A Bloch line is modeled as a meron represented as a charged, massless particle that slides along a linear domain wall in a background magnetic field, analogous to a gyroscopic bead on a string. Its gyroscopic behavior dynamically isolates the wall segments on either side, motivating a description of a Bloch line as two strings joined at a moving boundary. The model includes the effects of out-of-plane and in-plane magnetic fields and spin-transfer torques, which act either by exerting a force on the Bloch line itself or by driving the wall as a whole.

Result: The meron clamps the wall at the junction and completely reflects low-amplitude spin waves propagating along it. The model's predictions agree with numerical simulations.

Conclusion: The Bloch-line-as-gyroscopic-bead model captures the essential dynamics of merons in domain walls and provides predictions consistent with numerical simulations, offering a simple framework for understanding field- and torque-driven behavior of Bloch lines.

Abstract: We present a simple model of a Bloch line in a thin-film ferromagnet with strong easy-axis and intermediate in-plane anisotropies. The meron is represented as a charged, massless particle that slides along the linear domain wall in a background magnetic field- a gyroscopic bead on a string. Its gyroscopic behavior dynamically isolates the wall segments on either side of the meron, motivating the model for a Bloch line as two strings joined at a moving boundary. The meron clamps the wall at this junction, completely reflecting low-amplitude spin waves that propagate along it. Out-of-plane and in-plane magnetic fields and spin-transfer torques then act in two ways: by exerting a force on the Bloch line itself, or by driving the wall as a whole. Predictions of this model are in agreement with numerical simulations.

</details>


### [71] [Stability and current-driven dynamics of mixed-topology bimeron clusters](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05917)
*Philippe Heymes,Thomas L. Schmidt,Vladyslav M. Kuchkin*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: This work investigates bimerons and their clusters with mixed topological indices, showing that a bimeron-antibimeron pair has three competing decay mechanisms, that cluster dynamics under in-plane and out-of-plane currents follow Zhang-Li and Slonczewski torques, and that all cluster velocities lie on a single Hamiltonian-governed ellipse within the Thiele framework.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Bimerons and their clusters with mixed topological indices offer a tunable route to controlling the skyrmion Hall angle, which depends on the cluster's total topological charge. Understanding their stability and current-driven dynamics is therefore necessary for exploiting them in applications.

Method: The stability of the simplest cluster, a bimeron-antibimeron pair, is examined using the geodesic nudged elastic band method to identify decay mechanisms and their energy barriers. The dynamics of the clusters are then studied numerically for in-plane and perpendicular currents, corresponding to the Zhang-Li and Slonczewski mechanisms. Finally, a wide variety of bimeron clusters are analyzed within the Thiele approach.

Result: Three mechanisms are identified for the bimeron-antibimeron pair -- bimeron separation, interchange, and annihilation -- with comparable energy barriers that set the pair's overall stability and provide an upper bound for more sophisticated clusters. In the Thiele analysis, the velocities of all studied bimeron clusters are found to lie on a specific ellipse whose parameters are fully governed by the model Hamiltonian.

Conclusion: The comparable energy barriers of separation, interchange, and annihilation dictate the stability of bimeron-antibimeron pairs and bound that of larger clusters, while the universal velocity ellipse obtained from the Thiele approach provides a general description of current-driven bimeron cluster dynamics governed solely by the model Hamiltonian.

Abstract: In this work, we study bimerons (in-plane skyrmions) and their clusters composed of states with mixed topological indices. These clusters provide flexibility in controlling the skyrmion Hall angle, which depends on the cluster's total topological charge. First, using the simplest such cluster, a bimeron-antibimeron pair, we examine its stability with the geodesic nudged elastic band method and identify three mechanisms -- bimeron separation, interchange, and annihilation -- with comparable energy barriers that determine the pair's overall stability.
