This provides an upper bound for the stability of more sophisticated clusters of bimerons and antibimerons. Then, we numerically study the clusters' dynamics for currents applied in-plane and perpendicular to the plane, corresponding to the Zhang-Li and Slonczewski mechanisms. Finally, we analyze the dynamics of a wide variety of bimeron clusters within the Thiele approach and show that their velocities always lie on a specific ellipse whose parameters are fully governed by the model Hamiltonian.

</details>


### [72] [Beyond the Flat-Band Paradigm: Open Orbits as a Route to Correlated States in Dispersive Moiré Minibands](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06110)
*Zihao Wang,Pablo M. Perez-Piskunow,Calvin Pei Yu Wong,Matthew Holwill,Hao Sun,Wei Fu,Junxiong Hu,Zheng Yuntian,Zhang Xuanrui,T. Taniguchi,K. Watanabe,A. Ariando,Geliang Yu,Li Lin,Kuan Eng Johnson Goh,Stephan Roche,Jeil Jung,Kostya S. Novoselov,Nicolas Leconte*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors propose that strong correlations resembling fractional Chern insulator (FCI) behavior can arise in the highly dispersive second moiré miniband of monolayer graphene on hexagonal boron nitride, driven by a fermiology mechanism in the low-field classical-to-quantum crossover regime (B < 1.5 T).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Fractional Chern insulators have almost exclusively been realized in isolated, nearly flat bands of complex bilayer or multilayer moiré systems with ideal quantum geometry. Whether such correlated topological phases can emerge in genuinely dispersive bands, and via what mechanism, is an open question. The authors aim to identify an alternative, fermiology-driven route that does not require flat bands.

Method: The study focuses on the second moiré miniband of monolayer graphene on hexagonal boron nitride in the low-field classical-to-quantum crossover regime. Theoretically, it analyzes how the interplay of the moiré potential and weak magnetic fields (B < 1.5 T) alters electron dynamics: saddle-point van Hove singularities generating extended open orbits that suppress carrier propagation, weak-field lifting of valley degeneracy, and trigonal warping redistributing Berry curvature. Experimentally, it employs high-resolution magnetotransport and temperature-dependent measurements.

Result: Magnetotransport and temperature-dependent measurements reveal fractional-slope transport features and signatures of interaction-driven incompressibility. The Hall conductivity is not fully quantized and the longitudinal resistance remains finite, so the observations are described as "incipient FCI behavior" rather than a fully developed FCI phase.

Conclusion: The results suggest a possible fermiology-driven route toward correlated topological states in dispersive bands within the classical-to-quantum crossover, distinct from the conventional flat-band scenario, though the authors refrain from claiming a fully established FCI phase.

Abstract: The realization of fractional Chern insulators has largely relied on isolated flat bands in complex bilayer or multilayer moiré systems with ideal quantum geometry. Whether such phases can emerge in genuinely dispersive bands, and by what mechanism, remains an open question. Here, we propose a mechanism for magnetic-field-induced strong correlations in the highly dispersive bands of monolayer graphene on hexagonal boron nitride. In the low-field classical-to-quantum crossover regime of the second moiré miniband, we show that the interplay of the moiré potential and weak magnetic fields (B < 1.5 T) profoundly alters electron dynamics. Saddle-point van Hove singularities produce extended open orbits that strongly suppress carrier propagation, potentially favoring a regime in which Coulomb interactions become comparatively more important, while weak magnetic fields lift valley degeneracy and trigonal warping redistributes Berry curvature. High-resolution magnetotransport and temperature-dependent measurements reveal fractional-slope transport features and signatures of interaction-driven incompressibility. Because the Hall conductivity is not fully quantized and the longitudinal resistance remains finite, we describe these observations as incipient FCI behavior: transport signatures consistent with a developing fractional Chern insulating state, without claiming a fully developed FCI phase established by complete Hall quantization and vanishing longitudinal resistance. Our results suggest a possible fermiology-driven route toward correlated topological states in dispersive bands in the classical-to-quantum crossover, distinct from the conventional flat-band scenario.

</details>


### [73] [Intrinsic spin-orbit coupling in graphene stacks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05905)
*Guillermo Parra-Martinez,Alejandro Jimeno-Pozo,Ady Stern,Jose Angel Silva-Guillén,Francisco Guinea*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: Intrinsic spin-orbit coupling is re-evaluated in graphene multilayers including all carbon valence orbitals; the leading term combines a Rashba-like spin-flip with interlayer hopping, altering the phase diagram, Fermi surfaces, and low-field magnetization of finite rhombohedral stacks, proving SOC cannot be neglected in multilayer graphite.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: SOC in monolayer graphene is typically ignored because the electronic structure is nearly unchanged, and this neglect has been carried over to other graphitic systems. The authors challenge this assumption by examining whether SOC plays a more significant role in graphene stacks.

Method: Calculates the intrinsic SOC in multilayer graphene from a model including all carbon valence orbitals and intra-atomic SOC. The leading spin-orbit term is identified as a combination of a Rashba-like spin-flip process with interlayer hopping, then applied to finite rhombohedral stacks.

Result: The intrinsic SOC term modifies the phase diagram and Fermi surfaces of finite rhombohedral stacks and produces a distinct density dependence of the magnetization in weak magnetic fields.

Conclusion: Contrary to the single-layer case, SOC has important consequences in multilayer graphitic systems and should not be neglected when studying their physical properties.

Abstract: The effect of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) in monolayer graphene has been thoroughly studied in the past. Its impact is commonly disregarded since the electronic structure of graphene is almost unaffected. Consequently, SOC has also been ignored in other graphitic systems. In this work, we calculate the intrinsic SOC in stacks of graphene layers from a model that includes all the valence orbitals of carbon as well as the intra-atomic SOC.The leading spin-orbit term in a graphene multilayer combines a Rashba-like spin-flip process with interlayer hopping. We find that this intrinsic term modifies the phase diagram and Fermi surfaces of finite rhombohedral stacks, and it leads to a distinct density-dependence of the magnetization in weak magnetic fields. This Letter shows that, unlike in the single-layer case, SOC has important consequences in multilayer graphitic systems and it should not be neglected when studying their physical properties.

</details>


### [74] [An \textit{ab initio} based compact analytical formula for inelastic x-ray scattering from spatially localized excitons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06131)
*Joris Sturm,Duc Anh Truong,Andrei Benediktovitch,Nina Rohringer,Andreas Knorr$^{1}$*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The authors develop a compact analytical model for inelastic x-ray scattering (IXS) of spatially localized excitons, combine it with first-principles calculations, and apply it to LiF, finding that the choice of single-particle wave functions strongly affects the predicted spectrum.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Inelastic x-ray scattering of localized excitons requires a tractable yet accurate theoretical description. Existing treatments are either computationally heavy or lack a compact formulation, and the sensitivity of the spectrum to the underlying single-particle wave functions is not well characterized.

Method: A compact analytical model for IXS of spatially localized excitons, combined with first-principles calculations and applied to lithium fluoride (LiF). Two different approaches for the underlying single-particle wave functions are compared for computing the spectrum.

Result: Comparison of the two single-particle wave function approaches yields qualitatively good agreement with experimental data. However, the comparison also reveals that a detailed, accurate description of the wave functions is essential to obtain qualitative (and reliable) results.

Conclusion: The compact model provides a workable framework for describing IXS of localized excitons, but its predictive quality hinges critically on the fidelity of the single-particle wave functions used as input, as demonstrated for LiF.

Abstract: In this contribution we provide a compact model for the description of inelastic x-ray scattering of spatially localized excitons. We combine our analytical theory with first principles calculations and apply it to the material lithium fluoride (LiF). A comparison of two different approaches for the underlying single-particle wave functions to calculate the spectrum yield a qualitatively good agreement with experimental data but also shows that a detailed description of the wave functions is essential to obtain qualitative results.

</details>


### [75] [Phenomenology of reactive and dissipative nutation in antiferromagnets](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06145)
*Erlend Syljuåsen,Hans Gløckner Giil,Arne Brataas*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The paper formulates equations of motion for uniform magnetization dynamics in multisublattice magnets including inertial damping, derives susceptibilities and eigenfrequencies for a two-sublattice uniaxial antiferromagnet, and proposes an experiment to measure the dynamical coefficients.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To incorporate a recently proposed inertial damping torque into a general description of uniform magnetization dynamics in multisublattice magnets, addressing intra- and intersublattice contributions.

Method: Formulation of equations of motion with Gilbert damping, reactive inertia, and inertial damping terms; analytical derivation of dynamical magnetic susceptibilities and approximate eigenfrequencies for a two-sublattice uniaxial antiferromagnet in an easy-axis static field across all field-dependent equilibrium phases.

Result: Derived dynamical magnetic susceptibilities and approximate eigenfrequencies, showing how inertial damping affects relaxation of nutational modes.

Conclusion: Inertial damping measurably influences nutational mode relaxation, and an experimental protocol is proposed to determine the dynamical coefficients.

Abstract: We formulate equations of motion for uniform magnetization dynamics in multisublattice magnets, with emphasis on a recently proposed inertial damping torque. We include intra- and intersublattice contributions to Gilbert damping, reactive inertia, and inertial damping. For a two-sublattice uniaxial antiferromagnet in an easy-axis static magnetic field, we derive the dynamical magnetic susceptibilities and approximate eigenfrequencies across all field-dependent equilibrium phases. In particular, we show how inertial damping affects the relaxation of the nutational modes and propose an experimental protocol to determine the dynamical coefficients.

</details>


### [76] [The future of 3D NAND flash technology](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06363)
*Prasanna Venkatesan,Taeyoung Song,Gaurav Thareja,Luca Larcher,Andrea Padovani,Cristian Zambelli,Shimeng Yu,Suman Datta,Tajana Rosing,Priyankka Ravikumar,Biswajit Ray,Raisul Islam,Wanki Kim,Daewon Ha,Asif Khan*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: 3D NAND scaling is shifting from a lithography-driven challenge to a physics-limited regime, requiring tier-specific co-optimization rather than a single device roadmap.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: 3D NAND has become the foundational non-volatile storage platform of the AI era, but scaling is no longer governed primarily by process integration or lithography. Instead, it is constrained by the physics of charge storage: lateral charge migration, electrostatic coupling, read disturb, and transport limitations are entering a margin-limited regime where their collective interaction compresses operating margins each generation.

Method: As a Perspective, the paper examines converging bottlenecks and proposes an application-specific co-optimization framework rather than a monolithic device roadmap, positioning conventional charge-trap flash, ferroelectric storage, alternative channel materials, and system-level integration as complementary solutions.

Result: The analysis identifies that no single mechanism dominates margin compression; rather, the collective interaction of multiple charge-storage physics effects is the limiting factor as roadmaps target kilolayer stacks and tens of trillions of devices per die.

Conclusion: Sustaining NAND scaling requires application-specific co-optimization, with different storage and integration technologies targeted to distinct tiers of data-centric computing.

Abstract: 3D NAND flash has become the foundational non-volatile storage platform of the AI era, underpinning workloads from model training to large-scale inference and cold data archival. With roadmaps now targeting kilolayer stacks and tens of trillions of devices per die, scaling is no longer governed primarily by process integration or lithography. Instead, it is increasingly constrained by the physics of charge storage itself: lateral charge migration, electrostatic coupling, read disturb, and transport limitations are entering a margin-limited regime. In this regime, their collective interaction, not any single mechanism, compresses operating margins with each generation. In this Perspective, we examine these converging bottlenecks and argue that sustaining NAND scaling will require application-specific co-optimization rather than a monolithic device roadmap. In this framework, conventional charge-trap flash, ferroelectric storage, alternative channel materials, and system-level integration are best viewed as complementary solutions targeted to distinct tiers of data-centric computing.

</details>


### [77] [Exact vortex decomposition of the shift current](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06449)
*Matias Castro Schnaidt,Felipe Perez Riffo,Juan M. Florez,Eric Suarez Morell*

Main category: cond-mat.mes-hall

TL;DR: The shift vector is a singular field whose curl has two sources: interband Berry curvature and quantized point charges at transition-dipole zeros. This yields an exact decomposition of the shift conductivity into one integer-charge sector plus three continuous sectors. In graphene-Haldane and biased AB bilayers, the band-edge peak is governed by the winding of a single band-edge charge, not the Chern number.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper addresses whether an integer invariant survives in the shift current and whether the Chern number controls its sign. The goal is to resolve the role of topological integers and quantized charges in the shift photocurrent, especially at band edges and across topological transitions.

Method: The authors show that the shift vector is a singular field on the Brillouin torus, with its curl sourced by the interband Berry curvature and quantized point charges at zeros of the transition dipole. Inverting this relation gives an exact frequency-resolved decomposition of the shift conductivity into one sector linear in the integer charges and three continuous sectors. They test the formalism on a graphene-Haldane bilayer, sweeping the interband Chern number through five values at fixed gap, and on the time-reversal-symmetric biased AB bilayer.

Result: When a charge sits at the band edge and trigonal warping is present, the integer sector alone accounts for the band-edge peak. The relevant integer is the winding of that single charge, not the Chern number. Its sign, together with a valley-dependent profile, sets the sign of the photocurrent. Sign reversals across topological transitions follow this winding; the invariant changes with it only when the same gap closing does both.

Conclusion: The shift conductivity admits an exact decomposition into an integer-charge sector and continuous sectors. Band-edge shift current is controlled by the winding of a single band-edge charge rather than the Chern number. A band-edge charge requires only that the two band-edge sublattices not be connected by nearest-neighbour hopping, with trigonal warping making it visible.

Abstract: Does any integer survive in the shift current, and does the Chern number control its sign? We show that the shift vector is a singular field on the Brillouin torus. Its curl has two sources: the interband Berry curvature and quantised point charges at the zeros of the transition dipole. Inverting this relation gives an exact decomposition of the shift conductivity, valid at every frequency, into one sector linear in the integer charges and three continuous sectors. As a testing ground, we take a graphene-Haldane bilayer: a model that lets us sweep the interband Chern number through five values at fixed gap to isolate its role. We also test the formalism against the time-reversal-symmetric biased AB bilayer. Whenever a charge sits at the band edge, and trigonal warping is present, the integer sector alone accounts for the band-edge peak. The relevant integer is not the Chern number but the winding of that single charge. Its sign, combined with a valley-dependent profile, sets the sign of the photocurrent. In these bilayers, sign reversals across topological transitions follow this winding; the invariant changes with it only when the same gap closing does both. A band-edge charge needs no invariant. It only requires that the two band-edge sublattices not be connected by the nearest-neighbour hopping, and trigonal warping makes it visible.

</details>

### [78] [On hyperfine structure of the rotationally excited $1 s_μ n\ell_{e}$-states in helium muonic atoms](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03810)
*Alexei M. Frolov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper develops a new approach to determine the hyperfine structure of rotationally excited $1s_\mu n\ell_e$ states in three-body helium muonic ${}^3$He$\mu e$ and ${}^4$He$\mu e$ atoms.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: These hyperfine structure splittings for the bound rotationally excited states remain unknown because no analogous formulas existed in earlier studies.

Method: Derives general formulas that allow evaluation of hyperfine structure splittings for an arbitrary rotationally excited (bound) $1s_\mu n\ell_e$ state in the three-body helium muonic atoms.

Result: The derived formulas enable evaluation of the hyperfine structure splittings for arbitrary rotationally excited $1s_\mu n\ell_e$ states, addressing previously unknown splittings for these bound states.

Conclusion: The new approach provides the first general means to determine hyperfine structure splittings in rotationally excited three-body helium muonic ${}^3$He$\mu e$ and ${}^4$He$\mu e$ atoms, filling a gap left by earlier studies.

Abstract: The new approach is develop to determine the hyperfine structure of the rotationally excited $1 s_μ n \ell_{e}$-states in the three-body helium muonic ${}^{3}$He $μ\; e$ and ${}^{4}$He $μ\; e$ atoms. The formulas derived in this study allow one to evaluate the hyperfine structure splittings for an arbitrary rotationally excited (bound) $1 s_μ n \ell_{e}$-state in the three-body helium muonic atoms. Similar formulas have never been produced in earlier studies and this explains why these hyperfine structure splittings are still unknown for these bound states.

</details>


### [79] [Electrical and optical dipole polarizabilities of two lowest triplet states of the beryllium atom](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03901)
*Tymoteusz Mrozek,Krzysztof Strasburger*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: First high-accuracy static and dynamic polarizabilities of the 2³P_u and 3³S_g excited states of neutral beryllium, computed with explicitly correlated Gaussian basis sets.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: No prior calculations of static and dynamic polarizabilities for these excited beryllium states existed using explicitly correlated basis sets, leaving a gap in accurate response-property data.

Method: Explicitly correlated Gaussian (ECG) basis sets were used; first-order wave functions were obtained by minimizing the Hylleraas functional. Response-property basis sets contained ~7000–8000 functions, varying with polarizability component and symmetry.

Result: High-accuracy static and dynamic polarizabilities were obtained, covering frequencies from the static limit ω=0 up to the third resonance for 2³P_u and the second resonance for 3³S_g.

Conclusion: These are, to the authors' knowledge, the first static and dynamic polarizability calculations for these excited beryllium states using explicitly correlated basis sets.

Abstract: High-accuracy static and dynamic polarizabilities of the $2^3P_u$ and $^{3}S_{g}$ states of the neutral beryllium atom are reported. The calculations employed explicitly correlated Gaussian (ECG) basis sets, and first-order wave functions were obtained by minimization of the Hylleraas functional. The final basis sets used for the response-property calculations contained c.a. 7000-8000 functions, depending on the polarizability component and the chosen symmetry. The results cover frequencies from the static limit, \(ω= 0\), up to the third resonance for the \(2^{3}P_{u}\) state and up to the second resonance for the \(3^{3}S_{g}\) state. To the best of our knowledge, these are the first calculations of dynamic and static polarizabilities for these excited states of the beryllium atom using explicitly correlated basis sets.

</details>


### [80] [CDW-EIS calculations of atomic ionization by bare ions and antiprotons](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04059)
*Darío M. Mitnik,Jorge E. Miraglia,Claudia. C Montanari*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The authors compute CDW-EIS subshell-resolved total ionization cross sections for atomic targets from H to Kr under bare-ion and antiproton impact, release the data in an open repository, analyze systematic trends, and train a neural-network surrogate (CDWEIS-NN) that interpolates results to new projectile-target systems and energies.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ionization cross-section data are needed for elements in periods 3 and 4 (relevant to pigment analysis and technologically important materials), but comprehensive subshell-resolved datasets across many projectile-target systems and velocities are lacking, and full CDW-EIS computations are impractical to run everywhere.

Method: Ab initio CDW-EIS (continuum distorted-wave eikonal initial-state) calculations of atomic ionization by bare ions and antiprotons, covering targets from H to Kr over a broad range of projectile charges and velocities. Systematic trend analyses include projectile charge and velocity dependence, binding-energy scaling, and charge-sign effects in proton versus antiproton impact. A neural-network surrogate (CDWEIS-NN) is trained on the subset with projectile charge |Z_P| <= 8 to interpolate to additional systems and energies.

Result: The dataset provides subshell-resolved total ionization cross sections, openly available via an online repository across a broad range of projectile-target systems, occupied initial subshells, and projectile velocities. CDWEIS-NN reproduces both held-out CDW-EIS calculations and the experimental benchmarks considered with satisfactory accuracy.

Conclusion: The work delivers an openly accessible CDW-EIS ionization cross-section database plus an accurate neural-network surrogate, enabling practical interpolation and access to cross sections for a wide range of projectile-target systems, and highlights systematic scaling and charge-sign effects in ion and antiproton impact.

Abstract: We present \textit{ab initio} calculations of atomic ionization by bare ions and antiprotons within the continuum distorted-wave eikonal initial-state (CDW-EIS) model. The dataset covers atomic targets from H to Kr, impacted by bare ions over a broad range of projectile charges and by antiprotons. Particular attention is given to elements in periods 3 and 4 of the periodic table, which are relevant to pigment analysis and technologically important materials. The resulting subshell-resolved total ionization cross sections are made openly available through an online repository covering a broad range of projectile--target systems, occupied initial subshells, and projectile velocities. We analyze systematic trends in the cross sections, including their dependence on projectile charge and velocity, binding-energy scaling, and charge-sign effects in proton and antiproton impact. To extend the practical coverage of the database, we also develop a neural-network surrogate, CDWEIS-NN, trained on the subset of the database with projectile charge $|Z_\mathrm{P}|\leq 8$. The network interpolates the CDW-EIS results to additional projectile--target systems and impact energies, reproducing both held-out CDW-EIS calculations and the experimental benchmarks considered in this work with satisfactory accuracy.

</details>


### [81] [Frequency-Axis Calibration via Autler-Townes Splitting in an Electromagnetically Induced Transparency Feature](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04175)
*Stone B. Oliver,Noah Schlossberger,Tate McDonald,Christopher L. Holloway,Nikunjkumar Prajapati*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The paper presents a frequency-axis calibration method for laser scans in Rydberg atom-based electrometry, using a known rf frequency scale and Autler-Townes splitting via the avoided-crossing behavior of near-resonant rf radiation, fitted to a two-level model with weighted nonlinear least-squares regression.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Rydberg atom-based electrometry enables rf field measurements traceable to the International System of Units, but converting direct rf power measurements to frequency measurements typically requires well-defined spectral features, a commercial wavemeter, or narrow cavity lines from clock-disciplined electro-optic modulators—resources that are not always available.

Method: The relative separation between Autler-Townes split features is measured as a function of applied rf field power and detuning from atomic resonance. These peak separations are then fitted to a simple two-level model using weighted nonlinear least-squares regression with three fitting parameters, one being the desired calibration factor.

Result: The method is consistent with calibration via known fine-structure separation to within approximately 1.11% or better, and it is robust against substantial experimental deviation from the system's known rf power dependence.

Conclusion: A known rf frequency scale can be used to infer a laser's frequency scan via Autler-Townes splitting, achieving frequency-axis calibration through the avoided-crossing behavior of near-resonant rf radiation without an absolute frequency reference.

Abstract: Rydberg atom-based electrometry is a pivotal technology that enables radio-frequency (rf) field measurements traceable to the International System of Units. This is typically accomplished by converting the direct rf power measurement of classical probes to a frequency measurement referenced to two well-defined spectral features, a commercial wavemeter, or narrow cavity lines produced via clock disciplined electro-optic modulators, although there exist cases where none of these are available. In this article, we demonstrate that a known rf frequency scale can be used to infer a laser's frequency scan using Autler-Townes (AT) splitting. In other words, we show that frequency-axis calibration can be achieved using the avoided-crossing behavior of near-resonant rf radiation. The relative separation between AT-split features is measured as a function of an applied rf field's power and detuning from atomic resonance. These peak separations are then fit to a simple, two-level model using a weighted nonlinear least-squares regression analysis that utilizes three fitting parameters, one of which is the desired calibration factor. While we do not benchmark this method against an absolute frequency reference, we do show that this method is (1) consistent with calibration via known fine-structure separation to within approximately 1.11% or better, and (2) robust against substantial experimental deviation from the system's known rf power dependence.

</details>


### [82] [Origin of the inhomogeneous linewidth in a solid state $^{229}$Th:CaF$_2$ nuclear clock](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04205)
*Kai Li,Tian Ooi,Jack F. Doyle,Konstantin Herb,Emil Pellett,Martin Pimon,Thorsten Schumm,Steven M. Girvin,Leo Radzihovsky,Jun Ye*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: The tens-to-hundreds-of-kHz linewidth observed in $^{229}$Th:CaF$_2$ nuclear-clock transitions arises from inhomogeneous broadening caused by local electric-field-gradient (EFG) disorder that scales linearly with thorium concentration, explained by $1/r^3$ long-range dopant perturbations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Solid-state $^{229}$Th nuclear clocks require narrow-linewidth (high-Q) quantum discriminators, but observed transition linewidths are at the tens-to-hundreds-of-kilohertz level, vastly exceeding the sub-Hertz radiative-lifetime limit. The origin of this excess broadening was unknown, hindering materials engineering for better nuclear clocks.

Method: Systematic measurements of the $^{229}$Th:CaF$_2$ transition linewidth as functions of temperature, thorium concentration, and quadrupole state, combined with theoretical analysis, first-principle simulations, and complementary atomistic and ab initio density-functional-theory calculations of defect configurations.

Result: The linewidth is temperature-independent, increases linearly with thorium concentration, and depends strongly on the quadrupole state. Local EFG variations dominate the inhomogeneous broadening, with EFG disorder increasing linearly with thorium concentration. The Lorentzian line shapes and linear concentration dependence are explained by the $1/r^3$ scaling of a random distribution of long-range dopant-induced perturbations; DFT and atomistic simulations reproduce these features and reveal the responsible microscopic defect configurations.

Conclusion: The dominant broadening mechanism in solid-state $^{229}$Th is identified as EFG-disorder-induced inhomogeneous broadening, providing guidance for engineering nuclear-clock materials with reduced inhomogeneous broadening.

Abstract: Narrow linewidth (high-Q) quantum discriminators are essential for realizing solid-state $^{229}$Th nuclear clocks, yet the observed transition linewidths remain at the tens-to-hundreds-of-kilohertz level, far exceeding the sub-Hertz linewidth set by the radiative lifetime of the nuclear isomer. Here, we investigate the origin of this broadening in $^{229}$Th:CaF$_2$ through systematic measurements of the linewidth as functions of temperature, thorium concentration, and quadrupole state, accompanied by theoretical analysis and first-principle simulations. We find that the linewidth is independent of temperature, increases linearly with thorium concentration, and depends strongly on the quadrupole state. Data analysis identifies variations of the local electric-field gradient (EFG) as the dominant source of inhomogeneous broadening and reveals that the EFG disorder increases linearly with thorium concentration. The observed Lorentzian line shapes and linewidth linear concentration dependence are explained naturally from the $1/r^3$ scaling of a random distribution of long-range dopant-induced perturbations. Complementary atomistic and \textit{ab initio} density-functional-theory calculations reproduce these characteristic features and provide insights into the microscopic defect configurations responsible for the broadening. These results establish the dominant broadening mechanism in solid-state $^{229}$Th and provide guidance for engineering nuclear-clock materials with reduced inhomogeneous broadening.

</details>


### [83] [A cryogenic apparatus with a rotatable substrate for matrix isolation spectroscopy of BaF in neon crystals down to 3.2 K](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05148)
*Alessandro Lippi,Diego Bisero,Caterina Braggio,Giovanni Carugno,Roberto Calabrese,Federico Chiossi,Marco Guarise,Madiha M. Makhdoom,Giuseppe Messineo,Jacopo Pazzini*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A new cryogenic matrix isolation spectroscopy apparatus is presented that grows neon and parahydrogen matrices doped with BaF, a candidate for electron EDM measurements, and characterizes its fluorescence behavior.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: BaF is a candidate for electron electric dipole moment (eEDM) measurements, and embedding such molecules in cryogenic crystal matrices may offer advantages for precision spectroscopy. A dedicated apparatus was needed to produce, dope, and spectroscopically investigate BaF in solid rare-gas and parahydrogen matrices at very low temperatures.

Method: BaF molecules are produced in a cryogenic buffer-gas source and used to dope a neon matrix grown on a substrate cooled by a cryocooler down to 3.2 K. A rotatable flange enables multiple substrate orientations to optimize spectroscopic investigations and matrix crystal growth. Fluorescence emission analysis is performed by exciting the A 2Π1/2, A 2Π3/2 and B 2Σ+ states, and BaF production is also demonstrated using parahydrogen as the buffer gas.

Result: Fluorescence emission from BaF in neon crystals is observed when exciting the A 2Π1/2, A 2Π3/2 and B 2Σ+ states, together with fluorescence from barium atoms in the crystal. The column density of BaF in the crystal is first estimated to be on the order of 5 × 10−13 cm−2. Fast fluorescence decay attributed to optical bleaching effects is also observed, with amplitude increasing at lower temperatures or higher laser intensities.

Conclusion: The apparatus successfully grows cryogenic neon matrices doped with BaF and enables fluorescence spectroscopic characterization. The demonstration of BaF production with parahydrogen buffer gas is a first step toward BaF embedded in a parahydrogen matrix. The observed optical bleaching introduces a systematic effect that must be studied in future experiments.

Abstract: We present a new experimental apparatus for matrix isolation spectroscopy capable of growing cryogenic crystal matrices doped with barium monofluoride (BaF), a candidate for measurements of the electron electric dipole moment (eEDM). \\ In this work, BaF molecules are produced in a cryogenic buffer-gas source and used to dope a neon matrix grown on a substrate, cooled by a cryocooler, reaching temperatures as low as 3.2 K. A rotatable flange allows for multiple substrate orientations, optimizing spectroscopic investigations and matrix crystal growth. \\ Fluorescence emission analysis of BaF in neon crystal show emission when exciting the $A\,^2Π_{1/2}$, $A\,^2Π_{3/2}$ and $B\,^2Σ^+$ states, along with the presence of fluorescence from barium atoms in the crystal. A first estimation of the column density of BaF in the crystal is given on the order of $5 \times 10^{-13} \, \text{cm}^{-2}$. \\ We also demonstrated the production of BaF using parahydrogen as the buffer gas of the cryogenic source, a first step toward BaF molecules embedded in a para-hydrogen matrix. \\ Finally, we also observed fast fluorescence decay, attributed to optical bleaching effects, whose amplitude arise at lower temperatures or higher laser intensities, introducing a systematic effect to be studied in future experiments.

</details>


### [84] [Onset of Melting in Finite Ion Crystals: The Role of Structural Isomerization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05624)
*Boris V. Pashinsky,Boris Blinov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Melting in finite 2D ion crystals is governed by structural isomer identity, not merely particle number and trap anisotropy; melting proceeds via gradual thermally-driven accumulation of radial/angular disorder rather than a simple soft-mode instability.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Prior work on melting probability of 2D ion crystals vs. temperature and anisotropy is extended to finite systems to understand how structural isomerization reshapes melting pathways and to clarify structural rearrangements' role in finite-size phase transitions.

Method: Molecular dynamics simulations combined with Metropolis-Hastings equilibrium sampling; analysis of radial fluctuations, angular disorder, Lindemann parameters, and isomer-dependent energy landscapes for N=4 to 101 ions in anisotropic traps.

Result: Crystals with identical N and trap anisotropy melt substantially differently depending on their metastable structural isomer. Melting is characterized by gradual thermal accumulation of radial and angular disorder, contradicting the soft-mode instability picture; isomerization reshapes fluctuation channels, yielding nonuniform melting probabilities across temperature-anisotropy space.

Conclusion: The study offers a unified microscopic picture of melting in finite ion crystals and clarifies the role of structural rearrangements in finite-size phase transitions.

Abstract: We theoretically investigate the microscopic mechanism of melting in finite two-dimensional ion crystals confined in anisotropic traps. Building on our previous studies of melting probability as a function of temperature and anisotropy, we extend the analysis to crystal sizes ranging from $N=4$ to $N=101$ ions and examine how structural isomerization modifies the melting pathways. Using molecular dynamics simulations together with Metropolis-Hastings equilibrium sampling, we analyze radial fluctuations, angular disorder, Lindemann parameters, and isomer-dependent energy landscapes in these Coulomb crystals. We find that crystals with identical particle number and trap anisotropy can exhibit substantially different melting behavior depending on the metastable structural isomer they occupy. Contrary to a simple soft-mode instability picture, the observed melting process is characterized by a gradual thermally-driven accumulation of radial and angular disorder. Structural isomerization reshapes the fluctuation channels through which delocalization develops, leading to nonuniform melting probabilities across temperature and anisotropy space. These results provide a unified microscopic picture of melting in finite ion crystals and clarify the role of structural rearrangements in finite-size phase transitions.

</details>


### [85] [Bifurcation of laser-dressed band extrema in solids under intense low-frequency fields](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05895)
*Alexander Flegel,Liang-Wen Pi,Liang-You Peng,Mikhail Frolov*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: An intense low-frequency laser field can bifurcate the local extremal structure of a crystal's interband energy gap: at a critical field, a single symmetric gap minimum splits into two symmetry-related minima separated by a local maximum, producing an extra van Hove feature in the interband susceptibility.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Strong (low-frequency) laser fields are known to modify electronic spectra in crystals, but how they alter the local extremal geometry of an interband gap is not well understood. The paper aims to uncover a general mechanism by which strong-field dressing transforms the gap's local topology.

Method: The authors use an instantaneous laser-dressed basis derived from nonperturbative diagonalization of the multiband crystal Hamiltonian. They construct a quartic normal form for the dressed gap near a high-symmetry point and decompose its quadratic part into a homogeneous Stark contribution and a field-modified band-curvature contribution. The Kronig–Penney model is used as a realization, checking multiple high-symmetry points and different interband gaps.

Result: At a critical field, the quadratic part of the normal form changes sign, causing the gap to bifurcate: a single symmetric minimum splits into two symmetry-related minima separated by a local maximum. This occurs at multiple high-symmetry points and for different interband gaps in the Kronig–Penney model. The field-induced change of gap extrema originates from the multiband redistribution of interband polarizations (band-curvature contribution). The bifurcation gives rise to an additional van Hove feature in the instantaneous interband susceptibility above the absorption edge.

Conclusion: The results establish a general mechanism by which strong-field dressing qualitatively modifies the local geometry of the electronic spectrum, with observable spectral signatures in the interband susceptibility.

Abstract: We show that an intense low-frequency laser field can qualitatively modify the local extremal structure of an interband energy gap in a crystal. Using an instantaneous laser-dressed basis obtained from the nonperturbative diagonalization of the multiband crystal Hamiltonian, we derive a quartic normal form for the dressed gap near a high-symmetry point. At a critical field, the quadratic part of this normal form changes sign, and the gap experiences a bifurcation: a single symmetric minimum splits into two symmetry-related minima separated by a local maximum. We identify the microscopic origin of this bifurcation by decomposing the quadratic part into a homogeneous Stark contribution and a field-modified band-curvature contribution. The latter originates from the multiband redistribution of interband polarizations and provides the mechanism for the field-induced change of the gap extrema. A realization for the Kronig--Penney model demonstrates the same bifurcation at multiple high-symmetry points and for different interband gaps. As an illustration of its spectral consequences, we show that the bifurcation gives rise to an additional van Hove feature in the instantaneous interband susceptibility above the absorption edge. Our results establish a general mechanism by which strong-field dressing modifies the local geometry of the electronic spectrum.

</details>


### [86] [Sub-Doppler cooling in a compact conical hollow mirror integrated with anti-Helmholtz coils](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05983)
*Shintaro Nagase,Naoya Ozawa,Teruhito Nakashita,Masaki Nakazawa,Kota Abe,Yu Nezu,Ryosuke Tsutsui,Hideki Ueno,Hiromitsu Haba,Yasuhiro Sakemi*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: A compact single-beam conical MOT built from a conical hollow mirror with anti-Helmholtz coils traps ~1e7 87Rb atoms at low optical power and achieves sub-Doppler, anisotropic temperatures via polarization gradient cooling.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional six-beam magneto-optical traps require multiple beams and higher laser power, which limits the miniaturization and power efficiency of cold-atom sources for integrated quantum sensors. A single-beam, power-efficient geometry is therefore desirable.

Method: A conical hollow mirror integrated with anti-Helmholtz coils converts one incident beam into an effective six-beam MOT and focuses light toward the cone axis. The authors characterized atom number versus incident intensity, then applied polarization gradient cooling to measure radial (Tr) and axial (Tz) temperatures, and performed a modified release-and-recapture measurement to extract a radial temperature in the MOT.

Result: Optimal trapping occurs at 6.5 mW/cm2, several times lower than conventional six-beam MOTs, yielding ~1e7 87Rb atoms. PGC gives Tr = 13.4 +/- 1.2 uK and Tz = 106 +/- 11 uK, showing strong anisotropy attributed to the conical mirror's polarization pattern. Release-and-recapture yields Tr = 1.20 +/- 0.02 mK, about five times higher than expected for comparable light-shift parameters in a six-beam MOT, matching prior simulations.

Conclusion: Conical hollow mirrors provide a practical, power-efficient platform for compact cold-atom sources and integrated quantum sensors, though the geometry introduces anisotropic polarization effects and elevated radial temperature.

Abstract: We demonstrate sub-Doppler cooling of rubidium ($^{87}$Rb) atoms using a compact, single-beam configuration based on a conical hollow mirror integrated with anti-Helmholtz coils. The conical geometry converts a single incident beam into an effective six-beam magneto-optical trap (MOT) configuration while focusing the light towards the cone axis, thereby reducing the required laser power. The number of atoms in the conical MOT is maximized at an incident intensity of 6.5 $\mathrm{mW/cm^2}$ in our conical MOT, several times lower than in conventional six-beam MOTs. Under optimal conditions, we trap $\sim 1 \times 10^7$ $^{87}$Rb atoms. We obtain sub-Doppler temperature of $T_r=13.4\pm 1.2\ \mathrm{μK}$ and $T_z=106\pm 11\ \mathrm{μK}$ by polarization gradient cooling (PGC), revealing a strong anisotropy that we attribute to the polarization pattern created by the conical mirror. In addition, a modified release-and-recapture measurement in the MOT yields a radial temperature $T_r = 1.20\pm 0.02$ mK, approximately five times higher than that expected for comparable light-shift parameters in a six-beam MOT, in agreement with previous simulations. Our results establish conical hollow mirrors as a practical and power-efficient platform for compact cold-atom sources and integrated quantum sensors.

</details>


### [87] [The influence of the binding energy on Compton-scattering-driven ionization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06242)
*N. Melzer,M. Kircher,G. Kastirke,J. Rist,D. Tsitsonis,A. Pier,L. Kaiser,J. Kruse,N. Anders,J. Stindl,L. Sommerlad,O. D. McGinnis,M. Schmidt,L. Nowak,A. Kügler,I. Dwojak,P. Daum,P. Roth,M. Astaschov,J. Drnec,K. Lin,N. Wong,F. Trinter,M. S. Schöffler,L. Ph. H. Schmidt,D. M. Haubenreißer,N. M. Novikovskiy,N. Diehl,Ph. V. Demekhin,S. Brennecke,M. Lein,S. Eckart,T. Jahnke,R. Dörner*

Main category: physics.atom-ph

TL;DR: Compton scattering of 20 keV photons by bound electrons is measured fully differentially across a wide range of binding energies, revealing strong deviations from the quasi-free electron approximation due to ionic-potential scattering of escaping electrons.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quasi-free electron approximation is commonly used to describe Compton scattering, but its validity for bound electrons across different binding energies and orbitals is not well established. The authors aim to test this approximation by measuring fully differential cross sections.

Method: Fully differential cross sections were measured for Compton scattering of 20 keV photons by electrons bound in Ne 2p, Ne 2s, C 1s, O 1s, and Ne 1s orbitals. Specifically, electron angular distributions were recorded with respect to the photon momentum transfer direction at fixed electron energy.

Result: The data show strong deviations from the quasi-free electron approximation predictions. Both the binding energy and the shape of the Coulomb potential from which the electron escapes significantly influence the electron kinematics. Compton electrons are found to be scattered and even back-reflected by the ionic potential.

Conclusion: The quasi-free electron approximation fails to describe Compton scattering of bound electrons. The ionic potential plays a key role in modifying electron kinematics, and the magnitude of this effect depends on the orbital from which the electron is ejected.

Abstract: We present a comprehensive study of Compton scattering of 20 keV photons by bound electrons over a wide range of binding energies (Ne 2p, Ne 2s, C 1s, O 1s, and Ne 1s orbitals). In particular, we have measured fully differential cross sections, i.e., electron angular distributions with respect to the direction of the photon momentum transfer for fixed electron energy. Our data show strong deviations from the predictions of the ''quasi-free'' electron approximation. The binding energy and the shape of the Coulomb potential from which the electron escapes both play a significant role in its kinematics. We find that Compton electrons are scattered and even back-reflected by the ionic potential. The strength of this effect depends on the orbital from which the electron is ejected.

</details>

### [88] [The ALPS project release 3.0: open source software for strongly correlated systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03884)
*F. Alet,T. Chen,A. Feiguin,E. Gull,S. Iskakov,J. P. F. LeBlanc,F. Lin,A. Mirmira,G. Möller,L. Pollet,M. Rosales,V. W. Scarola,H. Shinaoka,H. Terletska,S. Todo,M. Troyer,M. Wallerberger,P. Werner,T. M. R. Wolf*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Release 3.0 of the ALPS open-source project brings modernized C++ code, a Python package on PyPI, GitHub-based development with CI, MIT licensing, and major DMRG and documentation updates, while removing legacy components.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To sustain and modernize ALPS, a long-standing open-source framework for simulating strongly correlated quantum lattice models, making it easier for both developers and non-experts to build, install, and run simulations through updated tooling, licensing, and governance.

Method: Centers on common data formats, supporting libraries, and full-featured simulation programs implementing key algorithms: classical and quantum Monte Carlo with non-local updates, extended-ensemble simulations, exact and full diagonalization, DMRG, and continuous-time QMC solvers for DMFT. Release 3.0 adds pyalps distribution via pip and Spack, GitHub migration with continuous integration and automated testing, relicensing to MIT, rebuilt documentation and Jupyter-notebook tutorials, C++17/Boost/NumPy 2.0 modernization with a major DMRG update, removal of legacy components, Zenodo DOI archival, and a governance and sustainability model under the NSF POSE program.

Result: A fully modernized, openly licensed (MIT) ALPS 3.0 is distributed through PyPI and Spack, developed on GitHub with automated testing, documented with tutorials, archived with a Zenodo DOI, and governed under a formal sustainability model.

Conclusion: ALPS 3.0 consolidates modernization, improved accessibility, and sustainable governance for a mature open-source simulation ecosystem for strongly correlated quantum lattice models. The software is available at https://alps.comp-phys.org.

Abstract: We present release 3.0 of the ALPS (Algorithms and Libraries for Physics Simulations) project, an open-source software project to develop libraries and application programs for the simulation of strongly correlated quantum lattice models such as quantum magnets, lattice bosons, and strongly correlated fermion systems. As in previous releases, development is centered on common data formats, on libraries to simplify and speed up code development, and on full-featured simulation programs that let non-experts carry out serial or parallel numerical simulations using the important algorithms for quantum lattice models: classical and quantum Monte Carlo (QMC) using non-local updates, extended-ensemble simulations, exact and full diagonalization (ED), the density matrix renormalization group (DMRG), and continuous-time QMC solvers for dynamical mean-field theory (DMFT). Major changes in release 3.0 include distribution of the pyalps binary through the Python Package Index (pip install pyalps) and through Spack for HPC systems; migration of development to GitHub with continuous integration and automated testing; relicensing of the package under the permissive MIT license; a rebuilt documentation and tutorial website, including a set of Jupyter-notebook tutorials and localized content; a broad modernization of the C++ codebase (C++17 compliance, Boost and NumPy 2.0 compatibility, and warning and dead-code cleanup) together with a major DMRG update and associated reliability and build-compatibility fixes; the removal of legacy components (the VisTrails provenance integration and the TEBD, MPS, and directed-worm-algorithm application codes); archival of release 3.0.0 with a Zenodo DOI (10.5281/zenodo.22775899); and a formal governance and sustainability model developed under the US National Science Foundation (NSF) POSE program. The software is available at https://alps.comp-phys.org.

</details>


### [89] [ToF-PAX-RIXS: A Time-of-Flight Spectrometer for combined ARPES and RIXS](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03993)
*Chafic Fawaz,Tom Lacmannn,Yaryna Lytvynenko,Sergii Chernov,Olena Tkach,Siegmar Roth,Jan O. Schunck,Amon Lanz,Jakob Dilling,Lukas Bruckmeier,Markus Scholz,Frank Scholz,Arne Meyer,Dirk Fuchs,Lara Schmieder,Yiran Liu,Matteo Minola,Bernhard Keimer,Dmytro Kutnyakhov,Kai Rossnagel,Olena Fedchenko,Angela Wittmann,Moritz Hoesch,Gerd Schönhense,Matthieu Le Tacon,Hans-Joachim Elmers,Martin Beye*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: ToF-PAX-RIXS is a compact instrument merging time-of-flight momentum-microscopy ARPES with PAX-based resonant inelastic X-ray scattering in a single multimode electron-optical column, enabling sequential same-sample measurements; benchmarked on CaCuO2 at the Cu L3 edge with sub-120 meV effective resolution and demonstrated via combined ARPES/RIXS on Ca-doped YBCO.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: ARPES and RIXS provide complementary information on electronic structure and collective excitations, but are conventionally performed on separate, large-scale instruments under different conditions, complicating direct correlation of quasiparticle bands with bosonic excitations on the same sample.

Method: The instrument integrates time-of-flight momentum-microscopy ARPES and PAX-RIXS using one multimode electron-optical column. In ARPES mode it records photoemission intensity versus kinetic energy and two in-plane momentum components. In PAX-RIXS mode, scattered soft X-ray photons are absorbed in a thin metallic converter; emitted photoelectrons are analyzed in energy and converter emission position, the latter encoding scattering angle and thus momentum transfer, giving simultaneous access to a finite momentum-transfer interval. Mechanical design, converter assembly, scattering geometry, and multimode electron optics are described. Performance is benchmarked at the Cu L3 edge on CaCuO2 with Au, Ag, and Pt converters, with deconvolution applied to the spectra.

Result: PAX-RIXS spectra reproduce the principal features of a conventional grating RIXS spectrometer, and deconvolution yields an effective reconstructed energy resolution better than 120 meV under present conditions. Combined ARPES and PAX-RIXS measurements on the same Ca-doped YBa2Cu3O6.45 sample confirm the ability to correlate electronic structure and collective excitations within one compact platform.

Conclusion: ToF-PAX-RIXS offers a compact, multimode platform for sequential ARPES and RIXS on identical samples, achieving competitive effective resolution and enabling correlated studies of electronic structure and collective excitations.

Abstract: We present ToF-PAX-RIXS, a compact instrument that combines time-of-flight momentum-microscopy angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (ARPES) with resonant inelastic X-ray scattering (RIXS). The latter is based on photoelectron spectrometry for analysis of X-rays (PAX). The two techniques are implemented with a single multimode electron-optical column, allowing measurements to be performed sequentially on the same sample under identical experimental conditions. In ARPES mode, the instrument records the photoemission intensity as a function of kinetic energy and two in-plane momentum components. In PAX-RIXS mode, scattered soft X-ray photons are absorbed in a thin metallic converter, and the generated photoelectrons are analyzed in energy and position of emission on the converter. The electron kinetic energy retains the spectral information of the scattered photons, while the position is related to the scattering angle and therefore to the momentum transfer, enabling simultaneous access to a finite momentum-transfer interval. We describe the mechanical design, converter assembly, scattering geometry, and multimode electron optics of the instrument. Its PAX-RIXS performance is benchmarked at the Cu $L_3$ edge using CaCuO$_2$, with Au, Ag, and Pt converters. The measured spectra reproduce the principal features obtained with a conventional grating RIXS spectrometer, and deconvolution yields an effective reconstructed energy resolution better than $120$~meV under the present conditions. Finally, combined ARPES and PAX-RIXS measurements on the same Ca-doped YBa$_2$Cu$_3$O$_{6.45}$ sample demonstrate the capability of the instrument to correlate electronic structure and collective excitations within a common compact platform.

</details>


### [90] [Magneto-transport and electronic structure studies of ternary antimonides, La$T$Sb$_2$ ($T$ = Cu, Ag)](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04290)
*Himanshu Pant,Neeraj Bhatt,Deepali Sharma,Asif Ali,A. Thamizhavel,Saurabh Singh,Kentaro Kuga,Tsunehiro Takeuchi,Ravi Shankar Singh,R. Bindu*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Magnetotransport and photoemission study of Sb square net-based Dirac semimetals LaTSb2 (T = Cu, Ag) reveals non-Fermi-liquid behavior in both, but CDW signatures and anomalous low-T core-level broadening only for T = Ag, linking Sb square nets to CDW formation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to clarify the role of local electronic environments and inequivalent Sb sites in CDW formation, and to benchmark spectroscopic differences between CDW and non-CDW systems in related Dirac semimetallic ternary antimonides.

Method: Magnetotransport measurements, temperature-dependent photoelectron spectroscopy (valence band, Sb 3d/4d core-levels, La 3d, UPS) and DFT+U ab initio calculations including SOC.

Result: Both compounds show positive temperature coefficient of resistivity, linear MR, and deviation from Fermi liquid behavior at 2-30 K. Only T = Ag exhibits CDW phenomena and temperature-dependent MR indicating single-band semiclassical transport is insufficient. DFT+U with SOC is needed to reproduce valence band spectra. Sb square nets are linked to CDW in T = Ag, which shows anomalous low-temperature core-level broadening and a small DOS decrement at εF below the CDW transition temperature.

Conclusion: The core-level studies provide a spectroscopic benchmark for comparing CDW and non-CDW systems and clarify the role of local electronic environments in CDW formation, with Sb square nets linked to CDW phenomena in LaAgSb2.

Abstract: We investigate Sb square net-based Dirac semimetallic ternary antimonides La$T$Sb$_2$ ($T$ = Cu, Ag) using magnetotransport and photoelectron spectroscopic studies, complemented by density functional theory (DFT) based ab initio calculations. Our results show that both compounds exhibit a positive temperature coefficient of resistivity, linear magnetoresistance (MR), and a deviation from Fermi liquid behaviour at low temperatures (2-30 K). But only $T$ = Ag compound exhibit signature of charge density wave (CDW) phenomena and the results of its temperature dependent MR data suggest that single-band semiclassical transport is not sufficient to understand the transport behaviour. $\text{DFT}+U$ studies show that in both samples, an on-site Coulomb interaction ($U$) for Cu 3$d$ and Ag 4$d$ electrons, in the presence of spin-orbit coupling (SOC) is required to reproduce experimental valence band spectra. Sb 3$d$, 4$d$ core-level spectra combined with $\text{DFT}+U$ calculations and La 3$d$ spectra confirm the presence and effects of inequivalent Sb sites for both the samples. In contrast to the $T$ = Cu compound, which follows expected temperature-dependent core-level sharpening, the $T$ = Ag compound exhibits an anomalous low-temperature broadening in the CDW phase. The analysis of Sb 3$d$ spectra suggests Sb square nets are linked to CDW phenomena in the $T$ = Ag compound. Temperature-dependent ultraviolet photoemission spectroscopic studies on $T$ = Ag show a small decrement in spectral DOS at the $ε_F$ for the temperature below the CDW transition temperature. These core-level studies provide a valuable spectroscopic benchmark for comparing CDW and non-CDW systems and clarifying the role of local electronic environments in CDW formation.

</details>


### [91] [State-selective entanglement and point-gap topology in the unidirectional Bose-Hubbard chain](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04331)
*Chuan-Fu Lin,Yu-Chin Tzeng,Po-Yao Chang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Repulsive interactions suppress skin accumulation in the unidirectional Bose-Hubbard model's ground state, but entanglement changes are state-selective: the ground state becomes uniform with decreasing entanglement, while steady states show a nonmonotonic entanglement crossover explained by competing subsystem-number sectors and interacting point-gap topology.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to clarify how repulsive interactions affect skin modes and entanglement in the unidirectional Bose-Hubbard model, distinguishing ground-state skin suppression from state-selective entanglement behavior and point-gap topology.

Method: The authors compare the ground state with the steady state (largest-imaginary-energy eigenstate in the zero-momentum sector) for sixteen sites at unit filling. They analyze density profiles, second Renyi entropy, subsystem-number sectors, dominant doublon-holon manifolds, and many-body winding, including an exact two-site example of the singular projected U/t→∞ limit.

Result: The ground-state density approaches uniform filling and its entanglement decreases toward the Mott limit, whereas steady states exhibit a nonmonotonic entanglement crossover. At U/t=10 the steady-state second Renyi entropy develops four spatial maxima, accounted for by competing subsystem-number sectors within a dominant four-doublon-four-holon manifold rather than by subsystem particle-number variance alone. At strong interaction, projection onto the one-doublon-one-holon manifold reproduces the full entropy profile and its two edge peaks. Many-body winding about the origin remains nonzero at strong interaction.

Conclusion: Ground-state skin suppression is distinct from state-selective entanglement and point-gap topology; entanglement depends on the selected many-body state, and the many-body winding remains nonzero at strong interaction though it becomes ill-defined in the singular projected U/t→∞ limit.

Abstract: We study the effect of repulsive interactions on skin modes and entanglement in the unidirectional Bose--Hubbard model. Although the ground state of this integrable model undergoes a superfluid--Mott transition with suppressed skin accumulation, the corresponding change in entanglement depends on the selected many-body state. We compare the ground state with the steady state, defined as the largest-imaginary-energy eigenstate in the zero-momentum sector. For sixteen sites at unit filling, we find that the ground-state density approaches uniform filling and its entanglement decreases toward the Mott limit, whereas the steady states exhibit a nonmonotonic entanglement crossover. At intermediate interaction ($U/t=10$), the second Rényi entropy of the steady state develops four spatial maxima. We show that these maxima are accounted for by competing subsystem-number sectors within a dominant configuration manifold of four doublons and four holons, but not by the subsystem particle-number variance alone. The dominant global onsite-pair manifold retains the same entropy peak structure. At strong interaction, projection onto the one-doublon--one-holon manifold closely reproduces the full entropy profile and its two edge peaks. Furthermore, we demonstrate that the many-body winding about the origin remains nonzero at strong interaction; an exact two-site example shows how it becomes ill-defined in the singular projected $U/t\to\infty$ limit. Our results distinguish ground-state skin suppression from state-selective entanglement and point-gap topology.

</details>


### [92] [Determining the j-Specific Unoccupied 5f Electronic Structure in Actinides](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04654)
*J. G. Tobin*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: High-resolution M4,5 XAS via HERFD is empirically compared with BIS/IPES for various actinide materials, showing that XAS-HERFD is essentially the j-specific counterpart of BIS, directly measuring separate 5f5/2 and 5f7/2 unoccupied density of states.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To establish the relationship between two high-energy spectroscopic techniques for probing the unoccupied electronic structure of actinide materials.

Method: Empirical comparison of high-resolution M4,5 X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy based on High Energy Resolution Fluorescence Detection (XAS-HERFD) with Bremsstrahlung Isochromat Spectroscopy (BIS), the high-energy variant of Inverse Photoelectron Spectroscopy (IPES), applied to various actinide materials.

Result: XAS-HERFD is essentially the j-specific version of BIS, providing a direct measure of the separate 5f5/2 and 5f7/2 Unoccupied Density of States (UDOS).

Conclusion: XAS-HERFD and BIS are closely related techniques, with XAS-HERFD offering j-resolved access to the 5f unoccupied states of actinide materials.

Abstract: An empirical comparison of high resolution M4,5 X-ray Absorption Spectroscopy (XAS), based upon High Energy Resolution Fluorescence Detection (HERFD), with Bremsstrahlung Isochromat Spectroscopy (BIS), the high energy variant of Inverse Photoelectron Spectroscopy (IPES), of various actinide materials is presented here. It will be shown that XAS-HERFD is essentially the j-specific version of BIS, providing a direct measure of the separate 5f5/2 and 5f7/2 Unoccupied Density of States (UDOS).

</details>


### [93] [Does a pole in the self-energy imply a violation of the Luttinger theorem?](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04663)
*Artem Abanov,Andrey Chubukov*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Luttinger's theorem, which links Fermi surface volume to electron density, is shown to survive a pole in the fermionic self-energy in certain cases. Using a two-gapped-band model with a narrow residual quasiparticle band (residue Z << 1) whose Green's function has two zeros and three poles, the authors find that Luttinger-Ward analysis remains fully applicable for any Z > 0 in Model I (where Z renormalizes bandwidth to ZW) and recovers beyond a density-dependent critical Z in Model II (unrenormalized W).


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Luttinger theorem is believed to break when a fermionic self-energy has a pole, a common situation in strongly correlated systems. The authors challenge this belief by examining whether the theorem can still hold despite such a pole.

Method: They study a fermionic system with two gapped bands arising from a self-energy Σ(ω,k) with a pole, plus a narrow residual quasiparticle band crossing ω=0 with small residue Z << 1 (a residual Fermi liquid). They analyze two models: Model I, where Z renormalizes the quasiparticle bandwidth W to ZW, and Model II, where W is unrenormalized. They apply Luttinger-Ward analysis to the Green's function G(ω,k), which contains two zeros and three poles (one from the quasiparticle band, two from gapped bands).

Result: In Model I, the Luttinger-Ward analysis is fully applicable for any Z > 0, yielding results identical to those without the gapped bands. In Model II, the Luttinger-Ward analysis recovers once Z exceeds a critical value that depends on the fermionic density.

Conclusion: A pole in the self-energy does not necessarily invalidate Luttinger's theorem. Whether the theorem holds depends on how the residue Z affects the quasiparticle bandwidth: for the bandwidth-renormalized case it holds for any Z, while for the unrenormalized case it requires Z above a density-dependent threshold.

Abstract: Luttinger theorem relates Fermi surface volume to electron density per spin $n$. It holds in an arbitrary Fermi liquid but is widely believed to break when a fermionic self-energy has a pole.
  We show that this is not necessarily the case.
  As an example, we consider a fermionic system consisting of two gapped bands, originating from a self-energy $Σ(ω, \mathbf{k})$ with a pole,
  and a narrow residual quasiparticle band crossing $ω=0$, with a small residue $Z \ll 1$
  (a residual Fermi liquid). We consider Model I, in which $Z$ also renormalizes quasiparticle bandwidth $W$ to $ZW$ and Model II, in which $W$ remains unrenormalized.
  The Green's function for such a system $G(ω, \mathbf{k})$ contains two zeros and three poles --- one from the quasiparticle band and two from the gapped bands.
  Yet, we show that in Model I, the Luttinger-Ward analysis is fully applicable
  for any $Z >0$,
  and the results are exactly the same as if the gapped bands did not exist. For Model II, the Luttinger-Ward analysis recovers once $Z$ exceeds some critical value, which depends on fermionic density.

</details>


### [94] [Peeling off Abelian Anyons and Proliferation Transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04704)
*Meng Cheng,Nathan Seiberg*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: This paper studies the invertible one-form global symmetry of a (2+1)-dimensional TQFT, showing it universally "peels off" Abelian anyons from the rest of the theory, and constructs a "transition field theory" by coupling these anyons to a scalar field to describe a phase transition to a gapless or another TQFT phase.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The motivation is to study the invertible one-form global symmetry and its consequences independently of the details of the rest of the TQFT, generalizing and extending the authors' earlier work (arXiv:2603.00245) with a more detailed description of global symmetries and their fates in various phases.

Method: The method involves a universal description that peels off all Abelian anyons from the rest of the TQFT, describing these Abelian anyons using U(1) gauge fields with Chern-Simons terms, and coupling them to a scalar field to construct a non-topological "transition field theory."

Result: The transition field theory exhibits a phase transition from the original TQFT to another phase, which is either gapless or another TQFT. A more detailed description of the global symmetries and their fates in the various phases is provided.

Conclusion: The discussion generalizes and extends the earlier work, offering a more detailed account of the global symmetries and their fates across phases, with the invertible one-form symmetry universally peeling off Abelian anyons to enable analysis independent of the rest of the system.

Abstract: We study the invertible one-form global symmetry of a (2+1)-dimensional topological quantum field theory (TQFT). A universal description of this symmetry ``peels off'' all the Abelian anyons from the rest of the TQFT. This allows us to study this symmetry and some of its consequences, independently of the details of the rest of the system. We then describe these Abelian anyons using $\mathrm{U}(1)$ gauge fields with Chern-Simons terms and couple them to a scalar field, thus constructing a particular non-topological theory. This new theory, our transition field theory, exhibits a phase transition from the original TQFT to another phase. The latter is either gapless or another TQFT. This discussion generalizes and extends our earlier work (arXiv:2603.00245). In particular, we give a more detailed description of the global symmetries and their fates in the various phases.

</details>


### [95] [Floquet dressing of bulk metallic bands in 2H-NbSe2](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04958)
*Cong Li,Qirui Cui,Maciej Dendzik,Magnus H. Berntsen,Wanyu Chen,Zhilin Li,Anna Delin,Wentao Zhang,Oscar Tjernberg*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The authors use time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy to observe Floquet dressing of the overlapping bulk metallic bands of 2H-NbSe2, showing photon-shifted replicas whose weight is polarization-controlled and captured by coherent Floquet-Volkov interference.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Floquet dressing enables optical control of electronic states, but its realization in solids has been limited to topological-insulator surface states, semiconductors, and 2D Dirac semimetals. Extending it to bulk metals would allow control of itinerant states governing metallic transport and collective order, yet strong screening, rapid scattering, and photoelectron dressing have made this seem infeasible or hard to identify.

Method: Time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy (tr-ARPES) on 2H-NbSe2, with pump linear polarization rotated at fixed probe polarization. A model accounting for the optical fields inside and outside the solid, using a common parameter set, describes the polarization-dependent redistribution via coherent Floquet-Volkov interference, compared against either pathway alone or their incoherent intensity sum.

Result: Photon-shifted replicas appear in a sparse unoccupied-state window, retain parent-band dispersions, and exist only during pump-probe overlap. Rotating pump linear polarization redistributes replica weight between momentum-space branches. Coherent Floquet-Volkov interference captures this redistribution more closely than either pathway alone or their incoherent sum.

Conclusion: Floquet dressing is extended to the itinerant bulk bands of a multiband metal, without relying on energy gaps from hybridization between parent bands and photon-dressed replicas, providing a basis for exploring how coherent optical dressing couples to metallic transport and collective electronic phases.

Abstract: Floquet dressing offers a route to controlling electronic states through periodic optical driving. Its realization in solids has centred primarily on topological-insulator surface states, semiconductors and two-dimensional Dirac semimetals. Extending this control to bulk metals would enable manipulation of the itinerant states underlying metallic transport and collective order. Yet strong screening and rapid scattering have long cast doubt on its feasibility in bulk metals, while photoelectron dressing complicates its identification. Here we identify Floquet dressing of the overlapping, bulk-derived metallic bands of 2H-NbSe2 using time- and angle-resolved photoemission spectroscopy. Photon-shifted replicas in a sparse unoccupied-state window retain the parent-band dispersions and appear only during pump--probe overlap. Rotating the pump's linear polarization at fixed probe polarization redistributes replica weight between momentum-space branches. With a common parameter set, a model accounting for the optical fields inside and outside the solid captures this redistribution through coherent Floquet--Volkov interference more closely than either pathway alone or their incoherent intensity sum. These findings extend Floquet dressing to the itinerant bulk bands of a multiband metal without relying on the observation of energy gaps opened by hybridization between parent bands and their photon-dressed replicas, providing a basis for exploring how coherent optical dressing couples to metallic transport and collective electronic phases.

</details>


### [96] [Unveiling High-Energy Ultrafast Responses Driven by Fermi-Level Gap Opening in Charge-Density Waves](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05175)
*Qiong Wu,Tianchen Hu,Junhan Huang,Yongjie Liu,Fei Sun,Shuxiang Xu,Li Yue,Zijian Lin,Xinyu Zhou,Tao Dong,Nan-Lin Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy on the CDW material EuAl4 shows that optical pumping substantially shrinks the CDW gap, and this band renormalization modulates interband transitions tied to the gap edge, producing a pronounced transient reflectivity change.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: It remains elusive how a small gap opening near the Fermi surface affects spectral changes in the high-energy interband transition region in superconductors and correlated electron systems.

Method: Systematic studies of carrier relaxation dynamics in the prototype charge-density-wave (CDW) material EuAl4 using ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy.

Result: The CDW gap shrinks substantially under optical pumping; this band renormalization strongly modulates interband transitions associated with the gap edge, inducing a pronounced transient reflectivity change at the corresponding probe energies.

Conclusion: The results establish a direct link between low-energy order-parameter dynamics and high-energy spectral responses, providing a general framework for interpreting transient signals in gapped quantum materials.

Abstract: Ultrafast pump-probe spectroscopy is indispensable for investigating interactions, collective excitations, and nonequilibrium dynamics in superconductors and correlated electron systems. However, how a small gap opening near Fermi surface affects the spectral changes in the high-energy interband transition region remains elusive. To address this issue, we perform systematic studies of carrier relaxation dynamics in the prototype charge-density-wave (CDW) material EuAl4. We find that the CDW gap shrinks substantially under optical pumping. This band renormalization strongly modulates interband transitions associated with the gap edge, inducing a pronounced transient reflectivity change at the corresponding probe energies. These results establish a direct link between low-energy order-parameter dynamics and high-energy spectral responses, providing a general framework for interpreting transient signals in gapped quantum materials.

</details>


### [97] [Complete Entanglement Structure of the Kitaev Honeycomb Spin Liquid from Exact Tensor Networks](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05395)
*Hidehiro Saito,Chisa Hotta*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: An exact 2D tensor network representation of the Kitaev honeycomb model, built from dimension-D=2 local matrix-product operators, reveals that topological entanglement entropy appears in all eigenstates only after Gutzwiller projection, showing that excited states are also topologically ordered.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Most Kitaev honeycomb model features are understood via the combined Z2 plaquette and Majorana fermion picture, but the exact quantum many-body form is needed to resolve remaining questions about its spin liquid property, particularly regarding entanglement structure and the topological order of excited states.

Method: The authors construct an exact two-dimensional tensor network description of the full set of solutions from a random product state, using sequential quantum-number projection and Gutzwiller projection, both expressed as local matrix-product operators of bond dimension D=2.

Result: Ground and excited states share the same bond dimension but show distinct Schmidt spectra, distinguishing area-law from volume-law entanglement.
