Topological entanglement entropy emerges for all eigenstates only after Gutzwiller projection onto the physical spin Hilbert space, where the gauge constraint removes exactly half of the Schmidt states. A projection-induced collapse of the wave function into a single gauge sector in the Parton mean-field states is also shown.

Conclusion: Excited states are likewise topologically ordered. Demonstrations on the S=1/2 kagome and triangular Heisenberg models suggest the perspective applies to a wider class of models hosting Z2 spin liquids, visualizing a bulk-boundary correspondence with Wilson-loop characterization of topological order.

Abstract: While most of the features of the Kitaev honeycomb model are already clarified from the combined ${\mathbb Z}_2$ plaquette and Majorana fermion picture, we show that retrieving the exact quantum many-body form gives a clue to fix the remaining issues on its spin liquid property. We provide an exact two-dimensional tensor network description of a full set of solutions from a random product state, by sequential quantum-number projection and Gutzwiller projection, both expressed as local matrix-product operators of dimension $D=2$. The ground and excited states share the same bond dimension yet exhibit distinct Schmidt spectra, distinguishing their area-law and volume-law entanglement. We demonstrate that the topological entanglement entropy emerges {\it for all eigenstates only after} the Gutzwiller projection onto the physical spin Hilbert space, where the gauge constraint removes exactly half of the Schmidt states. This establishes that excited states are likewise topologically ordered. We further show that there exists a projection-induced collapse of the wave function into a single gauge sector in the Parton mean-field states that is known to give a good description of the spin liquids. The demonstrations on the $S=1/2$ kagome and triangular Heisenberg models suggest that the present perspective is applied to a wider class of models hosting ${\mathbb Z}_2$ spin liquids. Our finding visualizes a bulk-boundary correspondence with the Wilson-loop characterization of topological order.

</details>


### [98] [Nonthermal nonlinear many-body excitonic dynamics in high-purity silicon](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06016)
*Patrick Pilch,Siyu Duan,Yang Liu,Manfred Bayer,Marc Assmann,Zhe Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Time-resolved optical pump–terahertz third-harmonic generation spectroscopy reveals transient excitonic states forming on picosecond timescales in high-purity silicon at room temperature, despite excitons being thermally unstable there in equilibrium.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: In thermal equilibrium, excitons in silicon are unstable because their binding energy is lower than the thermal energy at room temperature. The work probes whether nonthermal, photoexcited many-body dynamics can nonetheless produce transient excitonic states in this indirect-gap semiconductor.

Method: Time-resolved optical pump–terahertz third-harmonic generation (THG) spectroscopy with high resolution, applied to high-purity silicon at room temperature; non-resonant pulsed optical pumping creates nonthermal carriers probed via nonlinear terahertz response on picosecond timescales.

Result: Transient excitonic states are identified on the picosecond time scale in the nonlinear spectroscopic measurements. These nonthermal many-body excitonic dynamics exhibit a characteristic time-dependent evolution, a distinct dependence on charge density, and a nonperturbative nonlinear terahertz response, distinguishing them from unbound electron-hole pairs.

Conclusion: Nonthermal many-body excitonic states can form transiently in silicon at room temperature despite their equilibrium instability, and their signature is accessible through nonlinear terahertz spectroscopy, offering a route to study nonequilibrium excitonic physics.

Abstract: We report time-resolved optical pump-terahertz third-harmonic generation spectroscopy of picosecond nonthermal many-body charge dynamics in high-purity silicon at room temperature. Although in thermal equilibrium excitons in silicon are unstable due to a lower binding energy than the thermal energy at room temperature, following pulsed non-resonant optical pumping we can identify the formation of transient excitonic states on the picosecond time scale in our high-resolution nonlinear spectroscopic measurements. In contrast to unbound electron-hole pairs, the nonthermal many-body excitonic dynamics feature a characteristic time-dependent evolution, a distinct dependence on charge density, and a nonperturbative nonlinear terahertz response.

</details>


### [99] [Lifshitz transition and magnetic superconductivity in flat band systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06140)
*Sudeshna Pal,K. B. Yogendra,Madhuparna Karmakar,Rajesh Narayanan*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: The paper studies Lifshitz-type transitions in a two-dimensional Lieb-lattice attractive Hubbard–Kondo superconductor, identifying two inequivalent transitions—one driven by chemical potential in the decoupled case and one involving a dimensionality drop of the minimum-energy manifold at finite Kondo coupling, with only the spiral phase producing a gapless state.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Lifshitz transitions are normally discussed for metallic Fermi surfaces, but their superconducting analogue—reconstruction of the minimum-quasiparticle-energy manifold—is less explored, especially in multiband systems with flat bands. The work aims to characterize such transitions in a magnetic superconductor where Kondo and pairing channels compete.

Method: Real-space Bogoliubov–de Gennes mean-field theory combined with an analytic Green's function calculation, applied to the attractive Hubbard–Kondo model on the two-dimensional Lieb lattice with classical core spins coupled to itinerant electrons.

Result: A magnetic superconductor emerges from Kondo–pairing competition, with (π,π) order near half filling, then (0,π), and at stronger Kondo coupling an incommensurate spiral (0,q). Two inequivalent Lifshitz-type transitions are found: with zero Kondo coupling, the minimum-energy contour deforms, a hole pocket about M becoming a perfectly nested square at μ=−2t and reconnecting into pockets about Γ; at finite coupling the manifold dimension itself drops for certain chemical potentials, obtained analytically for each magnetic order. Only the spiral carries the minimum energy through zero, nucleating nodes and yielding a gapless magnetic superconductor.

Conclusion: Lifshitz-type transitions in multiband superconductors can involve not only contour deformation but also a drop in the dimension of the minimum-energy manifold, and the magnetic order strongly dictates whether the resulting superconductor is gapped or gapless, with the spiral phase giving nodal, gapless behavior visible in the density of states and spectral function.

Abstract: A Lifshitz transition is a class of non-symmetry-breaking transitions typically associated with metallic systems wherein the Fermi surface changes its connectivity as the chemical potential sweeps through a band extremum or a saddle point. The phenomenology naturally adapts itself to the superconducting state, where the object that reconstructs is the manifold of minimum quasiparticle energy rather than the Fermi surface. This paper concerns Lifshitz-type transitions in multi-band superconductors. Thus, we focus on the attractive Hubbard--Kondo model on the two-dimensional Lieb lattice, with classical core spins coupled to the itinerant electrons, by laying recourse to a real-space Bogoliubov--de Gennes mean-field theory and an analytic Green's function calculation. The competition between the Kondo and pairing channels yields a magnetic superconductor throughout, with $(π,π)$ order near half filling giving way to $(0,π)$ and then, at stronger Kondo coupling, to an incommensurate spiral $(0,q)$. In such a multiband superconductor hosting a flat band, we identify two inequivalent Lifshitz-type transitions. With the core spins decoupled (the Kondo coupling set to zero), the minimum energy contour deforms with chemical potential, a hole pocket about $M$ opening into a perfectly nested square at $μ=-2t$ and reconnecting into pockets about $Γ$; this nesting fixes where $(π,π)$ order sets in. At finite Kondo coupling, for certain chemical potential, the dimension of the manifold itself drops. We obtain it analytically for each magnetic order and show that only the spiral carries the minimum energy through zero, nucleating nodes and leaving a gapless magnetic superconductor, with clear signatures in the density of states and the spectral function.

</details>


### [100] [Pairing order and condensate fraction across the BCS-BEC crossover in the two-dimensional attractive Hubbard model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06319)
*Hao Du,Qian Sun,Zhuotao Xie,Yuan-Yao He*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: A numerically exact auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo study of the superconducting ground state in the 2D attractive Hubbard model, benchmarking pairing order and condensate fraction across the BCS-BEC crossover.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Superconductivity across the BCS-BEC crossover in the two-dimensional attractive Hubbard model requires unbiased, quantitatively reliable data; existing approaches are often approximate, motivating the use of a numerically exact method to establish benchmarks.

Method: Auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo (AFQMC), a numerically exact method, applied over interaction strengths U/t = 2–12 and fermion densities n = 0.25–1.0, with finite-size extrapolations to the thermodynamic limit; complementary quantities (double occupancy, total energy) computed, and next-nearest-neighbor hopping effects investigated.

Result: Systematic characterization of the pairing order and condensate fraction versus interaction strength and fermion density, with thermodynamic-limit values from finite-size extrapolation, plus high-precision double occupancy and total energy results; effects of next-nearest-neighbor hopping on superconducting properties.

Conclusion: The work provides a comprehensive set of unbiased numerical data for the attractive Hubbard model, offering quantitative benchmarks for superconductivity across the BCS-BEC crossover and for validating future theoretical and numerical approaches.

Abstract: We report a systematic study of the superconducting ground state in the two-dimensional attractive Hubbard model, employing the {\it numerically exact} auxiliary-field quantum Monte Carlo method. We focus primarily on the pairing order and condensate fraction across the BCS-BEC crossover. Our numerical calculations cover a broad and physically relevant parameter regime, with the interaction strength $U/t=2\sim 12$ (where $t$ is the nearest-neighbor hopping amplitude) and fermion density $n=0.25\sim 1.0$. We systematically characterize the dependence of the pairing order and condensate fraction on both the interaction strength and fermion density, and perform finite-size extrapolations to obtain their thermodynamic-limit values. As complementary quantities, we also provide high-precision numerical results for the double occupancy and total energy. Furthermore, we investigate the effects of next-nearest-neighbor hopping on the superconducting properties of the model. Our results offer a comprehensive set of unbiased numerical data for the attractive Hubbard model, establishing quantitative benchmarks for understanding superconductivity across the BCS-BEC crossover and for validating future theoretical and numerical approaches.

</details>


### [101] [Dynamical Valence Fluctuations and Magnetic Anisotropy in Sm(Co,Cu)$_5$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06568)
*Alexander B. Shick,Alexander V. Andreev,Evgenia A. Tereshina-Chitrova,Institute of Physics,the Czech Academy of Sciences,Na Slovance 2,CZ-18221 Prague,Czech Republic*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Using self-consistent DFT+U(ED), the study shows that dynamical Sm 4f valence fluctuations (Sm2+/Sm3+) control the contrasting magnetic moments and anisotropy of SmCo5 and SmCu5.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The magnetic properties of Sm-based intermetallics arise from an interplay of localized 4f states, exchange interactions, and valence fluctuations, but the role of dynamical 4f correlations has remained poorly understood.

Method: The authors perform self-consistent DFT+U(ED) calculations that explicitly treat dynamical fluctuations of the Sm 4f shell to investigate the electronic structure and magnetic anisotropy of SmCo5 and SmCu5.

Result: SmCo5 shows pronounced Sm2+--Sm3+ valence fluctuations with a 4f ground state dominated by f5 and f6 configurations; the dynamical 4f correlations nearly compensate the Sm spin and orbital moments and quantitatively reproduce the electronic structure, magnetic moments, and large uniaxial anisotropy. Substituting Co with Cu shifts the balance toward f6, strongly altering the Sm spin and orbital moments and markedly reducing both total magnetic moment and anisotropy in SmCu5.

Conclusion: Dynamical 4f valence fluctuations are essential for explaining the contrasting magnetic properties of SmCo5 and SmCu5, offering a microscopic framework for magnetic anisotropy upon Co-to-Cu substitution in Sm(Co,Cu)5.

Abstract: The magnetic properties of Sm-based intermetallics are governed by a delicate interplay between localized $4f$ states, exchange interactions, and valence fluctuations, yet the role of dynamical $4f$ correlations remains poorly understood. Here we investigate the electronic structure and magnetic anisotropy of SmCo$_5$ and SmCu$_5$ using self-consistent DFT+$U$(ED), which explicitly treats dynamical fluctuations of the Sm $4f$ shell. We find that SmCo$_5$ exhibits pronounced Sm$^{2+}$--Sm$^{3+}$ valence fluctuations, with the $4f$ ground state dominated by $f^5$ and $f^6$ configurations. The resulting dynamical $4f$ correlations yield near compensation of the Sm spin and orbital moments and provide a quantitative description of the electronic structure, magnetic moments, and large uniaxial magnetic anisotropy of SmCo$_5$. Upon replacing Co with Cu, the balance shifts toward the $f^6$ configuration, with substantial changes in the Sm spin and orbital moments. In SmCu$_5$, this evolution of the $4f$ configuration is accompanied by a pronounced reduction of both the total magnetic moment and magnetic anisotropy. These results demonstrate that dynamical $4f$ valence fluctuations are essential for understanding the contrasting magnetic properties of SmCo$_5$ and SmCu$_5$ and provide a microscopic framework for describing magnetic anisotropy upon Co to Cu substitution in Sm(Co,Cu)$_5$.

</details>


### [102] [Interacting quantum criticality with U(1) symmetry breaking in 1+1 dimensions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06682)
*Mutsumi Shimomura,Kohei Kawabata*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Contrary to the Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner expectation, spontaneous breaking of U(1) spin-rotation symmetry and an interacting quantum critical point emerge in one-dimensional spin-1/2 and spin-1 chains, with exponents ν = 0.60 ± 0.02 and z = 1.986 ± 0.005.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner theorem conventionally forbids spontaneous breaking of continuous symmetries in one-dimensional quantum ground states, so demonstrating a counterexample and characterizing its universality motivates this work.

Method: Subsystem Binder scaling is used to locate the critical point and extract the correlation-length critical exponent; finite-size scaling of the lowest neutral excitation gap yields the dynamical critical exponent.

Result: U(1) spin-rotation symmetry spontaneously breaks, an interacting quantum critical point appears, ν = 0.60 ± 0.02 (distinct from the Gaussian value 0.5, consistent with perturbative renormalization-group predictions), and z = 1.986 ± 0.005.

Conclusion: Since z < 2 is incompatible with microscopic realizations of this critical theory by frustration-free models, the mechanism for continuous symmetry breaking in one-dimensional quantum ground states differs from frustration freeness.

Abstract: The Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner theorem motivates the expectation that continuous symmetries remain unbroken in one-dimensional quantum ground states. Here, contrary to this expectation, we demonstrate the spontaneous breaking of U(1) spin-rotation symmetry and the emergence of an interacting quantum critical point in lattice spin-$1/2$ and spin-$1$ chains. Using subsystem Binder scaling, we locate the critical point and determine the correlation-length critical exponent as $ν= 0.60 \pm 0.02$, distinct from the Gaussian value $0.5$ but consistent with the perturbative renormalization-group prediction. We further analyze the finite-size scaling of the lowest neutral excitation gap and obtain the dynamical critical exponent $z=1.986 \pm 0.005$. The result $z < 2$ is incompatible with microscopic realizations of this critical theory by frustration-free models, implying a mechanism for continuous symmetry breaking in one-dimensional quantum ground states distinct from frustration freeness.

</details>


### [103] [Strong metamagnetic switching and anisotropic magnetotransport in the distorted kagome magnet Tb6Ti4Al43](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06732)
*Lipika,Sneh,Shobha Singh,Ralf Koban,Walter Schnelle,Rie Umetsu,Kaustuv Manna*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Single-crystal Tb6Ti4Al43, a distorted kagome antiferromagnet, shows strong magnetic anisotropy, AFM order near 19 K, field-tunable metamagnetic/spin-flop transitions, and a large intrinsic anomalous Hall contribution tied to Berry curvature, making it a multifunctional platform.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Kagome-lattice magnets are attractive for exploring the interplay of frustrated magnetism, topology, and transport, since their momentum-space topology can generate nontrivial Berry curvature and anomalous Hall effects. Tb6Ti4Al43, where Tb spins form a distorted kagome net within a Ti-Al framework, offers a route to couple these effects to magnetic field-tunable states, motivating a full characterization of its magnetic, electrical, and thermodynamic behavior.

Method: Comprehensive experimental investigation of single crystalline Tb6Ti4Al43: magnetic, electrical, and thermodynamic measurements. Magnetization and magnetotransport (magnetoresistance, Hall) were measured with magnetic field applied along the c-axis and within the ab plane to map the field-induced magnetic states and their transport signatures.

Result: Tb6Ti4Al43 shows pronounced magnetic anisotropy and antiferromagnetic ordering at ~19 K. For H applied along c, a strong metamagnetic switching with clear asymmetric low-temperature hysteresis is observed; for H in the ab plane, a spin-flop transition occurs. Field-induced magnetic states produce pronounced anomalies in magnetoresistance and an anomalous Hall contribution, including a significant intrinsic component to the anomalous Hall conductivity indicative of Berry curvature effects.

Conclusion: The results demonstrate a strong interplay of spin, charge, and lattice degrees of freedom in Tb6Ti4Al43 and establish this distorted kagome antiferromagnet as a promising platform for field-tunable multifunctional behavior, including electrically readable magnetic switching, spin manipulation, and magneto-mechanical functionalities.

Abstract: We report a comprehensive investigation of the magnetic, electrical, and thermodynamic properties of single crystalline Tb6Ti4Al43, in which Tb atoms form a distorted kagome lattice embedded within a complex Ti-Al framework. Tb6Ti4Al43 exhibits pronounced magnetic anisotropy and antiferromagnetic ordering at approximately 19 K. For magnetic field applied along the c-axis, a strong metamagnetic switching is observed, accompanied by a clear asymmetric hysteresis at low temperatures, while for field applied along the ab plane, a spin-flop transition is observed. The effect of field induced magnetic states is strongly reflected in magnetotransport measurements, giving rise to pronounced anomalies in magnetoresistance and an anomalous Hall contribution. A significant intrinsic contribution to the anomalous Hall conductivity is observed, highlighting the Berry curvature effects arising from the system's momentum-space topology. Our results highlight the interplay of spin, charge, and lattice degrees of freedom in Tb6Ti4Al43 and establish this distorted kagome antiferromagnet as a promising platform for field-tunable multifunctional behaviour, including electrically readable magnetic switching, spin manipulation, and magneto-mechanical functionalities.

</details>


### [104] [Unraveling Fermi liquid charge and heat transport in RuO$_{2}$](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06763)
*Harrison LaBollita,Fabian B. Kugler,Antoine Georges,Cyrus E. Dreyer*

Main category: cond-mat.str-el

TL;DR: Realistic DFT+DMFT calculations identify RuO2 as a moderately correlated Fermi liquid, achieving (semi-)quantitative agreement with optical conductivity, resistivity, and thermopower, but revealing a puzzle in the electronic thermal resistivity.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: RuO2 is a multi-orbital Fermi liquid with abundant complementary experimental data, making it an ideal stringent testbed for quantitative many-body electronic structure theory.

Method: Realistic dynamical mean-field theory (DFT+DMFT) calculations of spectroscopic and transport properties of paramagnetic RuO2, including exploration of low-temperature electron-phonon scattering.

Result: Moderate electronic correlations are found, with (semi-)quantitative agreement for optical conductivity, electrical resistivity, and thermopower. However, the calculated electronic thermal resistivity significantly undershoots the experimentally inferred low-temperature thermal resistivity.

Conclusion: The discrepancy in thermal resistivity raises a puzzle regarding relative relaxation of charge versus heat currents in the Fermi liquid regime; electron-phonon scattering is explored as a possible resolution, but full quantitative description remains open. The work demonstrates the power of combining precision DFT+DMFT with complementary experiments for testing correlated quantum materials descriptions.

Abstract: RuO$_2$ is a multi-orbital Fermi liquid for which an array of complementary experimental measurements has recently become available, making it a stringent testbed for quantitative many-body electronic structure theory. Using realistic dynamical mean-field calculations, we compute the spectroscopic and transport properties of RuO$_2$ in the paramagnetic state. We identify RuO$_2$ as a moderately correlated Fermi liquid and obtain (semi-)quantitative agreement with experiment for its optical conductivity, electrical resistivity, and thermopower. This broad agreement across complementary observables establishes a ``precision'' many-body description of RuO$_2$ and allows remaining discrepancies to be identified as meaningful tests of the theory. In particular, the calculated electronic thermal resistivity significantly undershoots the experimentally inferred low-temperature thermal resistivity, raising an important puzzle concerning the relative relaxation of charge and heat currents in the Fermi liquid regime. We explore low-temperature electron-phonon scattering as a possible mechanism for resolving this discrepancy, while a fully quantitative description remains an open problem. More broadly, our results demonstrate the power of combining precision DFT+DMFT calculations with complementary experimental probes to build and critically test quantitative descriptions of correlated quantum materials.

</details>


<div id='physics.chem-ph'></div>

# physics.chem-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [105] [Reaction mechanisms of sodium carbonate activation in ultra-high-volume slag-cement blends](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03748)
*Samira Hossain,Zhanzhao Li,Kai Gong*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Incorporating 5-20 wt.% dry-blended Na2CO3 into a 90% slag/10% PC one-part binder shows a trade-off: moderate dosages (5-10 wt.%) accelerate early strength but hamper later-age reaction, whereas a high dosage (20 wt.%) moderates early pH rise yet sustains alkalinity, yielding higher reaction extent, lower porosity, and strength approaching neat PC with 66-72% lower global warming potential.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Ultra-high-volume slag-cement blends can cut clinker ratio substantially, but slow slag reaction limits strength. Na2CO3 is a less caustic, lower-carbon activator, yet its moderate alkalinity can also delay strength development. The study tests whether integrating dry-blended Na2CO3 into a one-part slag-PC binder can mitigate slow strength development in both systems.

Method: Dry-blended Na2CO3 (0-20 wt.% relative to water) was added to a one-part binder of 90 wt.% ground-granulated blast-furnace slag and 10 wt.% Portland cement. Characterization combined compressive strength testing, isothermal calorimetry, time-resolved quantitative X-ray diffraction, Fourier-transform infrared spectroscopy, aqueous solution analysis, and thermodynamic modeling to link Na2CO3 dosage to phase assemblage, alkalinity, calculated porosity, and strength development. Screening-level cradle-to-gate life-cycle assessment quantified global warming potential.

Result: At 5-10 wt.% Na2CO3, calcite-dominated carbonate reactions caused a rapid alkalinity increase and accelerated early-age reaction and strength gain, but subsequent alkalinity decline limited later-age slag reaction and gel formation. At 20 wt.% Na2CO3, substantial early formation of gaylussite and carbonate-AFm phases moderated the initial pH rise and delayed early gel accumulation; the larger alkali inventory and evolving phase-solution partitioning sustained alkalinity at later ages, enabling continued slag dissolution and gel formation. This produced higher reaction extent and lower calculated porosities, with compressive strength approaching neat PC. Life-cycle assessment indicates 66-72% reduction in global warming potential relative to PC, depending on Na2CO3 production pathway.

Conclusion: Carbonate-mediated control of alkalinity persistence is key to sustaining slag reaction in ultra-high-volume slag-cement systems. Moderate Na2CO3 favors early strength via calcite formation but fails to sustain later-age alkalinity, whereas high Na2CO3 buffers pH through gaylussite and carbonate-AFm formation, supporting prolonged reaction and strength development at substantially reduced carbon footprint.

Abstract: Ultra-high-volume slag-cement (UHVS) blends can substantially reduce clinker ratio, but slow slag reaction limits strength development; Na2CO3 is a less caustic and lower-carbon activator for alkali-activated materials, yet its moderate alkalinity can similarly delay strength development. Here, dry-blended Na2CO3 (0-20 wt.% relative to water) was incorporated into a one-part binder with 90 wt.% ground-granulated blast-furnace slag and 10 wt.% Portland cement (PC) to determine whether this integration could mitigate slow strength development in both systems. Compressive strength testing, isothermal calorimetry, time-resolved quantitative X-ray diffraction, Fourier-transform infrared spectroscopy, aqueous solution analysis, and thermodynamic modeling were combined to determine how Na2CO3 controls phase assemblage, alkalinity, calculated porosity and strength development. At 5-10 wt.% Na2CO3, calcite-dominated carbonate reactions promoted a rapid alkalinity increase and accelerated early-age reaction and strength development; however, subsequent alkalinity decline limited later-age slag reaction and gel formation. In contrast, 20 wt.% Na2CO3 favored substantial early formation of gaylussite and carbonate-AFm phases, which moderated the initial pH rise and delayed early gel accumulation. The larger alkali inventory and evolving phase-solution partitioning helped sustain alkalinity at later ages, enabling continued slag dissolution and gel formation. This pathway produced higher reaction extent and lower calculated porosities, with compressive strength approaching that of neat PC. Screening-level cradle-to-gate life-cycle assessment indicates a 66-72% reduction in global warming potential relative to PC, depending on Na2CO3 production pathways. These results identify carbonate-mediated control of alkalinity persistence as key to sustaining slag reaction in UHVS systems.

</details>


### [106] [PyCC: A Reference Quantum Chemistry Python Package for Education, Validation, and Rapid Prototyping](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04107)
*T. Daniel Crawford,Benjamin G. Peyton,Zhe Wang,Aparna Krishnan,Brendan M. Shumberger,Jose Madriaga,Joshua Atta-Kumi,Aaron De Clercq,Ritvik Prabhu*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: PyCC is an open-source Python quantum chemistry package providing reference implementations of coupled cluster methods (through CC3) and related approaches, with extensive molecular property capabilities and unique features like real-time CC propagation and local correlation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To provide a clear reference implementation serving as a validation suite for production codes, a platform for rapid prototyping of new methods, and an educational resource for newcomers to quantum chemistry.

Method: PyCC covers closed- and open-shell single-determinant references in spatial- and spin-orbital formulations for the coupled cluster hierarchy (including triples-including methods like CC3), plus MP2 and CI. It is built around a shared Wavefunction base and a method-agnostic derivative layer, allowing new methods to inherit the full property set once reduced densities and first-order responses are supplied.

Result: The package delivers analytic first and second energy derivatives (gradients, Hessians, polarizabilities, atomic polar tensors), non-energy-derivative properties (velocity-gauge atomic polar tensors, diagonal Born-Oppenheimer corrections), dynamic linear response functions, and excitation energies. Unique capabilities include real-time CC propagation through CC3, atomic axial tensors for VCD spectra at MP2/CI levels, and local correlation methods with perturbation-aware correlation domains.

Conclusion: PyCC is fully open source, hosted on GitHub under the BSD 3-clause license, offering a comprehensive, modular framework for coupled cluster and related electronic structure methods.

Abstract: PyCC is a Python-based quantum chemistry package that emphasizes coupled cluster and related methods. Developed over the last five years, PyCC's purpose is to provide a clear reference implementation that will serve as a validation suite for production code, a platform for rapid prototyping and testing of new methods, and an educational resource for newcomers to the field. The package covers both closed- and open-shell single-determinant references in spatial- and spin-orbital formulations for the coupled cluster hierarchy through triples-including methods such as CC3, as well as for MP2 and CI methods. PyCC provides a broad range of molecular properties such as analytic first and second energy derivatives including gradients, Hessians, polarizabilities and atomic polar tensors, as well as non-energy-derivative properties, such as velocity-gauge atomic polar tensors and diagonal Born-Oppenheimer corrections, plus dynamic linear response functions, and excitation energies. The package includes a number of unique capabilities, such as real-time CC propagation (up through CC3), atomic axial tensors for simulating vibrational circular dichroism spectra at the MP2 and CI levels, and local correlation methods including perturbation-aware correlation domains. The code is designed around a shared Wavefunction base and a method-agnostic derivative layer that enables a new method to inherit the full property set once it supplies its reduced densities and their first-order responses. PyCC is fully open source and available on GitHub under the BSD 3-clause license.

</details>


### [107] [Dielectric Response of Short and Long Range Models of Nonuniform Liquids and Implications for Machine Learned Interatomic Potentials](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04224)
*Atul C. Thakur,Harender S. Dhattarwal,Richard C. Remsing*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Short-range models (including local MLIPs) reproduce the transverse dielectric profile of confined water but fail qualitatively on the longitudinal profile; long-range interactions stiffen polarization fluctuations and shorten the interfacial correlation length, and an LMF-based fluctuation relation restores accurate longitudinal dielectric prediction.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Interfaces and confinement modify dielectric screening, so modeling interfacial chemistry requires an accurate nonuniform dielectric tensor. Yet short-range machine-learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs) are widely applied to molecular interfaces without a quantitative understanding of their nonuniform dielectric response. This work aims to build that understanding by separating the roles of short- and long-range electrostatics.

Method: Local molecular field (LMF) theory is used to disentangle short- and long-range electrostatics in confined water. A Landau theory is developed to explain the effect of long-range interactions on polarization fluctuations. A fluctuation relation and integral equation for LMF theory are derived, and symmetry-preserving mean field (SPMF) theory is combined with short-range MLIPs to treat long-range interactions at linear scaling.

Result: Short- and long-range models yield the same transverse dielectric profile, but short-range models give qualitatively different longitudinal profiles. The Landau theory shows long-range interactions stiffen polarization fluctuations by a factor of the bulk dielectric constant and reduce the interfacial polarization correlation length by an order of magnitude. The derived LMF fluctuation relation and integral equation accurately predict the longitudinal dielectric response, and can map polarization fluctuations onto the nonuniform dielectric profile learned by MLIPs trained on interfacial configurations.

Conclusion: Long-range electrostatics are essential for correct longitudinal interfacial dielectric response, which short-range models miss. LMF theory, with the newly derived fluctuation relation, corrects both structure and dielectric response, and MLIPs effectively learn a static mean field correction at the interface. Combining short-range MLIPs with SPMF theory offers a linear-scaling route to long-range interactions and nonuniform dielectric functions.

Abstract: Dielectric screening of electric fields is modified by interfaces and confinement, and any accurate description of interfacial chemistry necessitates properly modeling the nonuniform dielectric tensor. However, models with short range interactions, such as local machine learned interatomic potentials (MLIPs), are increasingly used in simulations of molecular interfaces without quantitative understanding of their nonuniform dielectric response. To build this understanding, we use the framework provided by local molecular field (LMF) theory to quantify the roles of short and long range electrostatics in confined water. Short and long range models predict the same transverse dielectric profile but short range models predict qualitatively different longitudinal dielectric profiles than their long range counterparts. To understand these differences, we develop a Landau theory that shows that long range interactions stiffen polarization fluctuations by a factor of the bulk dielectric constant and reduce the interfacial polarization correlation length by an order of magnitude. LMF theory, which captures long range interactions with an averaged field, can correct the structure and dielectric response, but the standard fluctuation relation no longer holds. We derive a fluctuation relation and integral equation for LMF theory and demonstrate their accuracy for predicting longitudinal dielectric response. MLIPs that incorporate interfacial configurations in their training essentially learn a static mean field correction at the interface, such that the integral equation can be used to predict their nonuniform dielectric profile from polarization fluctuations. We also show that short range MLIPs can be combined with symmetry preserving mean field (SPMF) theory to provide a linear-scaling alternative for long range interactions and predicting nonuniform dielectric functions.

</details>


### [108] [From Ground State to Electronic and Vibronic Spectra via Memory Kernel Coupling Theory and Neural Quantum States](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04315)
*Xinchen He,Wenjie Dou*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: Aurora combines neural quantum states with continued-fraction memory-kernel coupling theory to reconstruct electronic and vibronic absorption spectra directly from variational ground states, avoiding explicit excited-state manifolds or time propagation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Modelling molecular absorption spectra from scalable ground-state representations is difficult, since conventional methods typically require explicit excited states or real-time propagation.

Method: Aurora maps the zero-temperature Liouvillian response hierarchy onto repeated wavefunction-space applications of a shifted Hamiltonian acting on a dipole-prepared state, using variational Monte Carlo, implicit-restarted-Lanczos-assisted NQS optimization, matrix-free Hamiltonian application, and adaptive recursion control. For electron-phonon Hamiltonians it adds Huang-Rhys mode selection with local SVD phonon encoders.

Result: Electronic spectra agree closely with full-configuration-interaction and conventional MKCT references for tractable molecules, with restarted-Lanczos improving difficult NQS cases. For H2, phonon compression retains the dominant vibronic envelope and outperforms bare local-basis truncation; paired calculations for N2, H2O, H2S, and NH3 show similar principal features with and without encoding. Vibronic spectra are also obtained for C6H6 and a 10-electron, 10-orbital C10H8 active space.

Conclusion: Aurora offers a unified route from variational molecular ground states to electronic and vibronic spectra without constructing excited-state manifolds.

Abstract: Modelling molecular absorption spectra from scalable ground-state representations is challenging because conventional approaches often require explicit excited states or time propagation. Here, we introduce a method to obtain Absorption Using Recursive Operator Response with vibronic Augmentation (Aurora), which combines neural quantum states (NQSs) with continued-fraction memory-kernel coupling theory (CF-MKCT). Aurora maps the zero-temperature Liouvillian response hierarchy onto repeated wavefunction-space applications of a shifted Hamiltonian to a dipole-prepared state, enabling direct spectral reconstruction from variational ground states. The implementation combines variational Monte Carlo, implicit-restarted-Lanczos-assisted NQS optimization, matrix-free Hamiltonian application, and adaptive recursion control. The resulting electronic spectra closely agree with full-configuration-interaction and conventional MKCT references for tractable molecules, with the restarted-Lanczos strategy improving difficult NQS cases. For electron-phonon Hamiltonians, Aurora combines Huang-Rhys mode selection with local singular-value-decomposition (SVD) phonon encoders. The H2 benchmark indicates that compression retains the dominant vibronic envelope and improves upon bare local-basis truncation, while paired calculations for N2, H2O, H2S, and NH3 yield similar principal features with and without encoding. We further obtain vibronic spectra for C6H6 and a 10-electron, 10-orbital C10H8 active space. Overall, Aurora provides a unified route from variational molecular ground states to electronic and vibronic spectra without constructing excited-state manifolds.

</details>


### [109] [Beyond ideal cavities: quantifying the impact of cavity dissipation on light-matter strong coupling](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04534)
*Giovanna Bruno,Enrico Busani,Lorenzo Gialli,Rosario Roberto Riso,Enrico Ronca*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: The authors introduce a minimal, computationally cheap modification to the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian that incorporates cavity dissipation via the quality factor Q, and show within QED-CCSD that realistic losses can reduce the Rabi splitting of p-nitroaniline by more than 20%.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: First-principles Cavity-QED methods typically assume ideal, lossless cavity fields, neglecting experimentally unavoidable cavity dissipation. Since realistic cavities have finite quality factors, this idealization may misrepresent polaritonic energetics and hinder quantitative guidance of experiments.

Method: A minimal extension of the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian is proposed in which the cavity field is treated as a damped harmonic oscillator under the very weak damping approximation. Cavity losses enter only the light-matter interaction and dipole self-energy terms, with no additional photonic degrees of freedom. A time-dependent unitary transformation recasts the Hamiltonian into a real-valued Hermitian form, enabling direct integration into existing ab-initio QED methods at no extra cost or scaling change. The approach is combined with QED-CCSD and applied to the optical spectrum of p-nitroaniline under strong light-matter coupling.

Result: For realistic quality factors, cavity dissipation reduces the Rabi splitting by more than 20% relative to the ideal-cavity limit, while the system still remains in the strong-coupling regime. Convergence to the ideal-cavity limit requires substantially higher quality factors than those needed merely to establish strong coupling.

Conclusion: Cavity dissipation significantly affects polaritonic energetics, so experimentally realistic cavity losses must be included in quantitative ab-initio Cavity-QED simulations to reliably interpret and guide experiments.

Abstract: Cavity Quantum Electrodynamics (Cavity-QED) offers a powerful framework for controlling light-matter interactions, yet first-principles approaches commonly assume ideal, lossless cavity fields. Here, we introduce a minimal extension to the Pauli-Fierz Hamiltonian that accounts for cavity dissipation through the experimentally accessible cavity quality factor Q. The electromagnetic field is described as a damped harmonic oscillator within the very weak damping approximation. In this framework, cavity losses only enter the light-matter interaction and dipole self-energy terms, without introducing additional photonic degrees of freedom. A time-dependent unitary transformation yields a real-valued Hermitian Hamiltonian that can be incorporated into existing ab-initio QED methods without additional computational cost or changes in scaling. We combined the approach to the QED-CCSD method and investigated the effects of cavity dissipation on the optical spectrum of a p-nitroaniline molecule under strong light-matter coupling. We found that, for realistic quality factors, cavity dissipation can reduce the Rabi splitting by more than 20% compared to the ideal-cavity limit, even though the system remains in the strong-coupling regime. Convergence towards the ideal-cavity limit then requires substantially higher quality factors than those needed to establish strong coupling. These results demonstrate that cavity dissipation can significantly affect polaritonic energetics and highlight the importance of incorporating experimentally realistic cavity losses into quantitative ab-initio Cavity-QED simulations to accurately guide experimental protocols.

</details>


### [110] [Basis pruning in rovibrational configuration interaction (RVCI) calculations](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05513)
*Kemal Oenen,Subhasish Das,Guntram Rauhut*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: This paper proposes a basis-pruning algorithm for rovibrational variational calculations, selecting approximate vibrational states by their impact on energies within a target spectral window, demonstrated on formaldehyde and thioketene.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Variational rovibrational calculations without model Hamiltonians are limited by the size and composition of the vibrational basis. Existing VCI basis reductions still require deciding which vibrational state functions to include.

Method: In analogy to configuration-selective VCI theory, candidate vibrational states are represented by inexpensive approximate wavefunctions and pruned according to their impact on rovibrational energies in a spectral window of interest.

Result: For formaldehyde, the approach is compared with widely used polyad schemes that control the vibrational basis. For thioketene, benchmark calculations assess accuracy and efficiency across different threshold values.

Conclusion: The pruning algorithm offers an effective alternative to conventional basis-selection schemes for rovibrational variational calculations.

Abstract: The variational calculation of rovibrational spectra without model Hamiltonians remains challenging, with the size and composition of the vibrational basis constituting a major bottleneck. Preceding vibrational configuration interaction (VCI) calculations reduce this basis, but leave the question of which vibrational state functions to include. In close analogy to configuration-selective VCI theory, we propose a basis-pruning algorithm in which candidate vibrational states are represented by inexpensive approximate wavefunctions and selected according to their impact on rovibrational energies in a spectral window of interest. For formaldehyde, comparisons with widely used polyad schemes controlling the vibrational basis are provided. Calculations on thioketene benchmark the accuracy and efficiency of the pruning approach for different threshold values.

</details>


### [111] [Spinorial Weyl Operator Reduction of Dirac (SWORD): Exact Relativistic Quantum Chemistry with Half the Spinor](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06392)
*Jacopo Masotti,Quentin Pitteloud,Luca Frediani*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: An exact two-component reformulation of the Dirac equation in the Weyl representation, solved iteratively with Helmholtz Green's functions and an adaptive multiwavelet basis for rigorous error control.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional energy-decoupling approaches to reduce the four-component Dirac problem to two components introduce inverse-potential (energy-dependent) operators, which are problematic. The paper seeks an exact two-component form that avoids such operators.

Method: The diagonal kinetic operator and purely scalar mass coupling in the Weyl basis are exploited to reduce the four-component Dirac problem to an exact two-component form without inverse-potential operators. The resulting second-order differential equations are recast in an integral formulation and solved iteratively via convolution with the Helmholtz Green's function. The formalism is implemented in an adaptive multiwavelet basis with user-defined error control.

Result: Proof-of-concept calculations on simple atomic systems demonstrate numerical stability, accuracy, and efficiency of the proposed algorithm.

Conclusion: The exact self-consistent two-component Weyl-representation reformulation, combined with Helmholtz Green's function iteration and adaptive multiwavelet discretization, offers a stable, accurate, and efficient scheme for relativistic electronic structure calculations.

Abstract: We present an exact self-consistent two-component reformulation of the Dirac equation in the Weyl representation. By exploiting the diagonal structure of the kinetic operator and the purely scalar mass coupling in the Weyl basis, the four-component Dirac problem is reduced to an exact two-component form without introducing inverse-potential operators that arise in conventional energy-decoupling approaches. The resulting second-order differential equations are cast into an integral formulation and solved iteratively via convolution with the Helmholtz Green's function. We implement this formalism within an adaptive multiwavelet basis framework, providing rigorous, user-defined error control. Proof-of-concept calculations for simple atomic systems demonstrate the numerical stability, accuracy, and efficiency of the proposed algorithm.

</details>


### [112] [Optimizing the Optimizer: Language Models Discover Faster Molecular Relaxation](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06577)
*Artem Tsypin,Vladimir Deshchenya,Kuzma Khrabrov,Denis Potapov,Maxim Radchenko,Artur Kadurin,Michael G. Medvedev*

Main category: physics.chem-ph

TL;DR: An autonomous agent rewrites the geometry optimizer Sella to create AutoSella, a family of two optimizers that consistently reduce force-call counts across held-out molecular benchmarks and potentials, including at r2SCAN-3c DFT level.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Geometry optimization is a major cost in quantum-chemical workflows: each step requires a force evaluation, and at the DFT level that evaluation dominates wall time. Although many optimization methods exist, the authors ask whether a language model can outperform the best of them through autoresearch.

Method: An agent rewrites the optimizer itself to minimize force-call counts, restrained by two admission gates that reject premature stopping and improvements that do not generalize to unseen molecules. The search starts from Sella, the fastest open-source optimizer available. Notably, the agent uses no DFT gradients during the search.

Result: The search produces AutoSella, a family of two optimizers. Both deliver consistent force-call reductions relative to Sella across held-out molecular benchmarks and potentials not used during the search. At the r2SCAN-3c DFT level, the best variant requires only 40.2–77.2% of Sella's force calls while achieving the same energy reduction.

Conclusion: A language-model-driven autoresearch loop can improve an existing state-of-the-art optimizer, yielding generalizable force-call reductions even on DFT-level benchmarks unseen during the search and without access to DFT gradients.

Abstract: Geometry optimization is a major cost in many quantum-chemical workflows: each optimization step requires one force evaluation, and at the density-functional level that evaluation dominates the wall time. Research in this area has produced a broad range of optimization methods, and we ask whether a language model can improve on the best of them through autoresearch. An agent rewrites the optimizer itself to minimize force-call counts, restrained by two admission gates that reject premature stopping and improvements that do not generalize to unseen molecules. Starting from Sella, the fastest open-source optimizer available, the search produces AutoSella, a family of two optimizers. Both of them deliver consistent force-call reductions relative to Sella across held-out molecular benchmarks and potentials not used during the search. Most notably, at the \texttt{r2SCAN-3c} DFT level, the best variant requires only $40.2$--$77.2\%$ of Sella's force calls while achieving the same energy reduction, even though agent used no DFT gradients.

</details>


<div id='physics.comp-ph'></div>

# physics.comp-ph [[Back]](#toc)

### [113] [Spectral properties of the Riemann zeta function and their physical applications](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04571)
*Kostadin G Gaminchev*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: Numerical study of the Riemann zeta function across three physical contexts: zero spectral statistics (GUE comparison, χ²/dof = 1.2), ζ(3/2) in Bose-Einstein condensation critical temperature (Tc = 0.13 K for an ideal gas), and zeta-function regularization of the Casimir effect (standard energy density −π²ℏc/720a³, ≈ −4.33×10⁻⁴ J m⁻² at a = 1 nm), all consistent with Montgomery-Odlyzko and Hilbert-Pólya-motivated spectral approaches.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The Riemann zeta function is central to number theory, yet its non-trivial zeros are conjecturally linked to physics via the Hilbert-Pólya and Montgomery-Odlyzko conjectures. This work aims to probe those links numerically by examining physical manifestations rather than purely analytic properties.

Method: Three numerical investigations: (i) spectral statistics of the non-trivial zeros compared against Gaussian Unitary Ensemble (GUE) predictions; (ii) evaluation of ζ(3/2) as it enters the critical temperature for Bose-Einstein condensation of an ideal gas; (iii) application of zeta-function regularization to compute the Casimir energy density between parallel plates.

Result: (i) Zero statistics agree with GUE at χ²/dof = 1.2; (ii) the Bose-Einstein critical temperature is Tc = 0.13 K for an ideal gas; (iii) zeta-function regularization reproduces the standard Casimir energy density −π²ℏc/(720a³), yielding ≈ −4.33×10⁻⁴ J m⁻² at a = 1 nm.

Conclusion: The numerical results are consistent with the Montgomery-Odlyzko conjecture and with spectral approaches motivated by the Hilbert-Pólya conjecture, within the statistical limitations of the analysis.

Abstract: This work studies the Riemann $ζ$ numerically with emphasis on its physical manifestations. Three areas are examined: (i) the spectral statistics of the non-trivial zeros, which we compare with the Gaussian Unitary Ensemble predictions, obtaining $χ^2/\text{dof} = 1.2$; (ii) the role of $ζ(\frac{3}{2})$ in determining the critical temperature for Bose-Einstein condensation, where we find $T_c = 0.13$ K for an ideal gas; and (iii) the application of $ζ$-function regularisation to the Casimir effect, yielding the standard Casimir energy density $-\frac{π^2 \hbar c}{720 a^3}$, which for $a=1\,\mathrm{nm}$ gives $\sim -4.33\times10^{-4}\,\mathrm{J\,m^{-2}}$. The numerical results are consistent with the Montgomery-Odlyzko conjecture and with spectral approaches motivated by the Hilbert-Pólya conjecture, within the statistical limitations of the analysis.

</details>


### [114] [HFS-TransNet: A Hybrid Fixed-Stress Transferable Neural Network for Quasi-Static Biot Poroelasticity](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04879)
*Zhequan Shen,Liyong Zhu,Mingchao Cai*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes HFS-TransNet, a hybrid fixed-stress transferable neural network that combines data-driven TransNet initialization with physics-informed fixed-stress splitting iterations to efficiently and robustly solve the strongly coupled, multiscale quasi-static Biot poroelasticity system.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The quasi-static Biot system for coupled fluid-solid interactions in poroelastic media is computationally demanding due to strong coupling and multiscale behavior, causing issues such as locking instability that challenge conventional solvers.

Method: HFS-TransNet employs a two-stage hybrid scheme. First, a data-driven TransNet module approximates observed data on an initialization domain to provide a high-quality starting point. Then a physics-informed stage drives fixed-stress splitting iterations via TransNet, admitting an error bound. After each iteration a greedy update strategy on a validation domain prevents model degradation, and a relative tolerance criterion governs convergence termination. Thus it inherits fixed-stress splitting robustness, data-driven flexibility, and TransNet transferability.

Result: Ablation studies and comparisons with FS-FEM and FS-PINN confirm HFS-TransNet's superior performance in capturing multiscale coupled physics across varying physical parameters and boundary conditions. It is successfully applied to brain edema simulations.

Conclusion: By bridging classical decoupled iterative schemes with hybrid scientific machine learning, HFS-TransNet offers a novel perspective for complex poroelastic simulations and effectively overcomes the strong coupling and locking instability of the Biot system.

Abstract: The quasi-static Biot system, which governs coupled fluid-solid interactions in poroelastic media, poses significant computational challenges due to its strong coupling and multiscale nature. To address these, we propose a hybrid fixed-stress transferable neural network (HFS-TransNet) method. In HFS-TransNet, a data-driven TransNet module first approximates observed data on an initialization domain to supply a high-quality starting point for the subsequent physics-informed stage, which then drives fixed-stress splitting iterations via TransNet and admits an error bound. After each iteration, a greedy update strategy on a validation domain prevents model degradation and a relative tolerance criterion governs convergence termination. HFS-TransNet thereby inherits the robustness of the fixed-stress splitting scheme, the flexibility of data-driven modeling, and the transferability of TransNet, effectively overcoming the strong coupling and locking instability inherent in the Biot system. Ablation studies and comparisons with FS-FEM and FS-PINN confirm HFS-TransNet's superior performance in capturing multiscale coupled physics across varying physical parameters and boundary conditions. By bridging classical decoupled iterative schemes with hybrid scientific machine learning, HFS-TransNet offers a novel perspective for complex poroelastic simulations, as demonstrated by its successful application to brain edema simulations.

</details>


### [115] [A Contrast-Source Inversion Scheme Based on Stochastic Optimization and Plug-and-Play Regularization](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05130)
*Lingqi Gao,Hakan Bagci*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: The paper proposes STO-PNP-CSI, an electromagnetic inversion scheme that combines stochastic optimization with plug-and-play regularization via a Swin-Conv-UNet denoiser to reduce computational cost and improve reconstruction accuracy over standard contrast-source inversion.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Standard contrast-source inversion (CSI) requires solving for the contrast source vector of every transmitter at each iteration, which is computationally expensive in multi-transmitter configurations. There is a need for a method that lowers per-iteration cost while escaping poor local minima and saddle points, without sacrificing reconstruction stability.

Method: The authors integrate stochastic optimization (STO) and plug-and-play (PNP) regularization into CSI, forming STO-PNP-CSI. STO solves only one randomly selected contrast source vector per iteration instead of all transmitters, reducing per-iteration cost. To counter the resulting information loss and increased ill-posedness, the Swin-Conv-UNet (SCUNet) denoiser is plugged into the CSI scheme as an implicit regularizer, providing a learned prior stronger than conventional hand-crafted ones.

Result: Applied to both synthetic and experimental data, STO-PNP-CSI yields accurate reconstructions at substantially lower computational cost than standard CSI, including under strong nonlinearity and measurement noise.

Conclusion: Stochastic optimization combined with a learned plug-and-play denoiser prior can stabilize contrast-source inversion and deliver accurate, computationally efficient electromagnetic reconstructions in challenging conditions.

Abstract: An electromagnetic inversion scheme that integrates stochastic optimization (STO) and plug-and-play (PNP) regularization into contrast-source inversion (CSI), termed STO-PNP-CSI, is developed. Standard CSI solves for the contrast source vector of every transmitter at each iteration, which is expensive in a multi-transmitter configuration. STO instead solves for only one randomly selected contrast source vector per iteration, which reduces the per-iteration cost and can help the inversion escape poor local minima and saddle points. The resulting loss of information, however, increases the ill-posedness of the inversion. To counter this, the Swin-Conv-UNet (SCUNet) denoiser is plugged into the CSI scheme as an implicit regularizer, supplying a learned prior that is stronger than conventional hand-crafted ones and stabilizes the reconstruction. The proposed STO-PNP-CSI is applied to both synthetic and experimental data. The results show that it yields accurate reconstructions at substantially lower computational cost than CSI, including under strong nonlinearity and measurement noise.

</details>


### [116] [Mechanism-resolved phase-field fracture of composite shells with a certified admissible constitutive operator](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06705)
*Altaf Ahmad Lone,R. T. Durai Prabhakaran,Timon Rabczuk,Xiaoying Zhuang*

Main category: physics.comp-ph

TL;DR: A phase-field fracture model for fibre-reinforced Reissner-Mindlin shells is reformulated with a replaceable constitutive operator expressed in four orthogonal invariants derived via Cholesky pull-back of the reference metric, enabling exact fibre/inter-fibre/interaction channel separation, thickness-dependent invariants via the shifter, and certified FE implementation.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Conventional phase-field shell fracture models are tied to a single closed-form stored energy, so stress, consistent tangent and plane-stress condensation must be re-derived and re-verified for each new expression, limiting flexibility and generalization (e.g., to learned constitutive laws).

Method: The Green-Lagrange strain of a geometrically exact Reissner-Mindlin shell is pulled back through the Cholesky factor of the reference metric and decomposed onto four mutually orthogonal invariants with an exact closure identity, turning fibre/inter-fibre/interaction separation into a basis change. On curved midsurfaces the metric, Cholesky factor and fibre direction vary through thickness via the shifter I - zeta b, and invariants inherit this dependence. Tension-compression split, channel degradation, plane-stress condensation, consistent tangent and FE assembly are all expressed in channel potentials and their derivatives, permitting closed-form or learned operators. Fibre-transverse interaction energy is stored once and degraded by the product of coupled fields. Six admissibility conditions are imposed; convexity of the two linear invariants, tangent independence and regularity of condensation are proved. A three-tier protocol certifies algebra, solver state and training-data admission; notched IM7/8552 shells are studied numerically.

Result: The two linear invariants are proved convex in the deformation gradient, the geometric tangent is shown independent of the material tangent, and plane-stress condensation is regular wherever residual stiffness is positive. Numerical studies on notched IM7/8552 shells verify the discretisation in thin and curved regimes and demonstrate that fibre orientation governs the damage envelope and load capacity, while curvature induces a through-thickness fracture asymmetry not representable by a single midsurface field.

Conclusion: Framing the shell constitutive law as a replaceable invariant-based operator yields a general, certifiable phase-field fracture framework for fibre-reinforced shells, with rigorous convexity, tangent and condensation properties, and demonstrates that shell curvature requires through-thickness resolution beyond single midsurface fields.

Abstract: Phase-field models of fracture in fibre-reinforced shells are usually formulated against a single closed-form stored energy, so that the stress, consistent tangent and plane-stress condensation are derived and verified for that expression alone. Here the constitutive law enters instead as a replaceable operator. The Green-Lagrange strain of a geometrically exact Reissner-Mindlin shell is pulled back through the Cholesky factor of the reference metric and resolved on four mutually orthogonal invariants with an exact closure identity, making the separation into fibre, inter-fibre and interaction channels a change of basis rather than a modelling assumption. On a curved midsurface, the metric, Cholesky factor and fibre direction vary through the thickness through the shifter I - zeta b, and the invariants inherit that dependence. The tension-compression split, channel degradation, plane-stress condensation, consistent tangent and finite-element assembly are all expressed in the channel potentials and their derivatives, allowing either closed-form or learned operators. The fibre-transverse interaction energy is stored once and degraded by the product of the fields whose mechanisms it couples. Six conditions define admissible operators; the two linear invariants are proved convex in the deformation gradient, the geometric tangent is shown independent of the material tangent, and the plane-stress condensation is regular wherever the residual stiffness is positive. A three-tier protocol certifies the algebra, solver state and admission of a state as training data. Numerical studies on notched IM7/8552 shells verify the discretisation in thin and curved regimes and show that fibre orientation governs the damage envelope and load capacity, while curvature drives a through-thickness fracture asymmetry that a single midsurface field cannot represent.

</details>


<div id='cond-mat.stat-mech'></div>

# cond-mat.stat-mech [[Back]](#toc)

### [117] [Multicatalyst reactions induce abrupt transition in a dense catalytic reaction network model](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04843)
*Kota Mitsumoto,Shuji Ishihara*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The Furusawa–Kaneko model of intracellular catalytic reaction networks is generalized to multicatalyst reactions and analyzed via dynamical mean-field theory, revealing that the number of catalysts per reaction qualitatively changes the bifurcation structure.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The original Furusawa–Kaneko model is a simple model of intracellular catalytic reaction networks but is limited to single-catalyst reactions; a generalization to multicatalyst reactions is needed to capture richer metabolic dynamics and to understand how network heterogeneity and reaction multiplicity affect metabolic–starvation transitions.

Method: The model is generalized to multicatalyst reactions and analyzed using dynamical mean-field theory. In the thermodynamic and dense-network limit, exact effective equations are derived and their fixed points analyzed for arbitrary degree distributions.

Result: Under nutrient-poor conditions, network heterogeneity suppresses the metabolic–starvation transition regardless of the number of catalysts per reaction. Under nutrient-rich conditions, the bifurcation structure depends on the number of catalysts: one catalyst yields only continuous transitions, whereas two or more catalysts produce bistable phases and discontinuous transitions. Two and three catalysts also differ, with three catalysts permitting bistability between metabolic states with different cell growth rates.

Conclusion: Multicatalyst generalization provides a possible mechanism for bistability and abrupt switching in metabolic dynamics, with the number of catalysts per reaction controlling the qualitative bifurcation structure.

Abstract: We generalize the Furusawa--Kaneko model, a simple model of intracellular catalytic reaction networks, to multicatalyst reactions and analyze the generalized model using dynamical mean-field theory. In the thermodynamic and dense-network limit, we derive exact effective equations and analyze their fixed points for arbitrary degree distributions. Under nutrient-poor conditions, network heterogeneity suppresses the metabolic--starvation transition irrespective of the number of catalysts per reaction. In contrast, under nutrient-rich conditions, the bifurcation structure changes with the number of catalysts. With one catalyst per reaction, only continuous transitions occur, whereas two or more catalysts give rise to bistable phases and discontinuous transitions. The bifurcation structure also differs between two and three catalysts per reaction, with three catalysts allowing bistability between metabolic states with different cell growth rates. Our results provide a possible mechanism for bistability and abrupt switching in metabolic dynamics.

</details>


### [118] [Uniform meandric systems as percolation on planar maps: conjectures and a numerical study](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03730)
*David Wei*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper numerically studies uniform meandric systems via pairs of simple random walks and their interpretation as critical percolation on random planar maps, providing evidence for negative association between opposite crossings and introducing a law-preserving local flip dynamics.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Understanding meandric systems and their connection to critical percolation on random planar maps, particularly the association structure of crossing events, motivates this numerical investigation.

Method: The authors encode uniform meandric systems using pairs of simple random walks, interpret them as critical percolation on random planar maps, study natural box-crossing events, and introduce a law-preserving dynamics based on local peak/valley flips in the walk encoding.

Result: Numerical evidence shows strong negative association between opposite open crossings, with conjectures formulated on their asymptotic behavior. Simulations indicate the percolation structure decorrelates much faster than the underlying random planar map.

Conclusion: The numerical study supports conjectures on crossing asymptotics and reveals a separation of timescales between percolation decorrelation and map decorrelation.

Abstract: We perform a numerical study of uniform meandric systems via their encoding by pairs of simple random walks and their interpretation as a model of critical percolation on random planar maps. We investigate certain natural box-crossing events and provide numerical evidence for a strong negative association between opposite open crossings and formulate conjectures on their asymptotic behavior. Our second contribution is a simple law-preserving dynamics on meandric systems, by local peak/valley flips in the walk encoding. Our simulations suggest that the percolation structure decorrelates much faster than the underlying random planar map.

</details>


### [119] [Harnessing Measurement Error as a Thermodynamic Resource in Two-Reservoir Information Engines](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03778)
*Yang Xiao,Qian Zeng,Jin Wang*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper introduces two-reservoir information engines (TRIEs) based on a two-level system, showing that measurement error's effect on work, efficiency, and fluctuations depends on the demon's control parameters, enabling useful work under any error level and efficiency surpassing the Carnot bound without violating the second law.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Measurement error is generally expected to constrain the performance of information engines. This work challenges that assumption by asking whether, and under what conditions, measurement error can instead be exploited or tolerated without degrading performance.

Method: The authors propose a class of two-reservoir information engines (TRIEs) built on a two-level system, analyze how the demon's control parameters govern the monotonic relationships between measurement error and performance metrics (work output, efficiency, work fluctuations), and design a dual Otto cycles TRIE with an experimental scheme using a trapped 40Ca+ ion.

Result: The monotonic relationships between work output, efficiency, work fluctuations, and measurement error are determined by the demon's control parameters. Conditions are established under which TRIEs produce useful work at any measurement error level, and regimes are identified where performance parameters can be simultaneously optimized by measurement error. When the demon's energy cost is supplied solely by the cold reservoir, efficiency accounting for this cost can exceed the standard Carnot bound without violating the second law.

Conclusion: Measurement error does not universally constrain information engine performance; its impact is tunable via the demon's control parameters. The results enable useful work under arbitrary measurement error, simultaneous optimization of performance metrics, and Carnot-bound-surpassing efficiency, with applicability extended to multi-level system information engines.

Abstract: In general, measurement error constrains the performance of information engines. However, this may not hold universally. In this work, we propose a class of two-reservoirs information engines (TRIEs) with a two-level system and demonstrate that the monotonic relationships between the work output, the efficiency, as well as the work fluctuations of the TRIEs and measurement error are determined by the demon's control parameters. Utilizing this, we establish the conditions that allow the TRIEs to generate useful work under any level of measurement error, and identify the regimes in which these performance parameters can be optimized simultaneously by the measurement error. Furthermore, we demonstrate that when the demon's energy cost is supplied solely by the cold reservoir, the efficiency that accounts for this cost can surpass the standard Carnot bound without violating the second law. Finally, we design a dual Otto cycles TRIE and provide an experimental scheme for its implementation based on a trapped $^{40}\mathrm{Ca}^+$ ion. Significantly, our results can be applied to the multi-level system information engines.

</details>


### [120] [Parisi's solution of the Sherrington--Kirkpatrick model from synchronous Monte Carlo dynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.05557)
*Yoshiyuki Kabashima*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The authors extend the Sompolinsky–Zippelius dynamical derivation of Parisi's solution to the Sherrington–Kirkpatrick model to a synchronous (parallel) discrete-time Monte Carlo dynamics, showing it reproduces the Parisi equations and matches numerical simulations.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Sompolinsky and Zippelius originally derived Parisi's solution of the SK model from the relaxational dynamics of soft spins in continuous time, relying on a hierarchy of widely separated time scales. The question is whether this dynamical route to the Parisi solution survives in a genuinely discrete-time, parallel-update setting, which is the natural simulation protocol and introduces auxiliary Gaussian fields and conditional independence of spins.

Method: They study synchronous Monte Carlo (SyncMC), in which all Ising spins are updated simultaneously. Gaussian auxiliary fields make the spins conditionally independent and the dynamics samples the Boltzmann distribution exactly for any system with symmetric pairwise couplings. They derive an exact discrete-time fluctuation-dissipation relation in equilibrium, and obtain dynamical mean-field equations for the SK model, which contain two extra terms relative to continuous-time dynamics: a self-coupling of each spin and a noise field correlated through the couplings, both affecting only fast relaxation. Under the Sompolinsky–Zippelius assumptions (widely separated time scales, and on each slow scale the same relation with a reduced ratio x), they analyze the long-time structure. Numerically, they perform replica-exchange equilibrium simulations up to N=2048 and measure the overlap distribution and relaxation response from random configurations up to N=4096.

Result: The correlation and response functions of SyncMC satisfy an exact discrete-time fluctuation-dissipation relation in equilibrium. The two additional terms in the mean-field equations affect only the fast relaxation, so the long-time structure reproduces the Parisi equations, including the Parisi partial differential equation and the equation for the distribution of local fields. Equilibrium simulations exclude the replica-symmetric distribution of the actual local fields and are consistent with the Parisi solution. The function x(q) extracted from the overlap distribution and from the relaxation response from random initial conditions agrees with Parisi's prediction.

Conclusion: The Sompolinsky–Zippelius programme can be carried through for a discrete-time, parallel-update dynamics: SyncMC yields the Parisi solution of the SK model in the long-time limit, and numerical simulations support the Parisi picture over the replica-symmetric one. This establishes synchronous Monte Carlo as a valid discrete-time dynamical route to the Parisi solution.

Abstract: Sompolinsky and Zippelius obtained Parisi's solution of the Sherrington-Kirkpatrick (SK) model from the relaxational dynamics of soft spins in continuous time, by assuming a hierarchy of widely separated time scales. Here we carry out the same programme for a discrete-time dynamics in which all Ising spins are updated at once. This dynamics, synchronous Monte Carlo (SyncMC), introduces Gaussian auxiliary fields so that the spins become conditionally independent, and it samples the Boltzmann distribution exactly for any system with symmetric pairwise couplings. We show that, for any such system, the correlation and response functions of SyncMC obey an exact fluctuation-dissipation relation in discrete time in equilibrium. The dynamical mean-field equations of SyncMC for the SK model contain two terms absent in continuous-time dynamics, a self-coupling of each spin and a noise correlated through the couplings; both affect only the fast relaxation. As a result, under the same assumptions as in the Sompolinsky-Zippelius construction (widely separated time scales, and on each slow scale the same relation with a reduced ratio x, as expected in relaxation from random initial conditions), the long-time structure of SyncMC reproduces the Parisi equations, including the Parisi partial differential equation and the equation for the distribution of local fields. Equilibrium simulations with replica exchange for up to N=2048 spins exclude the replica-symmetric distribution of the actual local fields and are consistent with the Parisi solution. The function x(q) estimated from the overlap distribution, and from the response in relaxation from random configurations for up to N=4096 spins, is also consistent with Parisi's.

</details>


### [121] [MUTACO: Simulator-Efficient Response Matching in Stochastic Nonlinear Systems via Compatibility-Aware Sequential Design](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04619)
*Ege Karadeniz,İlter Onat Korkmaz,Umut Can Turhan,Cem Tekin,Aydın Cem Keser*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: MUTACO is an uncertainty-aware sequential-design method for response matching in stochastic simulators lacking gradients, achieving lowest held-out response error under limited simulation budgets on a disordered oscillator network inverse problem.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
