Motivation: Many physical inverse problems rely on expensive stochastic simulators where gradients, adjoint sensitivities, or differentiable interfaces are unavailable. Existing approaches are ill-suited for response matching when only noisy forward evaluations are possible and when observables reflect ensemble statistics rather than a specific microscopic realization.

Method: MUTACO performs uncertainty-aware sequential design using only noisy forward evaluations, jointly learning the multicomponent response and its predictive uncertainty. It searches for parameter configurations whose predicted rotation and synchronization responses are compatible with the target response under multiple driving conditions, rather than requiring unique recovery of statistical properties.

Result: Under strictly limited simulation budgets, MUTACO achieves the lowest held-out response error among tested adaptive-search, space-filling, and simulation-based inference baselines, and also yields lower parameter-recovery error.

Conclusion: Response-targeted sequential design is a simulator-efficient approach to inverse modeling from finite noisy forward evaluations, with broader applicability to stochastic physical systems exhibiting correlated multicomponent responses and weakly constrained parameter directions.

Abstract: Many physical inverse problems are mediated by expensive stochastic simulators for which response gradients, adjoint sensitivities, or a differentiable simulator interface are unavailable. We introduce Multiple Target-based Confident Acquisition (MUTACO), an uncertainty-aware sequential-design method for response matching that requires only noisy forward evaluations and learns the multicomponent response together with its predictive uncertainty. We develop and evaluate MUTACO for a disordered network of stochastic phase oscillators. The inverse task is to infer statistical properties of the local potential and interactions from macroscopic rotation and synchronization curves under multiple driving conditions. Rather than requiring unique recovery of these statistical properties, MUTACO searches for parameter configurations whose predicted rotation and synchronization responses are compatible with the target response while accounting for predictive uncertainty. This response-centered formulation is appropriate when experimentally accessible observables characterize ensemble statistics rather than a particular microscopic realization. Under strictly limited simulation budgets, MUTACO achieves the lowest held-out response error among the tested adaptive-search, space-filling, and simulation-based inference methods, while also yielding lower parameter-recovery error than the compared methods. These results demonstrate that response-targeted sequential design is a simulator-efficient approach to inverse modeling from finite noisy forward evaluations and suggest broader applicability to stochastic physical systems with correlated multicomponent responses and weakly constrained parameter directions.

</details>


### [122] [Information Entropy and Dissipation Potential in Stochastic Nambu Nonequilibrium Thermodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03790)
*So Katagiri,Tatsuaki Wada*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This paper establishes a statistical interpretation of the dissipation potential S_NB in stochastic Nambu nonequilibrium thermodynamics (NNET). By deriving stationary distributions of the Fokker–Planck equation with a Nambu flow, the authors show that S_NB functions as the stationary quasipotential (not Shannon entropy) governing nonequilibrium steady states, and they extend this via a Massieu interpretation, a Nambu canonical distribution, and a saddle-point fluctuation covariance.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The role and interpretation of the dissipation potential S_NB within stochastic Nambu nonequilibrium thermodynamics (NNET) remain unclear, particularly whether it corresponds to Shannon entropy. Clarifying this relationship is needed to give S_NB a sound statistical meaning and to connect the NNET formalism with information-theoretic and nonequilibrium steady-state concepts.

Method: The authors derive the stationary distribution of the Fokker–Planck equation including a Nambu flow, analyzing three cases: pure diffusion, gradient-flow systems, and systems with Hamiltonian rotational currents. They impose conditions that the Nambu flow preserves the dissipation potential and that an Einstein-type relation holds between the diffusion matrix and the transport tensor. They then examine an open gas–piston system coupled to a thermal bath and an external-pressure reservoir to distinguish thermodynamic entropy from the dissipation potential, and use a saddle-point approximation to compute fluctuation covariance.

Result: When the Nambu flow preserves the dissipation potential and the Einstein-type relation holds, the stationary distribution is governed by an exponential weight determined by S_NB, so the local self-information relates directly to the dissipation potential. This shows S_NB is not the Shannon entropy but rather the stationary quasipotential governing the nonequilibrium steady-state distribution. The gas–piston analysis clarifies the Massieu interpretation of S_NB, yields a Nambu canonical distribution, and provides the covariance of fluctuations within the saddle-point approximation.

Conclusion: S_NB should be interpreted as the stationary quasipotential underlying nonequilibrium steady states rather than being identified with Shannon entropy. This provides a statistical interpretation of the NNET dissipation potential and links NNET to information-theoretic and fluctuation concepts.

Abstract: We investigate the relationship between the dissipation potential $S_{\mathrm{NB}}$ and information entropy in stochastic Nambu nonequilibrium thermodynamics (NNET). Starting from pure diffusion, gradient-flow systems, and systems with Hamiltonian rotational currents, we derive the stationary distribution of the Fokker--Planck equation including a Nambu flow. We show that, when the Nambu flow preserves the dissipation potential and an Einstein-type relation holds between the diffusion matrix and the transport tensor, the stationary distribution is governed by an exponential weight determined by $S_{\mathrm{NB}}$. Consequently, the local self-information is directly related to the dissipation potential, demonstrating that $S_{\mathrm{NB}}$ should not be identified with the Shannon entropy itself but instead serves as the stationary quasipotential governing the nonequilibrium steady-state distribution. Using the distinction between the thermodynamic entropy and the dissipation potential established for an open gas--piston system coupled to a thermal bath and an external-pressure reservoir, we further clarify the Massieu interpretation of $S_{\mathrm{NB}}$, introduce a Nambu canonical distribution, and derive the covariance of fluctuations within the saddle-point approximation. These results provide a statistical interpretation of the NNET dissipation potential by identifying it as the stationary quasipotential underlying nonequilibrium steady states.

</details>


### [123] [An Absorbing State Model with an Emergent Ising Transition](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03936)
*Shen Ai,Dov Levine,Paul Chaikin*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: A dynamical model of repulsive needles on a square lattice exhibits an absorbing-state transition, nematic order, a checkerboard-symmetric self-organized phase, and a phase boundary whose critical exponents match the 2D Ising universality class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to investigate how a simple nonequilibrium dynamical rule—repulsive rotation of overlapping needles fixed on a lattice—produces collective ordering and phase transitions, and to determine whether the resulting critical behavior belongs to a known universality class.

Method: Needles with centers fixed on a square lattice are modeled dynamically: overlapping needles are active and rotate repulsively by an angle θ ∈ [0, ε); non-overlapping needles remain immobile. The model is studied as a function of needle length and ε, with analysis of absorbing states, nematic order, checkerboard symmetry, and critical exponents at the melting phase boundary.

Result: The model displays an absorbing-state transition and nematic order for long needles. For certain needle lengths and ε, the system self-organizes into a checkerboard-symmetric state. Increasing ε melts this phase into a disordered state. The phase boundary exhibits critical exponents identical to those of the equilibrium 2D Ising model.

Conclusion: A nonequilibrium lattice model of repulsively rotating needles can self-organize into an ordered checkerboard phase, and its disordering transition belongs to the same universality class as the equilibrium 2D Ising model, highlighting the emergence of Ising-like criticality in a dynamical, absorbing-state system.

Abstract: We study a dynamical model of repulsive needles with centers fixed on a square lattice. Needles that overlap are active and receive a repulsive rotation $ θ\in [0,ε)$; needles with no overlap do not move. This model exhibits an absorbing state transition. Nematic order was observed for long needles. For a range of needle lengths and $ε$, the system self-organizes into a state with checkerboard symmetry. As $ε$ is increased, this phase melts and becomes disordered. Remarkably, we find that this phase boundary exhibits critical exponents identical to those of the equilibrium 2D Ising model.

</details>


### [124] [Transport, Hall Effect, and Collective Dynamics of Driven Particles in Active Chiral Media](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.03977)
*C. Reichhardt,C. J. O. Reichhardt*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: This study explores dc-driven transport of Yukawa-interacting particles through a bath of chiral active particles in circular motion, revealing distinct jammed, nonlinear plastic (with a finite Hall effect), and high-drive linear (phase-separated) regimes.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The paper aims to understand how dc-driven transport of Yukawa-interacting particles is affected by interactions with chiral active particles moving in circular orbits, specifically how the driven species and the chiral bath mutually influence each other's transport, drag, and Hall effects across different dynamical regimes.

Method: The authors simulate dc-driven Yukawa particles interacting with a bath of chiral active particles undergoing circular motion, measuring longitudinal and transverse (Hall) transport curves, drag and Hall effects on the chiral particles, and mapping the dynamical phases as functions of active orbit size, thermal fluctuations, and particle density.

Result: Three dynamical phases emerge: (1) a jammed phase at low drive; (2) a nonlinear disordered/plastic regime with a finite Hall effect induced by interactions with chiral active particles; and (3) a high-drive linear regime where driven and nondriven particles phase separate and the Hall effect nearly vanishes. The chiral particles experience strong drag and Hall effects from the driven particles, varying monotonically with thermal fluctuations or chiral motion parameters. Dynamical phases correlate with features in the longitudinal and transverse transport curves.

Conclusion: The interplay between a dc-driven Yukawa system and a chiral active bath produces a rich phase diagram with jammed, plastic, and phase-separated regimes, controlled by active orbit size, thermal fluctuations, and particle density, with characteristic signatures in the transport curves.

Abstract: We examine the dc driven transport of particles with Yukawa interactions through an assembly of chiral active particles undergoing circular motion. The driven particles exhibit a jammed phase, a nonlinear disordered or plastic regime where there is a finite Hall effect produced by interactions with the active chiral particles, and a high drive linear regime where the driven and nondriven particles phase separate and the Hall effect nearly disappears. The chiral particles experience strong drag and Hall effects from the driven particles, and these effects vary monotonically with changing thermal fluctuations or chiral motion parameters. The different dynamical phases are correlated with features in the longitudinal and transverse transport curves, and we map out the phases as a function of active orbit size, thermal fluctuations, and particle density.

</details>


### [125] [Casimir Force, Magnetization, and Susceptibility for \\ Ising Chain with an Impurity Atom in the Presence of an External Field](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04035)
*Daniel Dantchev*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Exact results are derived for the Casimir force in an Ising chain of N atoms with an impurity, as a function of N, temperature T, and external magnetic field h.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: To understand how an impurity atom affects the Casimir force in a ferromagnetic Ising chain and whether the force can be repulsive or attractive depending on impurity coupling.

Method: Exact analytical treatment of a short-ranged ferromagnetic Ising chain (J>0) with one impurity interacting with nearest neighbors via J_ia (ferro- or antiferromagnetic). The Casimir force is analyzed as a function of N, T, and h.

Result: The Casimir force can be either attractive or repulsive, determined by the sign and magnitude of the coupling ratio J_ia/J.

Conclusion: Impurity coupling ratio J_ia/J governs the sign of the Casimir force in the Ising chain, enabling both attractive and repulsive regimes.

Abstract: The article contains exact results for the behavior of the Casimir force of Ising chain of $N$ atoms containing an impurity atom. The force is considered as a function of $N$ and on both the temperature $T$ and the external magnetic field $h$. The interactions between nearest neighbors within the chain are supposed to be of a ferromagnetic short-ranged type $J>0$. The impurity atom interacts with constants $J_{ia}$ with its nearest neighbors, where $J_{ia}$ can be both of ferromagnetic type, i.e., $J_{ia}>0$, as well as of antiferromagnetic type, i.e., $J_{ia}<0$. It turns out that the Casimir force can be both attractive or repulsive, depending on the sign and the magnitude of the ratio $J_{ia}/J$.

</details>


### [126] [From Equilibrium Criticality to Universal Collective Phases through Nonreciprocal Coupling of Ordered Systems](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04450)
*Fabrizio Rippa,Jacopo A. Garofalo,Eugenio Lippiello*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Nonreciprocal couplings between spin systems with ordered isolated dynamics yield rich nonequilibrium phenomena—including multistability forbidden in equilibrium—inherited from the critical properties of the underlying isolated model, with a universal field equation derived for finite dimensions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Nonreciprocal interactions are a fundamental driver of collective behavior far from equilibrium, but prior work has largely focused on the oscillatory (swap) phase between systems whose isolated dynamics favors ordered states. The authors aim to uncover the broader nonequilibrium phenomenology and its connection to the critical properties of the isolated constituents.

Method: The authors analyze coupled spin systems with nonreciprocal couplings whose isolated dynamics favors ordered states. They use mean-field theory to map out the phase diagram, relating the emergent nonequilibrium behavior to the criticality (notably tricriticality) of the isolated model, and incorporate non-normal interactions. They then derive a universal field equation governing the onset of nonreciprocal oscillations in finite dimensions.

Result: Beyond the oscillatory (swap) phase, the system exhibits a multistable regime where ordered and disordered states coexist—a scenario strictly forbidden in equilibrium. This phenomenology is inherited from the isolated model's criticality: tricriticality produces multistability, hysteresis, and hard excitations in the oscillatory dynamics, while non-normal interactions further enrich the phase diagram by generating multistability. A universal field equation describes the onset of nonreciprocal oscillations in finite dimensions.

Conclusion: The results establish a unified theoretical framework linking critical phenomena, dynamical systems, and nonreciprocal collective dynamics, with potential applications from opinion dynamics to active matter.

Abstract: Nonreciprocal interactions have emerged as a fundamental driver of collective behavior far from equilibrium. Here, we show that nonreciprocal couplings between spin systems whose isolated dynamics favors ordered states, beyond the previously identified oscillatory (swap) phase, give rise to a richer nonequilibrium phenomenology, including a multistable regime where ordered and disordered states coexist, a scenario strictly forbidden in equilibrium. Crucially, we show that this nonequilibrium phenomenology is inherited directly from the critical properties of the underlying isolated model. In particular, tricriticality in the isolated system gives rise to multistability, hysteresis, and hard excitations in the oscillatory dynamics, while non-normal interactions further enrich the phase diagram by generating multistability. Moving beyond mean-field theory, we derive a universal field equation governing the onset of these nonreciprocal oscillations in finite dimensions. Our results establish a unified theoretical framework connecting critical phenomena, dynamical systems, and non-reciprocal collective dynamics, with applications ranging from opinion dynamics to active matter

</details>


### [127] [Active lattice percolation: an apparently new universality class of percolation transitions](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.04807)
*Vernon M. Hughes,David A. Huse*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: Dynamic percolation models with strictly forbidden cluster-splitting removals exhibit a hybrid percolation transition; making the model isotropic preserves it, adding further local moves removes the absorbing state and suppresses first-order features, yet exponents stay largely unchanged and differ from known percolation, suggesting a new universality class.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: The work is motivated by a model of local quantum error correction for a toric code (Chirame et al., PRX Quantum 6, 030363 (2025)), which was reported to show a first-order-like transition with bistability and a discontinuous order parameter. The authors aim to determine the true nature of this transition and to understand how generic its features are.

Method: The authors study a class of dynamic percolation models on a lattice with local stochastic rules for adding and removing connections, in which removal of any connection whose deletion would split a connected cluster is strictly forbidden. They analyze the original (non-isotropic) model numerically, then modify it to be fully isotropic while retaining the HPT. Finally, they add extra isotropic local stochastic moves that eliminate the absorbing state present on one side of the transition.

Result: Numerically, the transition is identified as a hybrid percolation transition (HPT), combining continuous features (diverging critical clusters) with discontinuous ones (a jump in the order parameter, as in a first-order transition). The HPT persists in the fully isotropic modification. Introducing additional isotropic local moves removes the absorbing state and strongly suppresses or eliminates the first-order transition, leaving an apparently continuous percolation transition. The measured percolation critical exponents change little across the hybrid and apparently continuous models, and differ significantly from those of known percolation transitions.

Conclusion: The reported transition is a hybrid percolation transition rather than a purely first-order one, and it survives isotropization. Removing the absorbing state suppresses first-order behavior, but the critical exponents remain essentially unchanged, indicating that these dynamic percolation models may constitute a new universality class distinct from known percolation transitions.

Abstract: We investigate a class of dynamic percolation models on a lattice, in which connections are added and removed by a set of local stochastic rules. In particular, the removal of any connection that can result in one connected cluster becoming two (or more) separate clusters is strictly forbidden. These models include and are inspired by a model for local quantum error correction for a toric code studied by Chirame, et al., PRX Quantum 6, 030363 (2025), who identified a phase transition with first-order characteristics, such as bistability and a discontinuous order parameter. We show numerically that this transition is a hybrid percolation transition (HPT), having characteristics of a continuous percolation transition such as diverging critical clusters while at the same transition having discontinuities like a first-order transition. We then modify this model to be fully isotropic, showing that the HPT remains. In both of these models, the steady state on one side of the HPT is an absorbing state; by turning on additional isotropic local stochastic moves, we remove the absorbing state and strongly suppress or remove the first-order transition, leaving an apparently continuous percolation transition. Curiously, the numerically-observed percolation critical exponents do not change significantly between the models with hybrid transitions and the model with an apparently continuous transition. These exponents are significantly different from known percolation transitions, indicating that this may be a new universality class.

</details>


### [128] [Confined Riesz gas: From Newtonian Dynamics to Emergent Hydrodynamics](https://arxiv.org/abs/2610.06626)
*Indranil Mukherjee,Abhishek Dhar,Manas Kulkarni*

Main category: cond-mat.stat-mech

TL;DR: The paper validates a hydrodynamic description of the Riesz gas by matching large-scale Newtonian simulations with a three-field (density, velocity, temperature) hydrodynamic theory, finding robust agreement across timescales and initial conditions.


<details>
  <summary>Details</summary>
Motivation: Equilibrium properties of the Riesz gas are well studied, but the link between microscopic dynamics, macroscopic evolution, and thermalization is largely unexplored.

Method: Large-scale Newtonian simulations combined with a hydrodynamic framework using three coarse-grained fields (density, velocity, temperature) and two phenomenological transport coefficients (bulk viscosity ξ and thermal conductivity κ).

Result: Excellent agreement between microscopic and hydrodynamic evolution across a broad range of timescales, from early times to steady state, and across a substantial window of ξ and κ.

Conclusion: The work systematically validates hydrodynamics in the Riesz gas and bridges Newtonian dynamics with continuum theories for power-law interacting systems from first principles.

Abstract: We investigate the real-time dynamics of the Riesz gas (RG), a paradigmatic system of particles with power-law interactions that encapsulates several many-body interacting classical systems. While the equilibrium properties of the system have been extensively studied the connection between microscopic dynamics, macroscopic evolution and thermalization has remained largely unexplored. Combining extensive large-scale Newtonian simulations with a hydrodynamic framework formulated in terms of three coarse grained fields -- density, velocity, and temperature -- we establish a direct and quantitative correspondence between the two descriptions. The hydrodynamic theory incorporates two phenomenological transport coefficients, the bulk viscosity $ξ$ and thermal conductivity $κ$, which encode dissipative effects at a macroscopic level. By analyzing representative classes of initial conditions, such as dome-like and Newton-cradle-type profiles, we find excellent agreement between microscopic and hydrodynamic evolution over a broad range of timescales, starting from relatively early all the way up to times at which steady state is attained. Notably, this agreement persists across a substantial window of $ξ$ and $κ$, demonstrating the robustness of the emergent hydrodynamic description. Our results provide a systematic validation of hydrodynamics in the RG and establish a concrete bridge between Newtonian dynamics and continuum theories in systems with power-law interactions, starting from first principles.

</details>
